are the abelian parallel transports along the A-cycle (in red) and the B-cycle (in purple) on
\Sigma
, respectively.
T^{ij}_{kl}
is the abelian parallel transport along an open path
\mathfrak{p}^{ij}_{kl}\subset\Sigma
which runs from the lift of the marked point for puncture
k
to sheet
i
to the lift of the marked point for puncture
l
to sheet
j
. Examples are the light green paths on the left and right.

Consider the path groupoid as illustrated in
fig.

12
. Nonabelianisation with respect to an FN network implies that the resulting
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-monodromies around the four punctures
z_{k}
, as well as the pants-curve
\mathfrak{a}
, are diagonal. We introduce parameters
M_{k}
and
A
such that these monodromies equal

\displaystyle\underline{M}_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}M_{k}&0\\
0&M_{k}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\quad\textrm{and}\quad M_{\mathfrak{a}}=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

(2.45)

respectively, in the directions indicated by little black arrows in
fig.

12
. The gauged 3-spheres admit an additional six dual spectral coordinates
T_{L/R,\,a/b/c}
, which we will denote here as

\displaystyle\{T^{ij}_{kl}:\,k,l\in\{\mathfrak{a},1,\ldots,4\},\,ij\in\{12,21\},\,T^{ij}_{kl}=-1/T^{ji}_{kl}=1/T^{ji}_{lk}\},

(2.46)

where
T^{ij}_{kl}
measures the abelian parallel transport along an open path
\mathfrak{p}^{ij}_{kl}\subset\Sigma
with runs from the lift of the marked point for puncture
k
to sheet
i
to the lift of the marked point for puncture
l
to sheet
j
. (The marked points are the red dots in
fig.

12
.)

Gluing the gauged 3-spheres reinstates the abelian gauge transformations at the marked point on the pants cycle.
Parallel transport on the resulting gauged 4-punctured sphere can be described in terms of the four masses
M_{k}
, the exponentiated FN length coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{A}=A
, and the five dual gauge-invariant coordinates

\displaystyle\{T^{12}_{12},\,T^{12}_{34},\,T^{12}_{1\mathfrak{a}}\,T^{21}_{\mathfrak{a}3},\,T^{12}_{2\mathfrak{a}}\,T^{21}_{\mathfrak{a}3},\,T^{12}_{1\mathfrak{a}}\,T^{21}_{\mathfrak{a}4}\}.

(2.47)

Nonabelianisation thus tells us that the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
monodromy representations on the gauged 4-punctured sphere is indeed parametrised by 10 independent spectral coordinates.

If we ”ungauge” the 4 punctures
z_{k}
, the resulting
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
monodromy representations can be solely expressed in terms of the two left-over spectral coordinates, which may be parametrised as

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{A}=A\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\mathcal{X}_{B}=-M_{1}M_{4}\,\frac{T_{12}}{T_{\mathfrak{a}1}\,T_{2\mathfrak{a}}}\,\frac{T_{34}}{T_{\mathfrak{a}3}\,T_{4\mathfrak{a}}},

(2.48)

where all upper labels of
T
’s are chosen as
ij=12
. These expressions match with the abelian parallel transport along the 1-cycles
A
and
B
on the cover
\Sigma
illustrated as well in
fig.

12
. The masses
M_{k}
have now turned into ”frozen” parameters and the additional
T
’s into gauge-dependent degrees of freedom.

Let us go back to the definition of the twist flow in
[
62
, §8.4]
to see that
\mathcal{X}_{B}
defines an FN twist coordinate. Suppose that we cut open the 4-punctured sphere along the pants curve. This fixes trivialisations at the marked points of both copies of the pants curve. Suppose that we now change the trivialisation (just) on the left pair of pants, using the transformation
G=\text{diag}(g,g^{-1})
, and then glue together the two pair of pants again. This operation defines the twist flow. Note that under the twist flow
T_{\mathfrak{a}1}\mapsto g\,T_{\mathfrak{a}1}
and
T_{2\mathfrak{a}}\mapsto g\,T_{2\mathfrak{a}}
, so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B}\mapsto g^{-2}\mathcal{X}_{B}.

(2.49)

The transformation above implies that
\mathcal{X}_{B}
is indeed an exponentiated (complexified) FN twist coordinate. (Recall from
[
62
, §8.4]
that there is an ambiguity in the FN twist coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{B}
parameterised by multiplying by a power of the FN length coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{A}
.)

The nonabelian parallel transport along any concatenation of paths on
C
can be expressed as a product of nonabelian parallel transport matrices, similar to
P_{a/b/c}^{\textrm{FN}}
and
{P^{\prime}}_{a/b/c}^{\textrm{FN}}
defined in eq. (
2.32
) and eq. (
2.44
), respectively. More precisely, a basis of nonabelian parallel transports on the gauged 4-punctured sphere is given by the matrices
11
11

11

In our nonabelianisation computation we moved the branch-cuts so that we first cross the branch-point and then the S-matrices, when moving along the open paths
\mathfrak{p}_{kl}
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P_{L,\mathfrak{a}1}^{\textrm{FN}}=P_{L}^{\textrm{FN}}\begin{bmatrix}M_{1}&M_{2}&A\\
T_{12}&T_{2\mathfrak{a}}&T_{\mathfrak{a}1}\end{bmatrix},&\quad P_{R,\mathfrak{a}4}^{\textrm{FN}}=P_{R}^{\textrm{FN}}\begin{bmatrix}A&M_{4}&M_{3}\\
T_{4\mathfrak{a}}&T_{34}&T_{\mathfrak{a}3}\end{bmatrix},\\
P_{L,12}^{\textrm{FN}}=P_{L}^{\textrm{FN}}\begin{bmatrix}M_{2}&A&M_{1}\\
T_{2\mathfrak{a}}&T_{\mathfrak{a}1}&T_{12}\end{bmatrix},&\quad P_{R,43}^{\textrm{FN}}=P_{R}^{\textrm{FN}}\begin{bmatrix}M_{3}&A&M_{4}\\
T_{\mathfrak{a}3}&T_{4\mathfrak{a}}&T_{34}\end{bmatrix},\\
P_{L,2\mathfrak{a}}^{\textrm{FN}}=P_{L}^{\textrm{FN}}\begin{bmatrix}A&M_{1}&M_{2}\\
T_{\mathfrak{a}1}&T_{12}&T_{2\mathfrak{a}}\end{bmatrix},&\quad P_{R,3\mathfrak{a}}^{\textrm{FN}}=P_{R}^{\textrm{FN}}\begin{bmatrix}M_{4}&M_{3}&A\\
T_{34}&T_{\mathfrak{a}3}&T_{4\mathfrak{a}}\end{bmatrix},\end{aligned}

(2.50)

as well as their inverses, where
P^{\textrm{FN}}_{L}=P^{\textrm{FN}}
and
P_{R}^{\textrm{FN}}
differs from
P_{L}^{\textrm{FN}}
by inverting the diagonal matrix diag
(T,T^{-1})
as well as the S-matrix
S^{\textrm{FN}}_{\textrm{av}}
, defined in (
2.35
). (This is because the orientation of the trajectories in the right molecule is opposite to that of the left molecule for the FN network. It might be possible to resolve the difference between
P^{\textrm{FN}}_{L}
and
P^{\textrm{FN}}_{R}
by adding an additional branch-cut along the pants cycle.) The equivariant invariant
\gamma^{2}
, as well as of course
\alpha\beta=\gamma-1
, is the same for both
P^{\text{FN}}_{L}
and
P^{\text{FN}}_{R}
.

The nonabelian parallel transport around the loop
\beta
, defined as the projection of the 1-cycle
B
to the surface
C
(oriented in the direction of the little black arrow near the puncture at
z=1
), is for instance given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P^{\text{FN}}_{\beta}&=P_{L}^{\textrm{FN}}\begin{bmatrix}M_{1}&M_{2}&A\\
T_{12}&T_{2\mathfrak{a}}&T_{\mathfrak{a}1}\end{bmatrix}\,\underline{M}_{1}\,\left(P_{L}^{\textrm{FN}}\begin{bmatrix}M_{1}&M_{2}&A\\
T_{12}&T_{2\mathfrak{a}}&T_{\mathfrak{a}1}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{-1}\times\\
&\qquad P_{R}^{\textrm{FN}}\begin{bmatrix}A&M_{4}&M_{3}\\
T_{4\mathfrak{a}}&T_{34}&T_{\mathfrak{a}3}\end{bmatrix}\,\underline{M}_{4}^{-1}\,\left(P_{R}^{\textrm{FN}}\begin{bmatrix}A&M_{4}&M_{3}\\
T_{4\mathfrak{a}}&T_{34}&T_{\mathfrak{a}3}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{-1},\end{aligned}

(2.51)

reproducing invariants already computed in
[
62
]
.

Using the
\mathcal{W}
-nonabelianisation method, it is straight-forward to compute the monodromy representation of any surface
C
(even with any type of punctures) in terms of FN-type spectral coordinates.

Let us restrict ourselves here to a surface
C
with only regular punctures, together with a pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
. Consider the FN spectral network whose restriction to each pair of pants is isotopic to molecule I. (Let us add branch-cuts around all pants cycles, so that the orientations of the saddle trajectories in each molecule are aligned.)

We start the
\mathcal{W}
-nonabelianisation procedure by fixing a path groupoid on
C
that, when restricted to any pair of pants in
\mathcal{P}
, is equivalent (up to homotopy) to the one illustrated in
fig.

10
. Any path
\mathfrak{p}
in this groupoid can thus be decomposed as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 78\relax}}\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 26\relax}}\cdots\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 74\relax}},

(2.52)

where each
\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 77\relax}}
is (a connected component of) the restriction of the path
\mathfrak{p}
to a single pair of pants labeled by
M
. The non-abelian parallel transport along
\mathfrak{p}
can be written in the form

\displaystyle P=P^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 78\relax}}P^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 26\relax}}\cdots P^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 74\relax}},

(2.53)

where each
P^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 77\relax}}
is equal to a non-abelian parallel transport
P_{a/b/c}
as computed in §
2.3
.

After gluing the pants boundaries, the associated abelian parallel transports
T_{L/R}
combine into abelian parallel transports
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}
along 1-cycles
\gamma
on
\Sigma
, which cross the lifts of the pants cycles. If we choose a basis of A-cycles on
\Sigma
consisting of lifts
A_{p}
of each pants cycle
\mathfrak{a}_{p}
, a basis of B-cycles
B_{p}
on
\Sigma
can be formed by additional closed 1-cycles
\gamma
. Through nonabelianisation, the nonabelian parallel transports are then expressed in terms of the abelian holonomies

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{A_{p}},\mathcal{X}^{\pm}_{B_{p}},~\text{and}~M_{k},

(2.54)

with
p
labeling the pants cycles and
k
the regular punctures on
C
. By a similar argument as presented around
eq.

2.49
, the coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{A_{p}}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\pm}_{B_{p}}
have the characteristics of exponentiated (complexified) FN length and twist coordinates, respectively.

3
Liouville CFT and quantum Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates

The goal of this section is to describe a quantisation of the Fenchel–Nielsen parallel transports derived in the previous section. We achieve this using the Moore-Seiberg transformations of conformal blocks in the framework of Liouville conformal field theory. By studying the transformation properties of the BPZ connection for Liouville theory, we are able to show that the Moore-Seiberg transformations quantise the Fenchel-Nielsen parallel transports.

In §
3.1
, we provide the necessary background by introducing the Lax connection, conformal blocks, (degenerate) vertex operators, and the BPZ connection. In §
3.2
, we summarise how to compute quantum parallel transports using the Moore-Seiberg groupoid, and how to bring the Moore-Seiberg (MS) parallel transport in the Fenchel-Nielsen gauge, relative to a chosen pants decomposition. In all this, we emphasize that results on the gauged three-punctured sphere can easily be generalised to an arbitrary (punctured) Riemann surface through a gluing construction. We furthermore show how the semi-classical conformal blocks are encoded in the evaluation of the Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates on the Lax connection, and how the Moore-Seiberg monodromies quantise this result. Finally, in §
3.4
, we observe that these results lead to an interpretation of Fenchel-Nielsen type spectral networks as topological defects in Liouville theory.

3.1
Basics of Liouville CFT

Liouville theory is a quantum field theory described by a scalar field
\varphi(z,\bar{z})
on a Riemann surface
C
with an exponential potential. It is governed by the (renormalised) action
[
8
]
,

S=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{C}d^{2}z\,\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left(\widehat{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\varphi\partial_{\beta}\varphi+Q\widehat{R}\varphi+4\pi\mu e^{2b\varphi}\right),

(3.1)

where
\hat{g}
is a constant-curvature background metric
12
12

12

Remember that, according to the uniformisation theorem, there is a unique such metric (up to diffeomorphisms) for every conformal class on
C
.
, the background charge

\displaystyle Q=b+\frac{1}{b},

(3.2)

measures the coupling of the quantum Liouville field
\varphi
to the curvature
\widehat{R}
, and
\mu
is called the cosmological constant.
13
13

13

See, for example,
[
99
,
106
]
for very helpful lecture notes on Liouville theory and CFT’s in general.

In the semi-classical limit
b\to 0
,
the equations of motion of the Liouville theory describe constant-curvature deformations

g=e^{2b\varphi}\widehat{g}

(3.3)

of the background metric
\widehat{g}
.
Another way to phrase this is to say that the Liouville equation of motion, in the semi-classical limit
b\to 0
, is equivalent to the rank-
2
linear differential equation

(d+A)\Psi=0,

(3.4)

where the flat
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
connection
A
is known as the
Lax connection

[
35
]
. Locally, the Lax equation (
3.4
) can be brought into the form
[
34
, Section 3]

\left(\partial_{z}^{2}+b^{2}T_{\textrm{cl}}(z)\right)\psi(z)=0,

(3.5)

where
T_{\textrm{cl}}
is the semi-classical energy-momentum tensor of the Liouville theory. As we detail in §
3.2
, the Lax connection transforms as an
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-oper connection.

At the quantum level, Liouville theory is a two-dimensional conformal field theory, whose symmetry algebra is the left-right Virasoro algebra
\textbf{Vir}_{c}\otimes\overline{\textbf{Vir}}_{c}
with central charge

c=1+6Q^{2}.

(3.6)

The quantum theory is invariant under the symmetry
b\leftrightarrow 1/b
(when simultaneously rescaling the cosmological constant
\mu
), which is obviously broken in the semi-classical limit
b\to 0
.

The basic local operators in Liouville theory are the primary vertex operators
V_{\Delta_{\alpha}}(z,\overline{z})
which are characterised by their conformal weights
\Delta_{\alpha}
. In particular,
V_{\Delta_{\alpha}}(z,\overline{z})
transforms as a
(\Delta_{\alpha},\Delta_{\alpha})
-form under holomorphic/anti-holomorphic changes of variables. In what follows, it is useful to write the conformal weight as

\Delta_{\alpha}=\alpha(Q-\alpha),

(3.7)

where
\alpha
is called the
Liouville momentum
(or simply momentum), and label the primary vertex operators by their momentum instead, i.e
V_{\alpha}
.

Through the state-operator correspondence

\displaystyle\lim_{z,\overline{z}\to 0}V_{\alpha}(z,\overline{z})|0\rangle=|v_{\alpha}\rangle,

(3.8)

primary vertex operators correspond to highest weight states (or primary states)
v_{\alpha}\otimes\overline{v}_{\alpha}
of left-right Virasoro modules
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\alpha}
with conformal dimension
(\Delta_{\alpha},\Delta_{\alpha})
. We denote the vertex operators that correspond to a generic state
v\otimes\overline{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\alpha}
by

\displaystyle V_{\alpha}(v\otimes\overline{v};z,\overline{z}).

(3.9)

For unitarity, it is essential that
c\in\mathbb{R}
– only then is the canonical inner product defined by
(L_{n})^{\dagger}=L_{-n}
positive-definite. This implies that
b
is either real (i.e.
c\geq 25
) or purely imaginary (i.e.
c\leq 1
). A Virasoro module
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
is unitary iff

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha}\geq 0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\alpha=\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathit{p}\in\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathbb{R}\quad\text{with}~\mathit{p}\in\mathbb{R},

(3.10)

and the theory has a continuous operator spectrum

\mathcal{H}=\int_{\alpha\in\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathbb{R}_{+}}d\alpha\,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\alpha}.

(3.11)

Remark 3.1
.

The only unitary theories with
b\in i\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and thus central charge
c\leq 1
, are part of the minimal model series which have
b=i\sqrt{p^{\prime}/p}
for coprime integers
p^{\prime}>p
. These minimal models have a finite spectrum, listed in the famous Kac table. In the limiting case
b=i
, Liouville theory reduces to the theory of a free boson
\varphi(z,\overline{z})=\varphi(z)+\overline{\varphi}(\overline{z})
. The free boson CFT has a continuous spectrum given by
eq.

3.11
with
Q=0
.

Although unitarity restricts the Liouville momentum to

\alpha=\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathit{p}
, it is customary to analytically continue results to allow
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
. In particular, we will often consider Verma modules
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
for arbitrary complex momenta
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, and will consider
complex-valued background charges
Q
. Within this generalization, the Lax connection is naturally a flat
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-connection.

Liouville theory was originally ”solved” in
[
30
,
109
]
, where it was proposed that the basic building blocks of Liouville theory, the three-point functions

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{C}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})=\lim_{|z|\to\infty}|z|^{\Delta_{\alpha_{3}}}\,\langle V_{\alpha_{1}}(0)V_{\alpha_{2}}(1)V_{\alpha_{3}}(z)\rangle,\end{aligned}

(3.12)

have the analytic expression

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{C}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})=\left(\pi\mu\gamma(b^{2})b^{2-2b^{2}}\right)^{(Q-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3})/b}\times\\
&\quad\times\,\frac{\Upsilon_{b}^{\prime}(0)\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{2})\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{3})}{\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-Q)\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3})\Upsilon_{b}(-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3})},\end{aligned}

(3.13)

with

\displaystyle\gamma(x)=\frac{\Gamma(x)}{\Gamma(1-x)}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\,\Upsilon_{b}(x)=\frac{1}{\Gamma_{b}(x)\Gamma_{b}(Q-x)},

(3.14)

in terms of the double Barnes functions
\Gamma_{b}(x)=\Gamma_{1/b}(x)
. Eq (
3.13
) is now known as the DOZZ formula
[
105
]
. The DOZZ proposal was put on a much more solid basis in
[
107
]
, and recently proved using probabilistic methods in
[
75
]
.

3.1.1
Conformal blocks

The correlation functions of Liouville theory factorise into holomorphic (chiral) and anti-holomorphic (anti-chiral) contributions. More precisely, given a pair of pants decomposition of the surface
C
, and given that the OPE between two primary fields necessarily has the form

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &V_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})V_{\alpha_{2}}(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})=\\
&\qquad\int d\alpha\,\mathcal{C}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha^{*})\,|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2(\Delta_{\alpha}-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2})}\,\left(V_{\Delta_{\alpha}}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})+\mathcal{O}(|z_{1}-z_{2}|)\right),\end{aligned}

(3.15)

where
\alpha^{*}=Q-\alpha
is the conjugate momentum, and where the subleading terms are corrections from descendant fields, it follows that any Liouville correlation function can be written as an integral over internal momenta through the pants cycles, with the integrand consisting of a product of three-point functions
\mathcal{C}
, one for each pair of pants, times a factorised product
\mathcal{F}\overline{\mathcal{F}}
over descendant contributions.

The factors
\mathcal{F}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
are known as holomorphic and anti-holomorphic
conformal blocks
, respectively. Whereas the three-point functions
\mathcal{C}
encode the dynamics of Liouville theory, the conformal blocks
\mathcal{F}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
are entirely determined by the Virasoro algebra.

For example, the four-point correlation function of primary vertex operators may be written as

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\langle V_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})V_{\alpha_{2}}(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})V_{\alpha_{3}}(z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})V_{\alpha_{4}}(z_{4},\bar{z}_{4})\rangle=

(3.16)

\displaystyle\int d\nu(\alpha)\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\alpha}(\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3},\bar{z}_{4}).

where the measure

\displaystyle d\nu(\alpha)=\int\frac{d\alpha}{2\pi}\,|q^{\Delta_{\alpha}-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}|^{2}\,\mathcal{C}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha^{*})\,\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}),

(3.17)

contains a product of two 3-point functions. The conformal block
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
is labeled by an internal momentum
\alpha
and has an expansion of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})=1+\frac{(\Delta_{\alpha}-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2})(\Delta_{\alpha}+\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{4})}{2\Delta_{\alpha}}\,x+\mathcal{O}(x^{2})

(3.18)

in terms of the cross-ratio

\displaystyle x=\frac{(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{3}-z_{4})}{(z_{1}-z_{3})(z_{2}-z_{4})}.

(3.19)

Remark 3.2
.

The three-point function
\mathcal{C}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
may also be brought into a factorised form

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})=|\mathcal{C}_{\text{hol}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})|^{2},

(3.20)

although there is no canonical way of doing this. One possibility is to split each
\Upsilon_{b}
-factor as in eq. (
3.14
)
[
5
]
. Another possibility is to consider each
\Upsilon_{b}
as the product
[
104
]

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{b}(x)=\sqrt{\Upsilon_{b}(x)\Upsilon_{b}(Q-x)}.

(3.21)

We refer to the former normalisation as the topological string normalisation, and to the latter one as the CFT normalisation. In contrast to the
Virasoro
conformal blocks
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}=\mathcal{F}
that are independent of the dynamics, we will define
Liouville
conformal blocks as the combination

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\text{Liou}}=\left(\prod\mathcal{C}_{\text{hol}}\right)\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}},

(3.22)

where the product runs over all pair of pants in the chosen pants decomposition of
C
.

In the following, we restrict ourselves to holomorphic Virasoro blocks
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}
, and drop the prefixes “holomorphic” and ”Virasoro”. Conformal blocks are typically defined on genus zero surfaces and extended to higher genus surfaces through a procedure known as gluing. We denote the space of conformal blocks on a surface
C
by
\mathrm{CB}(C)
.

On the Riemann sphere
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, with punctures at
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
labelled by Virasoro modules
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{n}}
, a
conformal block
is defined as a map

\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}:\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{n}}(z_{n})\to\mathbb{C},

(3.23)

that satisfies the so-called conformal Ward identities. The latter identities are derived by inserting a copy of the stress-energy tensor
T(z)
into the conformal block, or more precisely,

\displaystyle\oint_{\gamma}\eta(z)T(z),

where
\eta(z)
is a meromorphic function that is holomorphic away from the punctures and
\gamma
is a contour that does not encircle any of the punctures. The Ward identities thereby describe how the conformal blocks transform under infinitesimal conformal symmetries.

At times, it is useful to rewrite the
n-
point conformal block as a map between Virasoro modules

\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}:\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{n-1}}(z_{n-1})\to\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{n}}^{*}(z_{n}).

(3.24)

The dual Virasoro module
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}^{*}
is defined with respect to the standard inner product on
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
.
14
14

14

The Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
carries a well-defined inner product only when
\alpha\in\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathbb{R}
. Nevertheless, we can extend this to all values of
\alpha
through analytic continuation. This is discussed in detail in
[
105
, Section 4]
.

We shall refer to conformal blocks on genus zero surfaces with
n
insertions as
n-
point conformal blocks
. The Virasoro symmetry ensures that an
n
-point conformal block
\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{Vir}}
is fully determined by its action on the primary states
v_{\alpha_{k}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{k}}
. So, without any loss in generality, from here onwards, we restrict our attention to conformal blocks evaluated on primary vertex operators,

\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{Vir}}(V_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1}),\ldots,V_{\alpha_{n}}(z_{n})).

(3.25)

Gluing conformal blocks

A conformal block on an arbitrary (possibly punctured) Riemann surface can be built out of conformal blocks on
3
-punctured spheres. This construction builds on the plumbing construction that may be used to sew Riemann surfaces geometrically. This is particularly useful because, up to normalisation, there is a unique
3
-point conformal block,

\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{Vir}}:\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{2}}(z_{2})\to\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{3}}^{*}(z_{3}).

(3.26)

That is, suppose we want to glue the Riemann surfaces
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
at the punctures (or generic marked points)
p_{1}\in C_{1}
and
p_{2}\in C_{2}
(where
C_{1}
may be equal to
C_{2}
). Choose local coordinates
z
and
w
on
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, respectively, such that

z(p_{1})=0\text{ and }w(p_{2})=\infty.

Also, fix a sewing parameter
\kappa\in\mathbb{C}
. Then, cut open small discs around
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
of radius
r>|\kappa|
such that the chosen coordinates are well-defined in this neighbourhood. To glue the two surfaces together, we simply identify the regions,

\{\frac{|\kappa|}{r}<|z|<r,\frac{|\kappa|}{r}<|w|<r\}

(3.27)

through the condition

wz=\kappa.

(3.28)

We denote the newly obtained Riemann surface by
C
.

Now, suppose that both surfaces
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
carry conformal blocks
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{C_{1}}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{C_{2}}
respectively, and we would like to extend this to a conformal block on
C
. The vertex operators inserted outside the sewing region remain unaffected, but the Virasoro modules at
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are required to be conjugate to each other. We collectively denote the vertex operators away from
p_{1}
on
C_{1}
by
V_{C_{1}}
, and the vertex operators away from
p_{2}
on
C_{2}
by
V_{C_{2}}
.

Let
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}^{*}
be two conjugate representations at the points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
respectively. Then, the conformal block
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{C,\alpha}
on
C
is defined as

\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{C,\alpha}\left(V_{C_{1}},V_{C_{2}}\right)=\sum_{v,v^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{C_{1}}\left(V_{C_{1}},V_{\alpha}(v;0)\right)K_{\alpha;v,v^{\prime}}^{-1}\,\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{C_{2}}\,\left((V_{\alpha^{*}}(v^{\prime};\infty),\,V_{C_{2}}\right),

(3.29)

where the right-hand side is a sum over all possible states
v,v^{\prime}
in the representations
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}^{*}
, respectively, and
K_{\alpha;v,v^{\prime}}
is the Kac matrix defined by two-point conformal locks

K_{\alpha;v,v^{\prime}}=\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\left(V_{\alpha^{*}}(v;\infty),V_{\alpha}(v^{\prime};0)\right),

(3.30)

which can be computed systematically.

In principle, it is then possible to construct conformal blocks on any arbitrary Riemann surface
C
from the knowledge of the three-point conformal blocks for all possible Virasoro representations. This motivates the definition of the space
\mathrm{gCB}(C_{0,3})
of
gauged

3
-point conformal blocks as the space

\mathrm{gCB}(C_{0,3}):=\bigoplus_{\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2},\,\alpha_{3}}\hom\bigg(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{2}}(z_{2})\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{3}}(z_{3}),\,\mathbb{C}\bigg)^{\text{Ward id}}

(3.31)

of conformal blocks on the gauged 3-sphere
C_{0,3}
,
where the sum is over
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{k}}
for
generic
momenta
\alpha_{k}
(as opposed to degenerate momenta that will be introduced in
eq.

3.35
). Observe that
\mathrm{gCB}(C_{0,3})
is an infinite-dimensional vector space since all three momenta
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{3}
can take arbitrary (generic) complex values. Instead, its subspace
\mathrm{CB}(C_{0,3})
of conformal blocks on the bare (”non-gauged”) 3-punctured sphere is 1-dimensional. That is, its only freedom is the choice of normalisation.

The space of conformal blocks on an arbitrary Riemann surface
C
is, similarly, infinite-dimensional in general. Furthermore, we can also define the space of gauged conformal blocks
\mathrm{gCB}(C)
on any surface
C
by summing over all possible vertex operator representations at its punctures.

As we vary the complex structure on
C
, the space of conformal blocks organises itself into a holomorphic vector bundle
\mathcal{CB}
over the moduli space
\mathcal{M}
of complex structures on
C
. The variation of the conformal block in the direction of a Beltrami differential
\mu
can be expressed in terms of a
projectively flat
(or twisted) connection on
\mathcal{M}
, whose connection 1-form is defined by inserting

\displaystyle\int_{C}\mu\,T(z)

(3.32)

into the conformal block. More details can be found for instance in
[
59
, Section 2]
.

Suppose
C
is a genus
g
surface with
n
punctures. Given any pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
of
C
, we can construct a canonical basis

\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{\vec{\alpha}}

(3.33)

of the (possibly infinite-dimensional) space of conformal blocks on
C
that is obtained by gluing
2g-2+n
three-point conformal blocks, and labelled by a choice of
3g-3+n
internal momenta

\displaystyle\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{3g-3+n}),

(3.34)

i.e. one for each pants cycle in
\mathcal{P}
.
The bases associated with the different pairs of pants decompositions
\mathcal{P}
are known as channels in the physics literature.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that even though the expressions for these bases depend non-trivially on the choice of pants decomposition, the resulting correlation functions are independent of this choice by definition (since any two-dimensional CFT ought to be modular invariant).

3.1.2
Null-vector decoupling

Generically, a Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
forms an irreducible representation of the Virasoro algebra. However, for special values

\alpha_{p,q}=(1-p)\,\frac{1}{2b}+(1-q)\,\frac{b}{2}\quad\text{ with }~p,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

(3.35)

of the Liouville momentum, the corresponding Virasoro module is reducible and contains a sub-module generated by a null state (or null vector). All states in this sub-module have vanishing norm, and may thus be consistently quotiented out. The resulting finite-dimensional module
\mathcal{V}_{p,q}
is known as a
degenerate
representation, the associated primary vertex operator
V_{p,q}:=V_{\alpha_{p,q}}
is known as a degenerate primray vertex operator, and the corresponding momentum
\alpha_{p,q}
, as a degenerate momentum.
15
15

15

From a representation-theoretic perspective, an infinite-dimensional reducible Verma module and its finite-dimensional irreducible quotient are two starkly different objects. It will also be a consistent theme in this paper that the degenerate operators
V_{p,q}
often behave differently when compared to generic primary operators
V_{\alpha}
.

The degenerate momenta
\alpha_{p,q}
are closely related to the fundamental weights of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
. Indeed, we may rewrite
eq.

3.35
as

\alpha_{p,q}=-\frac{1}{b}\,\omega_{p-1}-b\,\omega_{q-1},

(3.36)

where
\omega_{p-1}
and
\omega_{q-1}
are the highest weights of the
p
th and
q
th symmetric tensor representations of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
, respectively. In this sense, a degenerate operator with momentum
\alpha_{1,q}

quantises
the
q
-dimensional representation of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
. This can be made more precise through skein theory (see §
4.1.3
). For quantum monodromy representations, the degenerate operator
V_{1,2}
corresponding to the
2
-dimensional or defining representation of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
is the most relevant to us.

If a degenerate vertex operator
V_{p,q}(z)
is inserted in a conformal block
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}(\ldots)
, the algebraic null-vector constraint turns into a differential operator of degree
pq
acting on the block
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}(V_{p,q}(z),\ldots)
. The resulting differential equation is known as the null-vector decoupling equation or the
BPZ equation

[
15
]
. For instance, for the degenerate vertex operator
V_{1,2}(z)
we obtain the degree 2 differential equation
16
16

16

The detailed equation can for instance be found in
[
13
, Appendix D]
.

(\partial_{z}^{2}+b^{2}T(z))\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}(V_{1,2}(z),\ldots)=0.

(3.37)

The space of conformal blocks
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}(V_{p,q}(z),\ldots)
may thus be identified with the space of solutions to the BPZ equation, and is therefore
pq
-dimensional.

The rank
pq
matches with the number of fusion channels in the OPE between the degenerate vertex operator
V_{p,q}(z)
and another generic vertex operator
[V_{\alpha}]\in\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to z_{k}}V_{p,q}(z)\left[V_{\alpha_{k}}\right](z_{k})=\sum_{r,s}c_{r,s}\,z^{\Delta_{\alpha_{k}-\frac{r}{2b}-\frac{sb}{2}}-\Delta_{\alpha_{k}}-\Delta_{p,q}}\,\left[V_{\alpha_{k}-\frac{r}{2b}-\frac{sb}{2}}\right](z_{k})

(3.38)

where

r\in\{-p+1,-p+3,\ldots,p-1\}\text{ and }s\in\{-q+1,-q+3,\ldots,q-1\}.

Then, letting the insertion point
z
approach a puncture
z_{k}
, the resulting conformal blocks

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}(V_{\alpha-\frac{r}{2b}-\frac{sb}{2}}(z_{k}),\ldots),

(3.39)

generate the
pq
independent local solutions to the BPZ differential equation near
z_{k}
.

Note that the OPE (
3.38
) also implies constraints on the internal momenta
\vec{a}
of the conformal block
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}
, which are generically unrestricted. For instance, the three-point conformal block
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(V_{\alpha_{1}}V_{\alpha_{2}}V_{\alpha_{3}})
with degenerate insertion
V_{\alpha_{2}}=V_{1,2}
is non-vanishing only when the non-degenerate momenta
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{3}
are related by

\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}-\,s\frac{b}{2},

(3.40)

with
s=\pm
. This restriction continues to hold when gluing three-point functions to construct conformal blocks on arbitrary surfaces.

Comparing the BPZ equation (
3.37
) with the Lax equation
eq.

3.5
suggests that the BPZ equation for
V_{1,2}
should be viewed as a
quantisation
of the Lax equation. This implies that the degenerate vertex operator
V_{1,2}(z)
should be viewed as the quantisation of the semi-classical field
\psi(z)
. More precisely, the BPZ equation (
3.37
) should be interpreted as a ”quantum oper” connection on a rank 2 vector bundle over
C
, associated with the fundamental representation of
SL(2)
. We clarify what we mean by this in §
3.2
, where we discuss the global transformation properties of the BPZ equation (
3.37
) on a general Riemann surface.

More generally, the BPZ equation for a degenerate operator
V_{1,p}(z)
should be thought of as a quantum connection in a rank
p
vector bundle, associated with the
p
th symmetric tensor representation of
SL(2)
, whereas the BPZ equation for
V_{p,q}(z)
is associated with a component of the tensor product of the
p
th and the
q
th symmetric tensor representations of
SL(2)
.

3.1.3
AGT correspondence and NRS proposal

We conclude the introduction to Liouville theory with a brief discussion on the AGT correspondence between 4d
\mathcal{N}=2

SU(2)
gauge theories and 2d Liouville CFT
[
5
]
. The AGT correspondence states that the supersymmetric partition function
\mathcal{Z}[S^{4}_{b}]
of a 4d
\mathcal{N}=2

SU(2)
theory
T[C]
on the squashed 4-sphere

S^{4}_{b}:=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5})\in\mathbb{R}^{5}\>:\>x_{5}^{2}+b^{2}\left(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\right)+b^{-2}\left(x_{3}^{2}+x_{4}^{2}\right)=\hbar^{-2}\},

can be identified with a Liouville correlation function whose vertex operator insertions are determined by the matter content of the 4d theory. This follows from defining a 6d
(2,0)

A_{1}
-theory in the background
S^{4}_{b}\times C
.

Similar to the factorisation of Liouville correlation functions into holomorphic and anti-holomorphic conformal blocks, the 4d partition function
\mathcal{Z}[S^{4}_{b}]
can also be (schematically) factorised as

\mathcal{Z}[S^{4}_{b}]=\int da\,|\mathcal{Z}^{\textrm{1-loop}}(a)|^{2}\,\mathcal{Z}[\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}](a)\,\overline{\mathcal{Z}[\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}]}(a),

(3.41)

given a weak-coupling description of
T[C]
(which is equivalent to a choice of pants decomposition of
C

[
49
]
). The Coulomb parameters are collectively denoted by
a
, the
\mathcal{Z}^{\textrm{1-loop}}
(or
\overline{\mathcal{Z}^{\textrm{1-loop}}}
) encodes the 1-loop contribution to the Nekrasov partition function in the
\Omega-
background
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}
, whereas
\mathcal{Z}[\mathbb{R}_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}^{4}]
(or
\overline{\mathcal{Z}[\mathbb{R}_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}^{4}]}
) contains its tree-level and instanton (or anti-instanton) contribution. The instanton parameters of
T[C]
may be identified with the complex structure parameters of
C
.

Comparing the factorisation (
3.41
) with the factorisation of Liouville correlation functions (see for instance
eq.

3.16
), the AGT correspondence proposes the identification of instanton partition functions
\mathcal{Z}[\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}]
with the holomorphic conformal blocks
\mathcal{F}
, where the deformation parameters are related as

\epsilon_{1}=\frac{\hbar}{b}\quad\text{ and }\quad\epsilon_{2}=\hbar b.

(3.42)

Additionally,
|\mathcal{Z}^{\textrm{1-loop}}|^{2}
may be identified with the product of Liouville three-point functions
C(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
contributing to the Liouville correlation function. For now, it will be convenient to set
\hbar=1
. We will reintroduce this parameter in §
5
.

The semi-clasical limit of the AGT correspondence can be understood via the Nekrasov-Rosly-Shatashvili (NRS) proposal
[
93
]
, which states that the generating function of the family of opers, when expressed in terms of complex FN-type coordinates, computes the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{eff}}
for the associated class S theory
T[C]
.

The easiest way to understand this proposal is to consider the theory
T[C]
on a background
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times D_{\epsilon}
where
D_{\epsilon}
is the cigar geometry with an
\Omega-
deformation.
17
17

17

Starting from the
\Omega-
background
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\epsilon_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\epsilon_{1}}
, we obtain the background
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times D_{\epsilon}
by first, sending
\epsilon_{2}\to 0
, and then replacing the standard metric on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\epsilon=\epsilon_{1}}
with the cigar metric
ds^{2}=dr^{2}+f(r)d\theta^{2}
, where
f(r)\sim r^{2}
as
r\to 0
and
f(r)=\rho^{2}
for a constant radius
\rho
as
r\to\infty
.
At low energies, we can reduce the theory along
D_{\epsilon}
to an effective 2d
\mathcal{N}=(2,2)
theory of twisted chiral superfields (sourced by the 4d vector multiplets) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, coupled to an effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}
. In
[
94
]
, Nekrasov and Shatashvili proposed that the effective twisted superpotential and the instanton partition function for the theory
T[C]
are related as

\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}(\epsilon)=\lim_{\epsilon_{2}\to 0}\epsilon_{2}\log\mathcal{Z}[\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon,\,\epsilon_{2}}].

(3.43)

Furthermore, they argued that the effective twisted superpotential plays the role of a Yang-Yang functional in a quantum integrable system underlying the Seiberg-Witten geometry of
T[C]
. This is now known as the Bethe-gauge correspondence.

Through the AGT-correspondence for general
C
, the exponent of the one-loop contribution to
\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{eff}}
is closely related to the square-root of the three-point contribution to the associated Liouville correlator (see the discussion around
eq.

3.93
and (
3.96
) for the precise statement), and the exponent of the instanton contribution to
\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{eff}}
is equal to a corresponding conformal block
\mathcal{F}
, all in the limit
\epsilon_{2}\sim b\to 0
. This establishes that the semi-classical (
b\to 0
) limit of Liouville conformal blocks is captured by the Yang-Yang function of a quantum integrable system determined by
T[C]
.

At the same time, it was argued by Nekrasov, Rosly and Shatashvili in
[
93
]
that the superpotential
\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}
computes the difference of two generating functions of holomorphic Lagrangian submanifolds inside the moduli space of flat
\epsilon
-connections. This can be seen by, instead of reducing the 4d theory on all of
D_{\epsilon}
to get a 2d theory, compactifying along the circle direction of the cigar (i.e. write
D_{\epsilon}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\times S^{1}
where the
S^{1}
degenerates at the tip of the cigar) to obtain a 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
theory on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Then, the two holomorphic Lagrangian submanifolds correspond to a choice of two
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS boundary conditions at the two ends of
\mathbb{R}^{+}

[
96
]
.

The boundary condition at the tip of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
is purely dictated by the geometry, and is described by the Lagrangian submanifold of
\epsilon
-opers inside the moduli space. The boundary condition at the other end is less restrictive. It was argued in
[
65
]
that for any choice of holomorphic Darboux coordinates
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}
on the moduli space of flat
\epsilon
-connections, the second boundary condition is given by fixing all
y_{i}\equiv 0\,(\text{mod }2\pi)
. We will come back to this in §
3.2.3
.

3.2
Moore-Seiberg monodromies on the gauged 3-sphere

We have learned in §
3.1
that the space of Liouville conformal blocks forms a vector bundle
\mathcal{CB}
over the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
of genus
g
Riemann surfaces
C_{g,n}
with
n
punctures. In particular, when
g=0
we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0,n}

\displaystyle=\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in(\mathbb{CP}^{1})^{n}:z_{i}\neq z_{j}\text{ for }i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\}/\text{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),

(3.44)

where the group
\text{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
acts via Möbius transformations. The vector bundle
\mathcal{CB}
is equipped with a projectively flat connection, and parallel transport around loops in
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
thus induces nontrivial transformations in the space of conformal blocks. In fact, since

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{g,n}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{g,n}/\text{MCG}(C_{g,n}),

(3.45)

where the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{g,n}
is the universal cover of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
and the mapping class group
\text{MCG}(C_{g,n})
parametrises non-trivial loops in
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
, the space of conformal blocks forms a projective representation of the mapping class group
[
88
]
.

We have also explained in §
3.1
that any pair of pants decomposition of the surface
C_{g,n}
induces a basis for the space of Liouville blocks on
C_{g,n}
. For instance, on the 4-punctured sphere, there are three standard choices known as the s-channel, the t-channel and the u-channel basis corresponding to the three different pants decomposition. See
fig.

13
. The generators of each basis are labeled by a single intermediate momentum
\alpha
.

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{(s)}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\\

\alpha

Figure 13
:
The three standard choices of bases for a four-point conformal block.

The mapping class group
\mathrm{MCG}(C_{g,n})
has a presentation with three generators known as fusion, braiding, and modular (or S) moves. Given a pair of pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
of the surface
C_{g,n}
, the action of each basic move on
\mathcal{P}
produces a new pair of pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. This structure may be captured in a groupoid whose vertices are all different pair of pants decompositions of
C_{g,n}
and the edges/morphisms are actions of the generators. This groupoid is known as the
Moore-Seiberg groupoid
.

Starting with a given pair of pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
, there may be several different sequences of basic moves leading to another decomposition
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Such consistency relations between the three basic moves correspond to closed 2-cells in the Moore-Seiberg groupoid. We refer the reader to
[
14
]
for more details. Since the S-move can be expressed in terms of the other generators (and is not relevant anyhow when discussing genus
0
surfaces), we will focus on the fusion and braiding moves from now on.

\alpha_{4}
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{3}
\alpha_{2}
\alpha

\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}
\alpha_{4}

\alpha_{3}

(a)

The fusion move expresses an s-channel block in terms of t-channel blocks.

\alpha^{\prime}

The braiding move (in our notation) expresses a t-channel block in terms of permuted t-channel blocks.

Figure 14
:
The action of the Moore-Seiberg moves on Liouville blocks. Figures
(a)
and

illustrate the fusion and inverse fusion moves, respectively. Figure
(c)
illustrates the braiding move.

The
fusion
move expresses an s-channel conformal block in terms of t-channel conformal blocks, as in

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{(s)}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\int d\alpha^{\prime}\;F_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(t)}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix},

(3.46)

with inverse

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{(t)}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\int d\alpha^{\prime}\;F^{-1}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(s)}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}.

(3.47)

This is illustrated in
figs.

14(a)
and
14(b)
. The integral kernel of the fusion transformation is called the fusion matrix
F_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}
(or also the
6j
-symbol). The consistency conditions in the Moore-Seiberg groupoid lead to the following symmetry properties for the fusion matrices:

F_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}=F_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{3}\\
\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{4}\end{bmatrix}=F_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\end{bmatrix},\quad F^{-1}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}=F_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{4}\\
\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}.

(3.48)

The
braiding
move can be expressed as a sequence of three moves, in which we first use the fusion move to express an s-channel block into t-channel conformal blocks, then twist the two ”upward-facing” legs of the t-channel blocks, and finally express the result in terms of s-channel blocks by using the inverse fusion move. This is illustrated in
fig.

14(c)
. The twisting action
B
on the t-channel blocks is simply given by the diagonal transformation

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{(t)}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=B_{\alpha}^{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{2}}\,\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{(t)}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{3}\\
\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix},

(3.49)

where the coefficient

B_{\alpha}^{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{2}}=\exp(-i\pi(\Delta(\alpha)-\Delta(\alpha_{2})-\Delta(\alpha_{3})))

(3.50)

is merely a complex number. The twisting action should be thought of as transporting
\alpha_{3}
around
\alpha_{2}
in the
clockwise
direction.

The fusion matrices are generally complicated, infinite-dimensional objects. They simplify drastically, though, if we pick one of the operators to be degenerate.

3.2.1
Computing Moore-Seiberg parallel transports

Consider a four-point conformal block
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{1,2}(z)=\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}(V_{1,2}(z),\ldots)
with one degenerate insertion
V_{1,2}
. This block is illustrated in
fig.

15
in the s-channel as well as the t-channel. Since the internal momenta in these channels are restricted to
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\pm b/2
and
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha_{2}\pm b/2
, respectively, it follows that the corresponding fusion and braiding matrices are two-dimensional.

\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{3}
-\frac{b}{2}
\alpha_{2}
\alpha
s-channel
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{3}
-\frac{b}{2}
\alpha_{2}
\alpha^{\prime}
t-channel

Figure 15
:
A 4-point conformal block with one degenerate operator (in red) inserted is shown in both s- and t-channel. Here,
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\pm b/2
and
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha_{2}\pm b/2
.

In particular, if we define

\displaystyle F_{ss^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}:=F_{\alpha_{1}-s\frac{b}{2},\alpha_{2}-s^{\prime}\frac{b}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix},

(3.51)

we have that
[
15
]
18
18

18

These expressions can also be found in
[
34
, Appendix B]
.

F_{++}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}=\frac{\Gamma[b(2\alpha_{1}-b)]\Gamma[b(Q-2\alpha_{2})]}{\Gamma[b(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-\frac{b}{2})]\Gamma[b(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}+Q-\frac{b}{2})]}\,,

(3.52)

and,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} F_{-+}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}&=F_{++}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&Q-\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix},\\
F_{+-}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}&=F_{++}\begin{bmatrix}Q-\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix},\text{ and }\\
F_{--}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}&=F_{++}\begin{bmatrix}Q-\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&Q-\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}.\end{aligned}

(3.53)

It is convenient to introduce the notation
19
19

19

Later, around eq. (
3.130
), we will introduce masses
\widetilde{m}_{k}
such that
b\widetilde{m}_{k}=m_{k}
. Even later, in §
5.2
, we will re-introduce
\hbar
such that
b\widetilde{m}_{k}/\hbar=m_{k}
. In terms of the
\Omega
-deformation parameters, this means that
m_{k}=\epsilon_{1}\widetilde{m}_{k}
. Even though the
m_{k}
’s make the notation most efficient here, the
\widetilde{m}_{k}
’s are closest to the gauge theory masses.

\displaystyle m_{k}:=b(2\alpha_{k}-Q),

(3.54)

so that

F_{ss^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}=\frac{\Gamma[1+sm_{1}]\Gamma[-s^{\prime}m_{2}]}{\Gamma[\frac{1}{2}(1+sm_{1}-s^{\prime}m_{2}+m_{3})]\Gamma[\frac{1}{2}(1+sm_{1}-s^{\prime}m_{2}-m_{3})]}\,.

(3.55)

In this form, it is easy to see that the fusion matrices are invariant under the simultaneous exchanges

(s\leftrightarrow-s,\>m_{1}\leftrightarrow-m_{1}),\quad\text{ as well as }\quad(s^{\prime}\leftrightarrow-s^{\prime},\>m_{2}\leftrightarrow-m_{2}).

(3.56)

The first exchange in each parenthesis swaps rows/columns, and is thus equivalent to multiplication by an off-diagonal matrix. The second exchange in each bracket conjugates the momentum
\alpha_{1,2}\leftrightarrow Q-\alpha_{1,2}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} F_{(-s)s^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}&=F_{ss^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&Q-\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{ and }\\
F_{s(-s^{\prime})}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}&=F_{ss^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}Q-\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}.\end{aligned}

(3.57)

These identities will come in handy when we glue 3-punctured spheres in §
3.3
.

In the notation (
3.54
), the weights
\Delta_{\alpha_{k}}=\alpha_{k}(Q-\alpha_{k})
are given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha_{k}}=\frac{(1+b^{2})^{2}-m_{k}^{2}}{4b^{2}},

(3.58)

so that the
clockwise
braiding matrix is computed by

\displaystyle B^{-\frac{b}{2},\alpha_{k}}_{ss^{\prime}}:=B^{-\frac{b}{2},\alpha_{k}}_{\alpha_{k}-s\frac{b}{2}}\,\delta_{ss^{\prime}}=e^{-\frac{i\pi}{2}(b^{2}+1+sm_{k})}\delta_{ss^{\prime}}=q^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{i\pi}{2}(1+sm_{k})}\delta_{ss^{\prime}},

(3.59)

where
q=e^{-i\pi b^{2}}
.

Figure 16
:
The fusion and braiding matrices correspond to parallel transport of the
q
-hypergeometric equation on the 3-punctured sphere.

You may recognise the fusion matrices
F_{ss^{\prime}}
in equation (
3.55
) as the connection matrices for the hypergeometric differential equation. The braiding matrices
B_{ss^{\prime}}
in eq. (
3.59
) agree with this interpretation up to the factor of
q^{1/2}
. Indeed, the Lax equation on the 3-punctured sphere is described by a hypergeometric (or rather Riemann’s) differential equation. This implies that the Moore-Seiberg parallel transport is identical to its semi-classical limit, up to the overall factor of
q
.

3.2.2
BPZ equation and quantum oper connections

In the next few paragraphs, we interpret the BPZ equation as a
quantum oper connection
, and argue that the fusion and braiding matrices (
3.55
) are computing the monodromies of the quantum oper connection on the
3
-punctured sphere. The paths corresponding to the individual fusion and braiding matrices are illustrated in
fig.

16
.

We previously noted that the fusion matrices
F_{ss^{\prime}}
in equation (
3.55
) are equal to the connection matrices for the hypergeometric differential equation. However, observe that the sequence of Moore-Seiberg moves (illustrated in
fig.

17
) transporting the degenerate operator along a path trivial in homology (we have suppressed the internal momenta for brevity),

\displaystyle F\begin{bmatrix}-b/2&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{3}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\left(B^{-\frac{b}{2},\alpha_{2}}\right)\cdot F^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{3}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\left(B^{-\frac{b}{2},\alpha_{3}}\right)\cdot F^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{3}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\left(B^{-\frac{b}{2},\alpha_{1}}\right),

(3.60)

is equal to
q^{3/2}
times the identity
20
20

20

This is possible because the braiding and fusion matrices define a
projective representation
of the mapping class group on conformal blocks.
; for the hypergeometric equation this monodromy is simply the identity. So, let us first understand the difference between the standard hypergeometric equation and the BPZ equation.

Figure 17
:
Conformal block computation corresponding to moving the degenerate vertex operator
V_{1,2}(z)
along a trivial loop on the 3-punctured sphere.
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{3}
-\frac{b}{2}
\alpha_{2}
F
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{3}
-\frac{b}{2}
\alpha_{2}
B
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{3}
\alpha_{2}
-\frac{b}{2}
F^{-1}
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{3}
\alpha_{2}
-\frac{b}{2}
B
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{3}
\alpha_{2}
-\frac{b}{2}
F^{-1}
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{3}
-\frac{b}{2}
\alpha_{2}
B

Indeed, suppose we consider the BPZ equation (or null-vector decoupling equation) for the four-point conformal block
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{1,2}(z)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{z(1-z)}{b^{2}}\partial_{z}^{2}+(2z-1)\partial_{z}+\Delta_{1,2}+\frac{\Delta_{1}}{z}-\Delta_{4}+\frac{\Delta_{2}}{1-z}\right)\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{1,2}(z)=0

(3.61)

as a
standard
Fuchsian ODE on the 3-punctured sphere. Then, it can be brought in the form of Riemann’s differential equation – a slight generalisation of the hypergeometric differential equation – with local exponents

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lambda_{1}^{\pm}&=\frac{b^{2}+1}{2}\pm\frac{m_{1}}{2},\\
\lambda_{2}^{\pm}&=\frac{b^{2}+1}{2}\pm\frac{m_{2}}{2},\\
\lambda_{3}^{\pm}&=-\frac{2b^{2}+1}{2}\pm\frac{m_{3}}{2},\end{aligned}

(3.62)

at the punctures
z=0,1
and
\infty
respectively. Check that these exponents add up to 1, as they should for any second-order Fuchsian differential equation on the 3-punctured sphere. This ensures that the monodromies around trivial loops are indeed trivial.

Also, note that the local exponents at
z=0,1
agree with the eigenvalues

\displaystyle-q\,e^{\pm i\pi m_{k}}

(3.63)

of the square of the braiding matrix (
3.59
). Yet, the local exponents at
z=\infty
do not. This explains why the monodromy of the differential equation (
3.61
) around a trivial loop is trivial, but the monodromy in terms of braiding and fusion matrices could be non-trivial.

In a standard ODE, the local exponent at
z=\infty
is computed through the Möbius transformation
z\mapsto 1/z
, where
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}(z)
is treated as a function on the complex plane. But, the four-point function
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}(z)
transforms as a
\Delta_{1,2}
-differential, with

\displaystyle\Delta_{1,2}=-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{4}b^{2}.

(3.64)

This property, in particular, implies that the Möbius transformation
z\mapsto 1/z
will act non-trivially on the BPZ equation (
3.61
) for generic values of
b
. Taking this into account, we can reproduce the “correct” local exponents at all three punctures
z=0,1
and
\infty
.

Oper connections

Before we work this out in detail, note that
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}(z)
transforms as a
-\frac{1}{2}
-differential when
b\to 0
. As we explain in the following, this implies that the BPZ connection reduces to an
SL(2)
-oper connection in the semi-classical limit. Indeed, it is well known that the Lax equation transforms as an oper connection (see for instance
[
104
]
). Whereas oper connections are defined in such a way that their defining equation stays invariant under holomorphic coordinate transformations
21
21

21

See, for instance,
[
61
, §8.1]
for a discussion on oper connections in accessible terms.
, this is certainly not the case for the BPZ connection at general values of
b
.

Indeed, let us find out how the following general, second-order differential equation

\displaystyle y^{\prime\prime}(z)+q_{1}(z)y^{\prime}(z)+q_{2}(z)y(z)=0

(3.65)

transforms when
y(z)
is a
\Delta_{1,2}
-differential. Under a holomorphic coordinate transformation
z\mapsto z(w)
, we have that

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}(w)=y(z(w))(z^{\prime}(w))^{\Delta_{1,2}},

(3.66)

and thus

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widetilde{y}^{\prime}(w)&=(z^{\prime}(w))^{\Delta_{1,2}+1}\left(y^{\prime}(z(w))+\Delta_{1,2}\,\frac{z^{\prime\prime}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}\,y(z(w))\right),\\
\widetilde{y}^{\prime\prime}(w)&=(z^{\prime}(w))^{\Delta_{1,2}+2}\left(y^{\prime\prime}(z(w))+(2\Delta_{1,2}+1)\,\frac{z^{\prime\prime}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}\,y^{\prime}(z(w))\right)\\
&\quad+(z^{\prime}(w))^{\Delta_{1,2}}\Delta_{1,2}\left((\Delta_{1,2}-1)\,\frac{z^{\prime\prime}(w)^{2}}{z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}+\frac{z^{\prime\prime\prime}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)}\right)y(z(w)),\end{aligned}

(3.67)

This implies that the transformed differential equation

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}^{\prime\prime}(w)+\widetilde{q}_{1}(w)\widetilde{y}^{\prime}(w)+\widetilde{q}_{2}(w)\widetilde{y}(w)=0

(3.68)

can be written in terms of the original variables as

\displaystyle 0\,=\,y^{\prime\prime}(z(w))+\left(\frac{\widetilde{q}_{1}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)}+(2\Delta_{1,2}+1)\,\frac{z^{\prime\prime}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}\right)y^{\prime}(z(w))\,+

(3.69)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\widetilde{q}_{2}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}+\widetilde{q}_{1}(w)\Delta_{1,2}\,\frac{z^{\prime\prime}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)^{3}}+\frac{\Delta_{1,2}}{z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}\left((\Delta_{1,2}-1)\,\frac{z^{\prime\prime}(w)^{2}}{z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}+\frac{z^{\prime\prime\prime}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)}\right)\right)y(z(w)),

where we have not yet specified how the coefficients
q_{1}(z)
and
q_{2}(z)
transform.

In particular, for weight
\Delta_{1,2}=-1/2
and coefficient
q_{1}(z)=0
, the transformed differential equation reads

\displaystyle 0\,=\,y^{\prime\prime}(z(w))+\left(\frac{\widetilde{q}_{2}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}+\frac{1}{2z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}\frac{z^{\prime\prime}(w)^{2}}{z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}-\frac{z^{\prime\prime\prime}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)}\right)\right)y(z(w))

(3.70)

\displaystyle\qquad=y^{\prime\prime}(z(w))+\left(\frac{\widetilde{q}_{2}(w)}{z^{\prime}(w)^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\{w,z\}\right)y(z(w)),

where the brackets
\{\,.\,,\,.\,\}
denote the Schwarzian derivative.
This implies that
q_{2}(z)
should transform as a projective connection, i.e. as

\displaystyle\widetilde{q}_{2}(w)=(z^{\prime}(w))^{2}\left(q_{2}(z(w))-\frac{1}{2}\{w,z\}\right),

(3.71)

in order to preserve the form of the original equation under holomorphic coordinate transformations. The resulting object is called an
SL(2)
-oper connection. (Note that the Schwarzian
\{\,.\,,\,.\,\}
vanishes for Möbius transformations, in which case all complications disappear, so that
q_{2}(z)
just transforms as a quadratic differential.)

Local exponents for
\Delta_{1,2}
-differentials

In our more general setup, we have
\Delta_{1,2}=-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{4}b^{2}
and
q_{1}(z)\neq 0
instead. For instance, for large values of
z\gg 1
, we have

\displaystyle q_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\lambda^{+}_{3}+\lambda^{-}_{3}}{z}=-\frac{2b^{2}}{z},

(3.72)

\displaystyle q_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{+}_{3}\lambda^{-}_{3}}{z^{2}}=\frac{b^{4}+b^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{z^{2}}-\frac{m_{3}^{2}}{4z^{2}}.

In this generality, the form of the differential equation is not even preserved by Möbius transformations. Indeed, for
z(w)=-1/w
, we compute that eq. (
3.69
) turns into

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &0\,=\,y^{\prime\prime}(z(w))+\left(\widetilde{q}_{1}(w)\,w^{2}+3b^{2}w\right)y^{\prime}(z(w))\,+\\
&+\left(\widetilde{q}_{2}(w)\,w^{4}+\widetilde{q}_{1}(w)\left(1+\frac{3}{2}b^{2}\right)w^{3}+\frac{3}{2}b^{2}\left(1+\frac{3}{2}b^{2}\right)w^{2}\right)y(z(w)).\end{aligned}

(3.73)

If we assume that the
q_{k}(z)
transform as
k
-differentials under Möbius transformations, i.e. that
\widetilde{q}_{k}(w)=(z^{\prime}(w))^{k}\,q_{k}(z(w))
, we find that the new coefficients
\overline{\underline{q}}_{k}(w)
of the transformed BPZ equation, locally around
z(w)=0
, are given by

\displaystyle\overline{\underline{q}}_{1}(w)

\displaystyle=-2b^{2}w+3b^{2}w=-\frac{b^{2}}{z(w)}

(3.74)

\displaystyle\overline{\underline{q}}_{2}(w)

\displaystyle=\left(b^{4}+b^{2}+\frac{1}{4}-\frac{m_{3}^{2}}{4}\right)w^{2}-2b^{2}\left(1+\frac{3}{2}b^{2}\right)w^{2}+\frac{3}{2}b^{2}\left(1+\frac{3}{2}b^{2}\right)w^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{(b^{2}+1)^{2}}{4}-\frac{m_{3}^{2}}{4}\right)\frac{1}{z(w)^{2}}.

(3.75)

This implies that the local exponents are given by

\displaystyle\overline{\underline{\lambda}}_{3}^{\pm}=\frac{b^{2}+1}{2}\pm\frac{m_{3}}{2},

(3.76)

restoring the symmetry between the local exponents at the three punctures!

This confirms our claim that the asymmetry in the local exponents (
3.62
) is merely an artifact of not carefully taking into account the transformation properties of the BPZ equation. That is, the monodromies of the BPZ equation
are
honestly computed by the braiding and fusion matrices.

Moreover, we also see that the BPZ equation should be considered a
quantum oper connection
on the holomorphic bundle of conformal blocks
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}(z)
, that only reduces to a genuine oper connection in the semi-classical limit
b\to 0
. It then immediately follows that any monodromy (or parallel transport, in general) computed through the braiding and fusion matrices has an interpretation as a quantum monodromy (or quantum parallel transport). We call this the
Moore-Seiberg (MS) parallel transport
.

Fenchel-Nielsen gauge

So far, we have learned that quantum parallel transports can be computed using fusion and braiding matrices, that fusion matrices
F
correspond to connection coefficients for the hypergeometric differential equation, and that braiding matrices
B
match with local monodromy matrices up to a simple factor of
q
. In this subsection, we relate these observations to parallel transports in terms of FN coordinates, as calculated in §
2
using
\mathcal{W}
-nonabelianisation.

We introduce the MS parallel transports

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P^{\mathrm{MS}}_{a,b,c}&=F\begin{bmatrix}\cdot&-b/2\\
\cdot&\cdot\end{bmatrix}B^{-\frac{b}{2},\cdot}\quad\textrm{and}\\
(P^{\prime})^{\mathrm{MS}}_{a,b,c}&=F\begin{bmatrix}\cdot&-b/2\\
\cdot&\cdot\end{bmatrix}\left(B^{-\frac{b}{2},\cdot}\right)^{-1}.\end{aligned}

(3.77)

along the paths
\mathfrak{p}_{a,b,c}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{a,b,c}
on the 3-punctured sphere (which were introduced in
eq.

2.25
and
eq.

2.43
).
22
22

22

Unfortunately, there is a clash between the subscript
b
used to label the open paths on the 3-punctured sphere and the quantum parameter
b
appearing in arguments and exponents.

Since the expressions
eq.

3.55
for the fusion matrices in terms of the
m_{k}
are devoid of any
b
-factors, and the only
b
-factors in the braiding matrices are in the form of a uniform, overall factor of
q^{\pm 1/2}
in the front, we can conclude that the MS parallel transport matrices within the 3-punctured sphere are “identical” to their semi-classical limits upto an overall power of
q
. We summarise this as

\displaystyle P^{\mathrm{MS}}_{a,b,c}=q^{\frac{1}{2}}\,P^{\mathrm{Rie}}_{a,b,c}\quad\textrm{and/or}\quad(P^{\prime})^{\mathrm{MS}}_{a,b,c}=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,(P^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Rie}}_{a,b,c},

(3.78)

where
P^{\textrm{Rie}}
computes the same parallel transport matrix for the semi-classical Riemann equation.

The above MS parallel transports can be brought in a form very similar to that of the parallel transport matrices in FN coordinates computed in
eq.

2.32
. Indeed,
23
23

23

Here we have labeled the first column/row with
+
and the second column/row with
-
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P^{\mathrm{MS}}_{b}&\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}\,=\\
&=\,q^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{-\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}}\,\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma&\alpha\,\frac{T^{\textrm{\,Rie}}_{a}T^{\textrm{\,Rie}}_{b}}{T^{\textrm{\,Rie}}_{c}}\\
\beta\,\frac{T^{\textrm{\,Rie}}_{c}}{T^{\textrm{\,Rie}}_{a}T^{\textrm{\,Rie}}_{b}}&\gamma\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{iT_{b}^{\textrm{\,Rie}}}{\sqrt{M_{2}}}&0\\
0&\frac{\sqrt{M_{2}}}{iT_{b}^{\textrm{\,Rie}}}\end{pmatrix},\end{aligned}

(3.79)

and similarly for
P^{\mathrm{MS}}_{a,c}
. Here,
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
are in agreement with (the cyclically permuted version of) equations (
2.33
) and (
2.34
), under the identification

M_{k}=-\exp(-i\pi m_{k})=q\exp(-2\pi ib\alpha_{k})

(3.80)

between the abelian holonomies and the external momenta.
In contrast to
eq.

2.32
, the objects
T_{a,b,c}^{\text{Rie}}
are not abstract coordinates, but have explicit expressions

\displaystyle\left(\frac{iT_{b}^{\textrm{Rie}}}{\sqrt{M_{2}}}\right)^{2}=\,\frac{\Gamma[1-m_{1}]\Gamma[1-m_{2}]}{\Gamma[1+m_{1}]\Gamma[1+m_{2}]}\frac{\Gamma[\frac{1}{2}(1+m_{1}+m_{2}\pm m_{3})]}{\Gamma[\frac{1}{2}(1-m_{1}-m_{2}\pm m_{3})]},

(3.81)

where the product is over all four choices of signs.

Comparing
eq.

3.79
with
eq.

2.32
, we note two key differences. One of the differences is the prefactor
q^{1/2}\sqrt{-m_{1}/m_{2}}
. While the
q^{1/2}
term has its origin in the braiding matrix, the factor
\sqrt{-m_{1}/m_{2}}
is due to the determinant of the fusion matrix

\displaystyle\det F\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{2}&-b/2\\
\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}\end{bmatrix}=\det\begin{pmatrix}F_{--}&F_{-+}\\
F_{+-}&F_{++}\end{pmatrix}=-\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}

(3.82)

not being equal to 1. The latter can be eliminated through a diagonal (but non-equivariant) scaling of the basis of conformal blocks at the marked points.

The second difference is the factor
i/\sqrt{M_{2}}
in front of the twist coordinate
T_{b}^{\text{Rie}}
. This factor is rather harmless, as it can be gauged away by a diagonal (and equivariant)
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-transformation on the basis of conformal blocks at the marked points. Instead, we could also define

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{b}^{\textrm{\,Rie}}=\frac{iT_{b}^{\textrm{\,Rie}}}{\sqrt{M_{2}}},

(3.83)

so that the coefficients
\alpha
and
\beta
change into

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\alpha}=-i\,\sqrt{\frac{M_{1}M_{2}}{M_{3}}}\,\alpha&\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\beta}=+i\,\sqrt{\frac{M_{3}}{M_{1}M_{2}}}\,\beta=-i\,\sqrt{\frac{M_{1}M_{2}}{M_{3}}}\,\alpha\,.\end{aligned}

(3.84)

This makes explicit that the Moore-Seiberg parallel transport prefers a slightly different gauge compared to the one arising from the averaging procedure in
\mathcal{W}
-nonabelianisation.

Comparing eq. (
3.79
) with eq. (
2.32
), we conclude that in the semiclassical limit, where the quantum monodromy reduces to the Lax monodromy, the FN twist coordinates on the gauged 3-sphere
evaluate
to a product of gamma-functions, as expressed in eq. (
3.81
).

We may thus phrase the relation between eq. (
3.79
) and eq. (
2.32
) as follows. The monodromy representation of the semi-classical limit of the BPZ equation (i.e. Riemann’s differential equation) on the 3-punctured sphere is equivalent to the monodromy representation in FN coordinates on the gauged 3-sphere, when the latter coordinates are evaluated on the oper connection
\nabla^{\textrm{Rie}}
associated to Riemann’s differential equation. That is,

\displaystyle T_{a,b,c}(\nabla^{\textrm{Rie}})=T_{a,b,c}^{\textrm{Rie}}.

(3.85)

(Recall that the FN twist coordinate, defined through the twist-flow argument, is only determined up to adding a complex multiple of the FN length coordinate. Since
\log T
and
\log\widetilde{T}
differ by such a factor, the choice of
T
versus
\widetilde{T}
as exponentiated FN twist coordinates is not too important.)

3.2.3
Relation to the NRS proposal

The above expression (
3.85
) is closely related to the NRS proposal introduced in §
3.1.3
. Recall that the NRS proposal states that the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}
of a class S theory
T[C]
can be identified with the generating function of the Lagrangian submanifold of (Lax) opers for a choice of holomorphic Darboux coordinates. For (non-exponentiated) FN-type coordinates
\{a_{k},t_{k}\}
, this proposal can be rephrased as

\displaystyle t_{k}(\nabla^{\textrm{Lax}})=b\,\frac{\partial\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\vec{a})}{\partial a_{k}}.

(3.86)

To write down the precise form of the NRS proposal on the gauged 3-punctured sphere, consider the symplectic basis of A and B-cycles

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A_{a}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\underline{\gamma}^{2}_{3}-\underline{\gamma}^{1}_{1}\right),\qquad&B_{a}=\mathfrak{p}^{21}_{a},\\
A_{b}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\underline{\gamma}^{2}_{1}-\underline{\gamma}^{1}_{2}\right),\qquad&B_{b}=\mathfrak{p}^{21}_{b},\\
A_{c}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\underline{\gamma}^{2}_{2}-\underline{\gamma}^{1}_{3}\right),\qquad&B_{c}=\mathfrak{p}^{21}_{c},\end{aligned}

(3.87)

on its cover
\Sigma
. This basis is illustrated in
fig.

18
. In the above set of equations, the 1-cycle
\underline{\gamma}^{i}
is the lift to sheet
i
of the 1-cycle
\underline{\mathfrak{a}}
, while the open paths
\mathfrak{p}^{ij}
are the lifts, starting at sheet
i
and ending at sheet
j
, of the open paths
\mathfrak{p}
. Then, the NRS proposal, in the form presented in
[
65
]
24
24

24

Be aware of some notational differences with
[
65
]
. For instance, their
x
would correspond to our
\frac{M}{b}
.
, tells us that the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{eff}}
can be extracted from the relations

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} t_{a}(\nabla^{\textrm{Lax}})&=b\left(\frac{\partial\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}}{\partial m_{3}}+\frac{\partial\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}}{\partial m_{1}}\right),\\
t_{b}(\nabla^{\textrm{Lax}})&=b\left(\frac{\partial\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}}{\partial m_{1}}+\frac{\partial\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}}{\partial m_{2}}\right),\\
t_{c}(\nabla^{\textrm{Lax}})&=b\left(\frac{\partial\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}}{\partial m_{2}}+\frac{\partial\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}}{\partial m_{3}}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.88)

Figure 18
:
The 1-cycles
A_{b}
and
B_{b}
on the covering
\Sigma
of the 3-punctured sphere.

Remark 3.4
.

We should be mindful about the following. The Lax connection
\nabla^{\textrm{Lax}}
on the gauged 3-sphere
reduces
to Riemann’s connection
\nabla^{\textrm{Rie}}
on the 3-punctured sphere, but is
not
strictly equal to it. In the limit where we ”ungauge” the punctures, the superpotential on the gauged 3-sphere simplifies to the superpotential on the 3-punctured sphere, and thus only retains its perturbative (i.e. classical + one-loop) part. If we therefore evaluate the FN twist coordinates
t
on
\nabla^{\textrm{Rie}}
, instead of on the more general
\nabla^{\textrm{Lax}}
, we only expect to reproduce the perturbative part of the complete
\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}
on the gauged 3-sphere. We will get back to the full statement in §
3.3.2
.

From the equations (
3.81
) and (
3.85
), we read off that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} t_{a}(\nabla^{\textrm{Rie}})&\sim\log\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma[1-m_{3}]\Gamma[1-m_{1}]}{\Gamma[1+m_{3}]\Gamma[1+m_{1}]}\frac{\Gamma[\frac{1}{2}(1+m_{3}+m_{1}\pm m_{2})]}{\Gamma[\frac{1}{2}(1-m_{3}-m_{1}\pm m_{2})]}},\\
t_{b}(\nabla^{\textrm{Rie}})&\sim\log\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma[1-m_{1}]\Gamma[1-m_{2}]}{\Gamma[1+m_{1}]\Gamma[1+m_{2}]}\frac{\Gamma[\frac{1}{2}(1+m_{1}+m_{2}\pm m_{3})]}{\Gamma[\frac{1}{2}(1-m_{1}-m_{2}\pm m_{3})]}},\\
t_{c}(\nabla^{\textrm{Rie}})&\sim\log\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma[1-m_{2}]\Gamma[1-m_{3}]}{\Gamma[1+m_{2}]\Gamma[1+m_{3}]}\frac{\Gamma[\frac{1}{2}(1+m_{2}+m_{3}\pm m_{1})]}{\Gamma[\frac{1}{2}(1-m_{2}-m_{3}\pm m_{1})]}},\end{aligned}

(3.89)

where all products are over all combinations of signs, and where
\sim
means that this equation holds up to multiplicative factors in the masses
M_{k}
. Making use of the identities

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma[1+X]^{2}}{\Gamma[1-X]^{2}}=-\frac{\Gamma[1+X]}{\Gamma[-X]}\frac{\Gamma[X]}{\Gamma[1-X]},

(3.90)

and

\displaystyle\partial_{X}\Upsilon(\kappa+\lambda X)=\gamma\log\frac{\Gamma[\kappa+\lambda X]}{\Gamma[1-\kappa-\lambda X]},

(3.91)

for the
\Upsilon
-function defined by

\displaystyle\Upsilon(X)=\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{X}\log\frac{\Gamma(X^{\prime})}{\Gamma(1-X^{\prime})}dX^{\prime},

(3.92)

we find that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})&=\frac{1}{2b}\,\Upsilon(\pm m_{1})+\frac{1}{2b}\,\Upsilon(\pm m_{2})+\frac{1}{2b}\,\Upsilon(\pm m_{3})\\
&\qquad-\frac{1}{2b}\,\Upsilon\left(\frac{1}{2}(1-m_{1}\pm m_{2}\pm m_{3})\right),\end{aligned}

(3.93)

where implicit sums are taken over all combinations of signs,
up to quadratic terms in the masses
m_{k}
(i.e. up to the classical contributions to
\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}
).

This correctly reproduces the one-loop contribution to
\widetilde{W}_{\textrm{eff}}
associated with the gauged 3-sphere (up to quadratic terms in the masses
m_{k}
). Indeed, the 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
field theory corresponding to the gauged 3-punctured sphere is the
SU(2)^{3}
gauge theory coupled to a trifundamental half-hypermultiplet. The first line in the expression (
3.93
) enumerates the one-loop contributions for the three gauge multiplets (with Coulomb parameters
m_{1,2,3}
). The second line computes the one-loop contribution for the trifundamental half-hypermultiplet (with masses
\pm m_{1}\pm m_{2}\pm m_{3}
).
25
25

25

See
[
60
]
for more details on this theory and half-hypermultiplets.

Note that the expression (
3.93
) is also closely related to the
square-root
of the semi-classical limit of the Liouville three-point function
C(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
from eq. (
3.13
)

[
109
,
104
]
. Indeed, we have that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\lim_{b\to 0}b\log\Gamma_{b}(x)\right)=\log\sqrt{\frac{b^{-bx}}{2\pi b}}\,\Gamma(bx),\end{aligned}

(3.94)

(see for instance
[
67
, Appendix A]
), so that

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to 0}\log\Upsilon_{b}(x)=\int dx\log b^{-2-2bx}+\frac{1}{b}\,\Upsilon(bx),

(3.95)

The semi-classical limit of the Liouville three-point function (
3.13
) is therefore given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lim_{b\to 0}\log C(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})&=\frac{1}{b}\,\Upsilon(-m_{1})+\frac{1}{b}\,\Upsilon(-m_{2})+\frac{1}{b}\,\Upsilon(-m_{3})\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{b}\,\Upsilon\left(\frac{1}{2}(1-m_{1}\pm m_{2}\pm m_{3})\right)+\mathcal{O}(\log b).\end{aligned}

(3.96)

Relations similar to eq. (
3.88
) came up independently in the work of Lisovyy and Naidiuk
[
77
]
, where they were proven and named ”Trieste formulae”, as a reference to analogous equations in
[
17
]
. Here, we want to emphasise that these relations are simply an example of more general equations that can be extracted from the ”geometric recipe”
[
64
]
, based on the NRS proposal
[
93
]
.

3.3
Moore-Seiberg monodromies on arbitrary surfaces

Thus far, we have only considered MS parallel transports within a gauged 3-punctured sphere. In the following, we construct MS parallel transports on arbitrary surfaces through the gluing construction.

Fix an arbitrary (possibly punctured) Riemann surface
C
together with a pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
. Choose a path groupoid on
C
that respects the pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
and is homotopic to the path groupoid illustrated in fig.
10
when restricted to any pair of pants.
26
26

26

If a pair of pants has punctures instead of boundary components, we may choose to ”gauge” the punctures.
Then, the MS parallel transport along any path
\mathfrak{p}
on
C
may be computed by decomposing the path
\mathfrak{p}
with respect to this groupoid, as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 78\relax}}\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 26\relax}}\cdots\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 74\relax}},

(3.97)

where each
\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 77\relax}}
is an open path, embedded in a single pair of pants, that connects two boundary components, and is thus homotopic to either
\mathfrak{p}_{a,b,c}
or
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{a,b,c}
. The MS parallel transport along
\mathfrak{p}
is then a concatenation of the MS parallel transports

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P^{\mathrm{MS}}_{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 77\relax}}&=F\begin{bmatrix}\cdot&-b/2\\
\cdot&\cdot\end{bmatrix}B^{-\frac{b}{2},\cdot}\quad\textrm{and}\\
(P^{\prime})^{\mathrm{MS}}_{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 77\relax}}&=F\begin{bmatrix}\cdot&-b/2\\
\cdot&\cdot\end{bmatrix}\left(B^{-\frac{b}{2},\cdot}\right)^{-1},\end{aligned}

(3.98)

and the braiding matrices.

Figure 19
:
A five-point conformal block including a single degenerate insertion on the 4-punctured sphere with a marked point. The internal momenta
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
differ by
\pm b/2
. The internal momentum through the pants cycle depends on which side of the pants tube the degenerate operator is inserted. If it is inserted to the left the internal momentum through the pants cycle is
\alpha
, whereas if it is moved to the right of the pants tube, the internal momentum through the pants cycle is given by
\alpha^{\prime}
.
