. The other off-diagonal component vanishes similarly. In total, we find
P_{\mathrm{FN}}^{q}[\mathfrak{p}_{1}]=P_{\mathrm{FN}}^{q}[\mathfrak{p}_{2}]
, and this establishes invariance under an isotopy across the double wall.

Figure 37
:
Equivalence between
Y_{\iota}^{11}Y_{2,L}^{11}Y_{m}^{11}Y_{2,R}^{12}Y_{f}^{22}
(on the left) and
Y_{\iota}^{11}Y_{2,L}^{12}Y_{m}^{22}Y_{2,R}^{22}Y_{f}^{22}
(on the right) inside
\mathbb{A}
. (We only show a single branch-cut to avoid clutter.)

4.2.2
Isotopy invariance across branch-point

We now show that
q
-nonabelianisation remains unchanged if we isotope a path across a branch-point as illustrated in
fig.

38
. We will once again make use of the fact that ordinary nonabelianisation is invariant under this isotopy. So, instead of trying to explicitly evaluate the
q
-nonabelianisation of the two paths
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
, we simply count the
q
-factors of each lift. The (projections of the) various lifts of
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
are illustrated in
fig.

39
. The detours of the path
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
are captured by
S^{q\mathrm{FN}}
, and it is also easy to see that
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
picks up no winding factors. So, only the lift starting and ending on sheet
1
is accompanied by a non-trivial
q
-factor, namely,
q^{-1}
(c.f.
eq.

4.26
), and the rest of the lifts receive no
q
-contributions.

Figure 38
:
Isotopy move across a branch-point.

We now compare this with the open path lifts of
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
. The detours of
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
are encoded in
(S^{q\mathrm{FN}})^{-1}
. So, in total, there are three potential sources of
q
-factors for its lifts:

1.

a winding factor
q^{-1}
if the winding takes place on sheet
2
,

2.

a detour-factor
q
for every detour from sheet
2
to sheet
1
(c.f.
eq.

4.26
), and finally,

3.

a skein-factor
q^{-1}
from resolving the self-intersection of a lift with two detours.

The lifts of
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
that start and end on sheet
1
either undergo no detours or two detours. They are illustrated in
fig.

40
. The lift that undergoes no detour is accompanied by a winding factor
q^{-1}
that matches that of
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
. For the lift that undergoes two detours, both detours are from sheet
1
to
2
, and do not contribute any
q
-factors. Furthermore, the winding factor is also
1
. But it does self-intersect, resolving which we get a factor of
q^{-1}
.

On the other hand, the lifts of
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
that start and end on sheet
2
also undergo no detours or two detours. However, its
q
-factors are all flipped. The lift that undergoes no detours now winds on sheet
1
, and hence, carries a trivial
q
-factor. Likewise, the lift that undergoes two detours, now picks up a winding factor
q^{-1}
, a detour-factor
q^{2}
and a skein factor
q^{-1}
from resolving the self-intersection, which all effectively cancel each other.

Figure 39
:
Projections of the lifts of the open path
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
to
C
with source and target sheets indicated. Only the lift from sheet
1
to sheet
1
has a non-trivial
q
factor.

Figure 40
:
Projections of the lifts of the open path
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
to
C
with source and target sheets indicated.

The lifts of
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
that start on sheet
1
and end on sheet
2
either undergo a
1\to 2
detour at the “first wall” or a
2\to 1
detour at the “second wall” (see
fig.

40
). First of all, there is no
q
-contribution from self-intersections in this case. Second, the former lift has no detour-factor and winding factor while the latter is accompanied by a winding factor
q^{-1}
and a detour-factor
q
which cancel each other. Ultimately, both lifts are accompanied by trivial
q
-factors. The same holds for lifts of
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
that start on sheet
2
and end on sheet
1
. From this, we can conclude straight away that
q
-parallel transport along
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
are the same. This establishes consistency of the average quantum FN S-matrices under isotopies across branch-points.

Remark 4.4
.

The argument above also extends to a small closed loop
L
around the simple branch-point. Since the local foliation of
C
around such a branch-point is topologically the same for any phase, it is always possible to isotope the loop such that there are no exchanges. By a further isotopy, the loop can also be arranged so that it is travelling upwards when crossing any trajectory (see
fig.

41
for an example). Let us then decompose
L=L_{u}\circ L_{d}
into a component
L_{u}
(shown in dark green in
fig.

41
) that is an upwards-travelling open path and
L_{d}=L\backslash L_{u}
(shown in light green). The
q
-nonabelianisation of
L
is then given by

\psi^{q}(L)=\psi^{q}(L_{u})\circ\psi^{q}(L_{d})=P^{q}(L_{u})\circ\psi^{q}(L_{d}).

(4.40)

Through construction,
\psi^{q}(L_{d})
only consists of direct lifts, and we have already established that the
q
-parallel transport map is invariant under an isotopy across a branch-point. It then follows that
\psi^{q}(L)
is also invariant under an isotopy across a simple branch-point.

Figure 41
:
On the left: example of a small loop (in green) around a simple branch-point, projected onto
C
. On the right: the same configuration (without the spectral network trajectories and the branch-cuts), but now projected onto a plane in
C\times I
, perpendicular to one of the spectral network trajectories.

4.2.3
First-principle derivation

We would now like to derive
eq.

4.26
starting from the rules of
q
-nonabelianisation listed in §
4.1
. Consider the infinitesimal path
\varepsilon\subset C\times I
, across the double wall marked by
S_{2}
in
fig.

33
, that starts on
C\times\{0\}
at the base-point to the left of the double wall and ends on
C\times\{1\}
at the base-point to its right. In each resolution
\pm
, we may decompose
\varepsilon
into three infinitesimal subpaths
\iota
,
m
and
f
, where
\iota
is the segment of
\varepsilon
before it intersects the double wall,
m
is the segment that lies in between the first and the second wall, and
f
is the segment after the double wall. The nonabelian
q
-parallel transport along
\varepsilon
is a sum of abelian
q
-parallel transports along all possible lifts. We can decompose it into the form

P^{q,-}_{\varepsilon}=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{\iota}^{11,-}&0\\
0&Y_{\iota}^{22,-}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&s^{12,-}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{m}^{11,-}&0\\
0&Y_{m}^{22,-}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
t^{21,-}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{f}^{11,-}&0\\
0&Y_{f}^{22,-}\end{pmatrix}

(4.41)

in the American resolution, and

P^{q,+}_{\varepsilon}=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{\iota}^{11,+}&0\\
0&Y_{\iota}^{22,+}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
t^{21,+}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{m}^{11,+}&0\\
0&Y_{m}^{22,+}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&s^{12,+}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{f}^{11,+}&0\\
0&Y_{f}^{22,+}\end{pmatrix}

(4.42)

in the British resolution, where
Y_{A}^{ii,\pm}
denotes the quantum generator corresponding to the lift of
A\in\{\iota,m,f\}
to the
i
th sheet in the
\pm
resolution, and where the off-diagonal entries
s^{12,\pm}
and
t^{21,\pm}
encode the infinitely many detours associated with the family of
12
and
21
-trajectories, respectively.

Let us, for example, detail how this works for the lift
\ell_{1}
shown in
fig.

42
. The lift
\ell_{1}
corresponds to one of the infinitely many detours encoded in
t^{+}
: it is a single
(21)
-detour around the bottom branch-point in the British resolution. The decomposition of the lift
\ell_{1}
in homology is simply
2\gamma_{1}^{\textrm{odd}}+\delta_{\ell}
, where we recall that

\displaystyle\eta^{\textrm{odd}}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\eta^{(1)}-\eta^{(2)}\big)\text{ for any }\eta\in H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}),

(4.43)

and
\delta_{\ell}
is a detour around the bottom branch-point (see
fig.

42
for notation). A similar decomposition holds inside
\mathbb{A}
, except that one now needs to be careful with
q
-contributions that arise from applying the
GL(1,\mathbb{C})
-skein relations.

For instance, consider the open path lift
\widetilde{\underline{\fraka}}_{1}^{11}\subset\Sigma\times I
of the
based
loop
\underline{\fraka}_{1}\subset C
with base-point
z_{B}
as shown in
fig.

10
. Note that the open path
\widetilde{\underline{\fraka}}_{1}^{11}
is unique inside
\mathbb{A}
because we have fixed a base-point for
\underline{\fraka}_{1}
. Then, using the abelian skein relations, we can write

Y_{\underline{\fraka}}^{11}=q^{-1}X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}}Y_{z_{B}}^{11},

(4.44)

where
X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}}
is the quantum torus generator corresponding to the closed 1-cycle
\underline{\gamma}^{(1)}_{1}\subset\Sigma
that we obtain by forgetting the base-point of
\underline{\fraka}_{1}
, and
Y_{z_{B}}^{11}
is the generator corresponding to the open path
\{z_{B}^{(1)}\}\times I
. There is yet another way to write
Y_{\widetilde{\underline{\fraka}}}^{11}
as

Y_{\underline{\fraka}}^{11}=q\,X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime(1)}}Y_{z_{B}}^{11}

(4.45)

where
X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime(1)}}
is the quantum torus generator corresponding to the 1-cycle
\underline{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1}\subset\Sigma
illustrated in
fig.

43
. While the difference between the two closed cycles
\underline{\gamma}_{1}
and
\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime}
is apparent in this set up – namely,
\underline{\gamma}_{1}
is a closed cycle around the puncture, and
\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime}
is a closed cycle encircling the puncture and the open path – this distinction is subtle for a general pants cycle. We will expand on this in §
4.4
.

Figure 42
:
Decomposition of the lift
\ell_{1}
(on the left) in terms of
\underline{\gamma}_{1}
and
\delta_{\ell}
(on the right). (The path
\ell_{1}
is a detour path in the British resolution of the FN molecule drawn in the background in light blue.)

Figure 43
:
Two different ways to decompose the open path on the left in terms of the quantum torus generators
X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}},X_{\underline{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1}}
and
Y^{11}_{z_{B}}
.

Returning to our example, we can decompose the open path
Y_{\ell_{1}}^{+}
as

Y_{\ell_{1}}^{21,+}=X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}}^{+}\,X_{-\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}}^{+}\,Y_{\delta_{\ell}}^{21,+}=\big(X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{\textrm{odd}}}^{+}\big)^{2}\,Y^{21,+}_{\delta_{\ell}}.

(4.46)

More generally, a generic path in
t^{+}
that undergoes a single
21
-detour around the bottom branch-point while winding
k
times on each sheet, contributes as

Y_{\ell_{k}}^{21,+}=\big(X^{+}_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}}\big)^{k}\,\big(X^{+}_{-\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}}\big)^{k}\,Y_{\delta_{\ell}}^{21,+}=\big(X^{+}_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{\textrm{odd}}}\big)^{2k}\,Y_{\delta_{\ell}}^{21,+}\quad\text{ for }k\geq 0.

(4.47)

In §
3.3.2
, we learned that quantum spectral coordinates
X_{\gamma}
act as operators on the space of Liouville conformal blocks. In particular, the quantum generators
X_{\underline{\gamma}_{k}^{\textrm{odd}}}
, associated with odd 1-cycles
\underline{\gamma}_{k}^{\textrm{odd}}
around the punctures
z_{k}
, act by multiplication by the mass eigenvalues
M_{k}
. We denote this by

\displaystyle X_{\underline{\gamma}_{k}^{\text{odd}}}\rightsquigarrow M_{k}.

(4.48)

The same holds true in the presence of open paths (recall the 3d picture from §
4.1.3
). This implies that
eq.

4.47
evaluates to

X_{\ell_{k}}^{21,+}\rightsquigarrow M_{1}^{2k}\,X_{\delta_{\ell}}^{21,+}.

(4.49)

Remark 4.5
.

Since
X_{\underline{\gamma}^{\textrm{odd}}}
evaluates to
M
, one might think that the
X_{\underline{\gamma}^{(i)}}
themselves evaluate to
M^{\pm 1}
. But this is
not
true in the presence of open paths because

\displaystyle\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}+\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}+\underline{\gamma}_{2}^{(1)}+\underline{\gamma}_{2}^{(2)}+\underline{\gamma}_{3}^{(1)}+\underline{\gamma}_{3}^{(2)}\neq 0\in H_{1}(\Sigma-\pi^{-1}(\mathbb{X});\mathbb{Z}).

(4.50)

Instead, in the example above, we find that

X_{\underline{\gamma}^{(1)}}\rightsquigarrow qM,\quad\text{ while }X_{-\underline{\gamma}^{(2)}}\rightsquigarrow q^{-1}M.

(4.51)

More generally, the result depends on the number of open paths in the configuration.

Figure 44
:
We can further write
\delta_{\ell}
in terms of a detour
\delta_{u}
around the upper branch-point. The first two figures show the 2d projections, and the last figure the 3d arrangement. We have suppressed two out of the three branch-cuts in the third figure for clarity.

It is more convenient to write the detour around the bottom branch-point
\delta_{\ell}
in terms of a detour around the top branch-point
\delta_{u}
, as shown in
fig.

44
. This allows us to write

Y_{\delta_{\ell}}^{21,+}=X_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{b}}}^{+}\,Y_{\delta_{u}}^{21,+}

(4.52)

where
\gamma_{\mathfrak{b}}
is a closed 1-cycle that encircles both branch-points.
39
39

39

Note that this is also the sequence of moves that relates the primed and unprimed off-diagonal generators
Y^{ij}
and
Y^{{}^{\prime}ij}
in
eq.

4.26
and
eq.

4.30
.
In order to evaluate
X_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{b}}}
, we will have to write
\gamma_{\mathfrak{b}}
in terms of the odd mass cycles. In homology, this is simply

\gamma_{\mathfrak{b}}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}+\underline{\gamma}_{2}^{(1)}-\underline{\gamma}_{3}^{(2)}-\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}-\underline{\gamma}_{2}^{(2)}+\underline{\gamma}_{3}^{(1)}\big)\in H_{1}^{\textrm{odd}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}).

However, in the presence of the open path
d_{u}
, this relation is no longer valid inside
\mathbb{A}
. Using the abelian skein relations, we can equivalently write
\gamma_{\mathfrak{b}}
as a closed cycle on sheet
1
or sheet
2
(also shown in
fig.

45
). Only
\gamma_{\mathfrak{b}}^{(1)}
has a non-trivial intersection pairing with
\delta_{u}
. So, the above relation is modified to

\displaystyle[\gamma_{\mathfrak{b}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg([\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}]+[\nu]+[\underline{\gamma}_{2}^{(1)}]-[\underline{\gamma}_{3}^{(2)}]-[\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}]-[\underline{\gamma}_{2}^{(2)}]+[\underline{\gamma}_{3}^{(1)}]\bigg)\in\mathbb{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[\nu]+[\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{\textrm{odd}}]+[\underline{\gamma}_{2}^{\textrm{odd}}]+[\underline{\gamma}_{3}^{\textrm{odd}}]

(4.53)

where
\nu
is a small clockwise loop around
d_{u}
. This decomposition yields

X^{+}_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{b}}}\rightsquigarrow q^{-1}\frac{M_{1}M_{2}}{M_{3}},

(4.54)

where we have used that
X_{\nu}=q^{-2}
. Recall that not all three mass eigenvalues are defined symmetrically (see
eq.

2.26
).

This concludes the decomposition of the open path generator
Y^{21,+}_{\ell_{k}}
as

\displaystyle Y^{21,+}_{\ell_{k}}\rightsquigarrow q^{-1}M_{1}^{2k+1}M_{2}M_{3}^{-1}\,Y^{21,+}_{\delta_{u}}.

(4.55)

Thus, the total contribution to the abelian
q
-parallel transport from
\ell_{k}
for all windings
k\in\mathbb{N}
is

q^{-1}\frac{X^{+}_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{\textrm{odd}}}X^{+}_{\underline{\gamma}_{2}^{\textrm{odd}}}X^{+}_{\underline{\gamma}_{3}^{\textrm{odd}}}}{1-X^{+}_{b\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{\textrm{odd}}}}\,Y_{\delta_{u}}^{21,+}\rightsquigarrow q^{-1}\frac{M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}^{-1}}{1-M_{1}^{2}}\,Y_{\delta_{u}}^{21,+}.

(4.56)

Figure 45
:
Two representations
\gamma_{b}^{(1)}
and
\gamma_{b}^{(2)}
of the 1-cycle
\gamma_{b}
in the presence of the open path
\delta_{u}
.

The rest of the exercise is similar in nature: we find a decomposition of every possible lift in our preferred basis for the space of open skeins
\mathbb{A}
on
\Sigma
, and evaluate the closed generators to get masses
M_{k}
. This is a straightforward exercise in abelian skein theory. We find that

P^{q,+}_{\varepsilon}\rightsquigarrow\begin{pmatrix}Y^{11,+}_{\varepsilon}&\frac{1-M_{1}^{-1}M_{2}M_{3}}{1-M_{2}^{2}}\,Y^{12,+}_{\delta_{u}}\\
q^{-1}\,\frac{M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}^{-1}-M_{1}^{2}}{1-M_{1}^{2}}\,Y^{21,+}_{\delta_{u}}&\left(1+\frac{M_{1}^{2}-M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}-M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}^{-1}+M_{2}^{2}}{(1-M_{1}^{2})(1-M_{2}^{2})}\right)Y^{22,+}_{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.57)

Note that the coefficients of the open path generators are identical to the semi-classical expression
eq.

2.28
, up to the quantum factor
q^{-1}
in the off-diagonal entry. Likewise, in the American resolution we find

P^{q,-}_{\varepsilon}\rightsquigarrow\begin{pmatrix}\left(1+\frac{M_{1}^{2}-M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}^{-1}-M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}+M_{2}^{2}}{(1-M_{1}^{2})(1-M_{2}^{2})}\right)Y^{11,-}_{\varepsilon}&\frac{1-M_{1}M_{2}^{-1}M_{3}}{1-M_{1}^{2}}\,Y^{12,-}_{\delta_{u}}\\
q^{-1}\,\frac{M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}^{-1}-M_{2}^{2}}{1-M_{2}^{2}}\,Y^{21,-}_{\delta_{u}}&Y^{22,-}_{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.58)

We are now in a position to determine the average quantum FN S-matrix
S^{q\mathrm{FN}}
. As in the semi-classical case
2.36
, we define the
ij
-th entry of
S^{q\mathrm{FN}}
as the entry-wise average

S^{q\mathrm{FN}}_{ij}=\sqrt{\left[P^{q,+}_{\varepsilon}\right]_{ij}\otimes\left[P^{q,-}_{\varepsilon}\right]_{ij}}.

(4.59)

This directly recovers the expressions for
\alpha,\beta
and
\gamma
in
eq.

4.26
. If we furthermore define the open path generators

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &Y^{11}_{2,L}=\sqrt{Y^{11,+}_{\varepsilon}\otimes Y^{11,-}_{\varepsilon}},\quad Y^{12}_{2,L}=\sqrt{Y_{\delta_{u}}^{12,+}\otimes Y_{\delta_{u}}^{12,-}},\\
&Y^{21}_{2,L}=\sqrt{Y_{\delta_{u}}^{21,+}\otimes Y_{\delta_{u}}^{21,-}},\quad Y^{22}_{2,L}=\sqrt{Y^{22,+}_{\varepsilon}\otimes Y^{22,-}_{\varepsilon}},\end{aligned}

(4.60)

we recover the average quantum Fenchel-Nielsen S-matrix
S^{q\mathrm{FN}}\in\mathrm{Mat}_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{A})
defined in
eq.

4.26
.

Instead, to write the quantum S-matrix in terms of the primed generators, we simply have to undo the relation
eq.

4.52
,

Y_{\delta_{u}}^{21,\pm}=q\,M_{1}^{-1}M_{2}^{-1}M_{3}Y_{\delta_{\ell}}^{21,\pm}.

(4.61)

We now define
Y_{2,L}^{{}^{\prime}21}=\sqrt{Y^{21,+}_{\delta_{\ell}}\otimes Y^{21,-}_{\delta_{\ell}}}
and
Y_{2,L}^{{}^{\prime}12}=\sqrt{Y^{12,+}_{\delta_{\ell}}\otimes Y^{12,-}_{\delta_{\ell}}}
. Then, by comparing the coefficients of the primed and unprimed generators, we immediately find

\beta^{\prime}=\frac{M_{3}}{M_{1}M_{2}}\,\beta,

(4.62)

and likewise for
\alpha^{\prime}
. This concludes the derivation of the quantum FN S-matrix.

4.3
Quantum Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates

The semi-classical limits of the abelian
q
-parallel transports
Y^{ij}_{L/R}
are not gauge-invariant, and therefore do not define functions on the moduli space of flat connections and cannot be consistently quantised. In contrast, the abelian parallel transports
M_{1,2,3}
along the lifts of the based pants cycles
\underline{\fraka}_{1,2,3}\subset C
, and the abelian parallel transports
T_{a,b,c}
along the lifts of the paths
\mathfrak{p}_{a,b,c}\subset C
(defined in §
2.3
)
are
gauge-invariant (since we fixed a trivialisation at the marked points), and thus can be quantised. Consequently, the semi-classical limits of the generators

\displaystyle Y^{ii}_{\underline{\fraka}_{1},\underline{\fraka}_{2},\underline{\fraka}_{3}}\quad\text{ and }\quad Y^{ij}_{\mathfrak{p}_{a},\mathfrak{p}_{b},\mathfrak{p}_{c}},

(4.63)

are gauge-invariant. Moreover, since any gauge-invariant abelian parallel transport can be expressed in terms of the spectral coordinates, it follows that the open path generators (
4.63
) form a basis for the space of all abelian
q
-parallel transports whose semi-classical limit is gauge-invariant. Thus, the (linear combination of) generators
eq.

4.63
(corresponding to the symplectic basis of 1-cycles (
3.87
)) have the right characteristics to be interpreted as exponentiated quantum FN length and twist spectral coordinates on the gauged 3-sphere.

As an example, consider the
q
-parallel transport along the (unique open path lift of the) path
\frakp_{b}\subset C\times I
in
eq.

2.25
. We show that we can read off the quantum Fenchel-Nielsen twist coordinates from this
q
-parallel transport. If we decompose
\frakp_{b}
into two segments
\iota
and
f
, where
\iota
is the segment before crossing the double trajectory and
f
is the segment after crossing the double trajectory (illustrated in
fig.

46
), the non-abelian
q
-parallel transport along
\frakp_{b}
is given by

P^{q}_{b}=\begin{pmatrix}0&Y^{12}_{\iota}\\
Y^{21}_{\iota}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma Y^{11}_{2,L}&\alpha Y^{12}_{2,L}\\
-q^{-1}\beta Y^{21}_{2,L}&\gamma Y^{22}_{2,L}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}qY^{11}_{f}&0\\
0&Y^{22}_{f}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\beta Y^{11}_{b,L}&\gamma Y^{12}_{b,L}\\
q\gamma Y^{21}_{b,L}&\alpha Y^{22}_{b,L}\end{pmatrix},

(4.64)

in terms of the open path generators
Y^{ij}_{b,L}
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &Y^{11}_{b,L}=Y^{12}_{\iota}Y^{21}_{2,L}Y^{11}_{f},\qquad Y^{12}_{b,L}=Y^{12}_{\iota}Y^{22}_{2,L}Y^{22}_{f},\\
&Y^{21}_{b,L}=Y^{21}_{\iota}Y^{11}_{2,L}Y^{11}_{f},\qquad Y^{22}_{b,L}=Y^{21}_{\iota}Y^{12}_{2,L}Y^{22}_{f}.\end{aligned}

(4.65)

Unlike the generators
Y_{2,L}^{ij}
in the quantum S-matrix (
4.26
), the generators
Y^{ij}_{b,L}
are gauge-invariant in the semi-classical limit, and admit a decomposition in terms of the generators (
4.63
). We can immediately identify

\displaystyle Y_{b,L}^{ij}=Y_{\frakp_{b}}^{ij},\quad\text{ for }~~i\neq j.

(4.66)

while the diagonal
Y_{b,L}^{ii}
can be expressed as the composition

Y^{ii}_{b,L}=Y^{ij}_{-\frakp_{a}}\,Y^{ji}_{-\frakp_{c}},\quad\text{ for }~~j\neq i.

(4.67)

Indeed, comparing the expression for
P^{q}_{b}
with its semi-classical limit
P_{b}
in
eq.

2.37
, we can simply verify that the off-diagonal
Y^{21}_{b,L}
reduces to the twist coordinate
T_{b}
, whereas the diagonal
Y^{ii}_{b,L}
reduce to a ratio of the twist coordinates
T_{a}
and
T_{c}
.

More precisely, recall that a basis of semi-classical spectral coordinates can be given in terms of the abelian parallel transport along a basis consisting of
odd
1-cycles in the relative homology on
\Sigma
. Similarly, a basis of quantum spectral coordinates can be labelled by
odd
open paths (see also the discussion around
remark

4.5
). The quantisation of the exponentiated FN twist coordinate
T_{b}
is therefore

Y_{\frakp_{b}}^{\textrm{odd}}:=Y_{\frac{1}{2}\big(\widetilde{\frakp}^{21}_{b}-\widetilde{\frakp}^{12}_{b}\big)},

(4.68)

where, in our conventions,
-\widetilde{\frakp}^{12}=(-\widetilde{\frakp})^{21}
. We may also informally think of
eq.

4.68
as the square-root of the product of the generators
Y_{b,L}^{21}
and
Y_{b,R}^{21}
. Likewise, the quantisation of the exponentiated FN length coordinate
M_{k}
is

Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{k}}^{\textrm{odd}}:=Y_{\frac{1}{2}\big(\widetilde{\underline{\fraka}}_{k}^{11}-\widetilde{\underline{\fraka}}_{k}^{22}\big)}.

(4.69)

We emphasise that this is a quantisation in terms of open skeins
Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}}\in\mathbb{A}
, and
Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{k}}^{\textrm{odd}}
should be treated differently from the quantum torus generators (closed skeins)
X_{\underline{\gamma}_{k}^{\textrm{odd}}}
.
40
40

40

Here, we distinguish between the open path generator
Y_{\underline{\fraka}}
and the quantum torus generator
X_{\gamma}
: the former is the quantisation of the abelian parallel transport (for a choice of base-point) around the puncture, while the latter is the quantisation of the trace of the aforementioned parallel transport. Although the two are identical in the semi-classical limit, their quantisations are elements of different quantum algebras.

Remark 4.6
.

There is a subtlety in writing down the
q
-parallel transport matrices
P^{q}
in
eq.

4.64
. We have assumed that the open path approaches its end-points transversely to the WKB foliation (see
fig.

46
). However, one could equally well choose a path that ends tangentially to the WKB foliation. In the latter case, the sheet-dependent winding factor is different. Consequently, special care is required when composing
P^{q}
-matrices to construct general
q
-parallel transport matrices.

Figure 46
:
Since the path
\frakp_{b}
extends between two marked points on
C
, the abelian parallel transports along all the lifts of
\frakp_{b}
are gauge-invariant.

4.3.1
Physical interpretation: action on conformal blocks

In §
4.1.3
, we realised the quantum torus generators
X_{\gamma}
as abelian Verlinde operators (i.e. Heisenberg-Verlinde operators) on the space of abelian conformal blocks on
\Sigma
.
41
41

41

Remember that the notion of abelian conformal block was still abstract in §
4.1.3
. We will attempt to make this concrete in terms of the free-field formalism in §
5.4.3
.
Similarly, the 3d Chern-Simons picture allows us to interpret the open path generators
Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}}
as operators moving the position of an abelian degenerate operator insertion inside an abelian conformal block
\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\vec{\alpha}}
(c.f.
eq.

4.24
),

Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}}\,\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\vec{\alpha}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z^{\textrm{in}})\ldots\big)=C(\ldots)\,\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\vec{\alpha}^{\prime}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z^{\textrm{out}})\ldots\big)\>\text{ for }\>\partial\widetilde{\frakp}=\{z^{\textrm{in}}\}\cup\{z^{\textrm{out}}\}.

(4.70)

In the following paragraphs, we shall detail how to compute the coefficients
C(\ldots)
for the basis generators
Y_{\underline{\fraka}}^{ii}
and
Y_{\frakp}^{ij}
on the gauged 3-sphere.

This is easiest to spell out for the generators
Y_{\underline{\fraka}}^{ii}
. Recall from
eq.

4.44
that the abelian skein relations allows us to write

Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}^{11}=q^{-1}X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}}Y_{z_{B}}^{11}\quad\text{ and }\quad Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}^{22}=q^{-1}\,X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}}Y_{z_{B}}^{22},

(4.71)

and likewise for the other
\underline{\fraka}_{k}
, where we remind the reader that
Y_{z_{B}}^{ii}
is the identity generator associated with the open path
\{z_{B}^{(i)}\}\times I
. Now, we know the evaluation of the quantum torus generators (see
remark

4.5
),

X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}}\rightsquigarrow qM_{1}\quad\text{ and }X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}}\rightsquigarrow\frac{q}{M_{1}},

(4.72)

in the presence of an open path.
This implies that we can write the action of the open path generator
Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}
as

\displaystyle Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}^{11}\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z_{B}^{(1)})\ldots\big)

\displaystyle=M_{1}\,\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z_{B}^{(1)})\ldots\big),\text{ and }

(4.73)

\displaystyle Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}^{22}\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z_{B}^{(2)})\ldots\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}_{1}\,\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z_{B}^{(2)})\ldots\big),

and similarly for the other
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
.
In this form, it is easy to see that the open path generators
Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1,2,3}}^{ii}
commute with the action of the quantum torus generators
X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1,2,3}}
. Further, observe that the above expressions
eq.

4.73
match with the MS braiding matrices in
eq.

3.59
, up to an overall
q
-factor.

For the open path generators
Y_{\frakp}^{ij}
, we remind ourselves that we positioned the loops
{\underline{\gamma}_{1,2,3}^{(i)}}
in between the punctures
z_{1,2,3}^{(i)}
and the marked points
z_{A,B,C}^{(i)}
(see
fig.

51
). This implies that the open path generators
Y_{\frakp_{a,b,c}}^{ij}
commute with the Heisenberg-Verlinde operators
X_{\underline{\gamma}_{1,2,3}^{(i)}}
. Since Liouville blocks on the gauged 3-sphere are fully fixed by the masses
M_{1,2,3}
, and also their abelianisation as a consequence, we conclude that the open path generators
Y_{\frakp_{a,b,c}}^{ij}
act multiplicatively on the abelian conformal block
\mathcal{F}^{\text{ab}}
. The multiplicative constant is the semi-classical limit of
Y_{\frakp}^{ij}
evaluated on the corresponding BPZ connection. Thus, we can write the action of
Y^{ij}_{\frakp_{a,b,c}}
as

Y_{\frakp_{a,b,c}}^{ij}\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z_{A,B,C}^{(i)})\ldots\big)=T^{\textrm{Rie}}_{a,b,c}\,\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z_{A,B,C}^{(j)})\ldots\big).

(4.74)

We can either read off the expressions for
T^{\textrm{Rie}}_{a,b,c}
from
eq.

3.81
42
42

42

Recall that when expressed as in
eq.

3.79
, the MS parallel transports
P^{\textrm{MS}}_{a,b,c}
are not
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant. So, a further basis rescaling is required at every marked point before the two quantum parallel transports
P^{\textrm{MS}}
and
P^{q}
can be compared (see the discussion around
eq.

3.82
). Thus, in all our comparisons with MS parallel transports, we assume that such a basis rescaling is implemented, and the MS parallel transport has been brought in the same gauge as
\mathcal{W}-
nonabelianisation.
or compute them directly knowing the generating function for the subspace of opers. Note that this statement is only true up to an overall power of
q
because it is only the evaluation of the quantum twist coordinate
Y_{\frakp_{a,b,c}}^{\textrm{odd}}
that is unambiguously defined (see
remark

4.5
).

Remark 4.7
.

As we will see in §
5.1
, the space of genus zero Heisenberg conformal blocks (for any number of generic vertex operator insertions with fixed momenta) is 1-dimensional. Then, any operator that commutes with the action of the Heisenberg-Verlinde operators (i.e. doesn’t modify the vertex operator insertions) must act multiplicatively.

This analysis extends even in the presence of multiple degenerate operators (inserted at any of the points in
\mathbb{X}
). The crucial thing to note is that all basis generators
eq.

4.63
always commute with the action of the Heisenberg-Verlinde operators. Then, for any number of composable open path generators, not necessarily associated to connected open paths, we can immediately conclude that the action of these generators is multiplicative.

In particular, this implies that the action of the quantum FN coordinates on the gauged 3-sphere is multiplicative, and that the multiplicative constant is the corresponding abelian parallel transport evaluated on the BPZ connection.
It is in this sense that we refer to the open path generators

\displaystyle Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1,2,3}}^{\textrm{odd}},Y_{\frakp_{a,b,c}}^{\textrm{odd}}

(4.75)

as (exponentiated) quantum Fenchel-Nielsen length and twist coordinates, respectively, on the gauged 3-sphere.

CFT-abelianisation

Below, we take a slight detour to investigate CFT-(non)abelianisation under the lens of
q
-parallel transports. We define the space of Liouville conformal blocks on the gauged 3-sphere
C
with one marked point
z^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{gCB}(C):=\bigoplus_{\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2},\,\alpha_{3}}\hom\bigg(\mathcal{V}_{1,2}(z^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{2}}(z_{2})\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{3}}(z_{3}),\,\mathbb{C}\bigg)^{\text{Ward id}},

(4.76)

as the space of gauged 3-point conformal blocks on
C
(with unspecified generic Virasoro representations at the punctures, as in
eq.

3.31
) and additionally one degenerate vertex operator insertion
V_{1,2}
at the marked point. Recall that this is an infinite-dimensional vector space, but that fixing the Virasoro representations at the punctures produces a two-dimensional subspace
\mathrm{CB}(C)
. This subspace can be equivalently characterised as an eigenspace of the Liouville-Verlinde operators
\mathcal{L}_{\underline{\gamma}_{1,2,3}}
.

Our aim in this paragraph is to find the corresponding abelianised conformal blocks. Below, through a Riemann-Hilbert type argument, we will specify their abelianisations as Heisenberg conformal blocks on the gauged 6-punctured sphere
\Sigma
with one marked point. In the argument we assume some ingredients from CFT-abelianisation that we explain in more detail in
section

5.4
(as well as that
q
-abelianisation and CFT-abelianisation commute).

The standard choices of a basis for
\mathrm{CB}(C)
can be characterised in terms of the position of the degenerate vertex operator. Examples are the s- and t-channel bases from
fig.

15
. This position, in turn, fixes the MS parallel transport around the closest puncture to be diagonal. For instance, in the s-channel, the two-dimensional basis is spanned by the Liouville blocks

\mathcal{F}_{s,\pm}^{\textrm{Liou}}\big(V_{1,2}(z_{B})\,V_{1}(z_{1})\,V_{2}(z_{2})\,V_{3}(z_{3})\big),

(4.77)

where
\pm
refers to the two allowed intermediate momenta
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\pm b/2
, and the MS parallel transport
P^{\textrm{MS}}_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}
can be written as

P^{\textrm{MS}}_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}=q\begin{pmatrix}M_{1}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{M_{1}}\end{pmatrix}=q\begin{pmatrix}Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}^{11}&0\\
0&Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}^{22}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.78)

Meanwhile, we know from
eq.

4.73
that the open path generators
Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}^{11}
and
Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}^{22}
act on abelian conformal blocks with (abelian) degenerate operator insertions at either of the lifts of
z_{B}
. The interplay between
q
-parallel transports and CFT-abelianisation furthermore tells us that
generic
Liouville vertex operators on
C
(at any of the three punctures) are abelianised into pairs of (conjugate) abelian vertex operators on the cover
\Sigma
(c.f.
eq.

5.183
). Together this implies that the open path generators
Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{1}}^{ii}
must act on the Heisenberg conformal blocks

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}_{(1)}\,(z_{B})&=\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}\bigg(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z_{B}^{(1)})\,\widetilde{V}_{1}^{\textrm{ab}}(z_{1})\,\widetilde{V}_{2}^{\textrm{ab}}(z_{2})\,\widetilde{V}_{3}^{\textrm{ab}}(z_{3})\bigg),\\
\mathcal{F}_{(2)}^{\textrm{ab}}\,(z_{B})&=\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}\bigg(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z_{B}^{(2)})\,\widetilde{V}_{1}^{\textrm{ab}}(z_{1})\,\widetilde{V}_{2}^{\textrm{ab}}(z_{2})\,\widetilde{V}_{3}^{\textrm{ab}}(z_{3}))\bigg),\end{aligned}

(4.79)

where
\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}(z)
denotes the abelianisation of the Liouville vertex operator
V(z)
(also see
eq.

5.183
and
eq.

5.226
).

From the identity
eq.

4.78
, it then follows that CFT-abelianisation must map

\displaystyle\psi^{\text{CFT}}_{\,\text{FN}}:\quad\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}_{s,+}^{\textrm{Liou}}\!\left(V_{1,2}(z_{B})\,V_{1}(z_{1})\,V_{2}(z_{2})\,V_{3}(z_{3})\right)\\[6.0pt]
\mathcal{F}_{s,-}^{\textrm{Liou}}\!\left(V_{1,2}(z_{B})\,V_{1}(z_{1})\,V_{2}(z_{2})\,V_{3}(z_{3})\right)\end{pmatrix}\;\mapsto\;\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}_{(1)}^{\textrm{ab}}\\[4.0pt]
\mathcal{F}_{(2)}^{\textrm{ab}}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.80)

In particular, this implies that we can identify the abelian blocks
\mathcal{F}_{(i)}^{\textrm{ab}}
with the standard hypergeometric solutions to the BPZ equation (
3.61
).
43
43

43

As shown in
[
18
]
, these solutions can be obtained from the perspective of the exact WKB analysis as a Borel sum of the asymptotic solutions in the direction of the FN phase.

We can similarly obtain the CFT-abelianisation of any other choice of basis for CB(
C
) by displacing the position of the degenerate operator insertion to any of the other punctures. Furthermore, more general open path generators
Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}}
(with distinct end-points) appear in the description of basis change transformations on the space of Liouville blocks as in
eq.

4.64
.

Remark 4.8
.

Note that CFT-abelianisation treats generic and degenerate vertex operator insertions differently. This differential treatment can be explained through the 6d
(2,0)
-theory in the background
\mathbb{R}^{4}\times C
that sources the AGT correspondence.

In the 6d theory, the punctures on
C
are the insertion points for codimension-
2
defects (space-filling defects inside
\mathbb{R}^{4}
) that describe the matter content of the 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
theory on
\mathbb{R}^{4}

[
49
]
. Under the action of the UV-IR map described in §
4.1.3
, the low-energy limit of the codimension-
2
defects fixes the flavour charges of the 4d theory on the Coulomb branch. Importantly, this is a one-to-one mapping between the UV and IR limits of the 4d theories.

The degenerate vertex operator insertions, in contrast, correspond to codimension-
4
defects in the 6d
(2,0)
-theory that specify a canonical surface defect in the 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
theory
[
4
]
. At low energies, this surface defect admits a discrete set of vacua (IR surface defects) which the UV-IR map selects from. That is, the UV-IR map is
not
one-to-one when acting on surface defects. In particular, the two basis elements in
eq.

4.79
can be explicitly identified with the two brane configurations engineering IR surface defects in the low-energy limit of the 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
theory (see for instance
[
28
, Fig. 14]
).

For a general surface
C

The story thus far admits a straight-forward extension to a general punctured surface
C
(possibly with gauged punctures). Any system of FN length-twist coordinates corresponds to a symplectic basis of based loops
\widetilde{\underline{\mathfrak{a}}}_{k,\Sigma}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{p,\Sigma}
, open paths
\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{k,\Sigma}
and based closed loops
\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{p,\Sigma}
on
\Sigma
. The corresponding open path generators

\displaystyle\{Y_{\underline{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}}^{\textrm{odd}},Y_{\mathfrak{b}_{k}}^{\textrm{odd}},Y_{\mathfrak{a}_{p}}^{\textrm{odd}},Y_{\mathfrak{b}_{p}}^{\textrm{odd}}\}\subset\mathbb{A}

(4.81)

define a set of quantum FN length-twist coordinates. And as before, all generators
Y^{ii}
can be realised as operators that move the position of an abelian degenerate operator an open path.

For an arbitrary genus
g
surface
\Sigma
, the space of conformal blocks (for fixed vertex operator insertions) depends on
g
additional complex parameters, corresponding to the eigenvalues of the Heisenberg-Verlinde operators
X_{\gamma_{p}}
for cycles
\gamma_{p}
around the pants tubes. We collectively denote these parameters as
\vec{\alpha}
in
\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\vec{\alpha}}
. The generators
Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{k}}^{ii}
, associated with paths around the punctures
z_{k}
, still admit a decomposition of the form
eq.

4.71
. Consequently, these generators commute with all the Heisenberg-Verlinde operators on
\Sigma
. In particular, the action of a generator
Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{k}}^{ii}
given by

Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{k}}^{ii}\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\vec{\alpha}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z_{p}^{(i)})\ldots\big)=\mathcal{X}_{\underline{\fraka}_{k}}^{ii}\,\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\vec{\alpha}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(z_{p}^{(i)})\ldots\big),

(4.82)

where
\mathcal{X}_{\underline{\fraka}_{k}}^{ii}
is the semi-classical limit of
Y_{\underline{\fraka}_{k}}^{ii}
, preserves the labels
\vec{\alpha}
.

Interestingly, we find in §
4.4
that the basis generator
Y_{\fraka_{p}}
, and its dual
Y_{\mathfrak{b}_{p}}
, associated with a pants tubes (in contrast to punctures), do not commute with the Heisenberg-Verlinde operator
X_{\gamma_{p}}
. As a result, the basis generators
Y_{\mathfrak{b}_{p}}
will act as operators

Y_{\mathfrak{b}_{p}}^{ij}\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\vec{\alpha}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(\partial\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}^{\textrm{in},(i)})\ldots\big)=\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{b}_{p}}^{ij}\,\mathcal{F}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\vec{\alpha}^{\prime}}\big(\widetilde{V}^{\textrm{ab}}_{\textrm{deg}}(\partial\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}^{\textrm{out},(j)})\ldots\big)

(4.83)

that shift the labels
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma_{p}}\in\vec{\alpha}
. We identify these operators with the quantum twist coordinates
\hat{T}^{\textrm{BPZ}}
from
eq.

3.126
.

Even more generally, for any system of spectral coordinates
\{\mathcal{X}_{\widetilde{\frakp}_{\Sigma}}^{\mathcal{W}}\}
on an arbitrary surface
C
(possibly with gauged punctures),
q
-nonabelianisation produces a basis of open path generators
\{Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}}^{\mathcal{W}}\}\subset\mathbb{A}
that quantises this system. The open paths
\widetilde{\frakp}\subset\Sigma\times I
are simply the unique open path lifts of the based paths
\widetilde{\frakp}_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma
that determine the basis of spectral coordinates. Note that the evaluation of
Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}}^{\mathcal{W}}
for generic spectral networks (say of Fock-Goncharov type) is much more complicated though, because the space of conformal blocks does not generically admit a diagonalisation with respect to any subset of these generators (see §
3.3.2
). Nonetheless, we propose a method to access this sector through CFT-abelianisation in §
5.4
.

4.3.2
Relation between
X
and
Y
-coordinates

Although the quantum torus generators
X_{\gamma}
and the open path generators
Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}}
are valued in different algebras, it is possible to obtain the
X
-coordinates from the
Y
-coordinates. Semi-classically, the trace function is simply the trace of the parallel transport computed for a choice of base-point, and for an abelian connection there is no distinction between the parallel transport around a (based) loop and its trace. Likewise in the quantum theory, the open path generator
Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}}
for any open path
\widetilde{\frakp}
, such that
\widetilde{\frakp}_{\Sigma}
is a loop based at
z_{b}\in\Sigma
, can be decomposed in terms of
X_{\gamma}
, where
\gamma
represents the homology class of
\widetilde{\frakp}_{\Sigma}
, and the identity open path
Y_{z_{b}}^{ii}
. This is precisely how we were able to read off the quantum FN coordinates from
q
-parallel transports.

Alternatively, the trace can be computed as follows. Trivialize the abelian gauge bundle at two nearby points
z_{b}
,
z^{\prime}_{b}\in\Sigma
and use this trivialization to identify the fiber at
z^{\prime}_{b}
with the dual of the fiber at
z_{b}
via the standard evaluation pairing. The semi-classical parallel transport along the closed path
p_{\Sigma}
then defines an endomorphism of the fiber, and contracting its output with its input using the pairing yields the ordinary trace of the holonomy.
This is the semi-classical translation of the computation of a Heisenberg–Verlinde operator in the abelian CFT.

z_{b}
z_{b}^{\prime}
t

t
z_{b}
z_{b}^{\prime}

Figure 47
:
Geometric representation of the cup and cap maps
\iota^{\text{ab}}
and
\pi^{\text{ab}}
that describe the fusion product between an abelian degenerate vertex operator and its conjugate.

Upon quantisation, the fibre at
z_{b}
is realised in terms of the identity open path
Y_{z_{b}}^{ii}
and the dual fibre at
z^{\prime}_{b}
in terms of the conjugate path
(Y_{z^{\prime}_{b}}^{ii})^{*}
, which is simply the open path
Y_{z^{\prime}_{b}}^{ii}
running in the opposite direction. The pairing is then implemented by a choice of
non-open
paths called the
cup
and the
cap
: the cup realises the identification
\iota^{\text{ab}}
of the dual fibre at
z^{\prime}_{b}
with the fibre at
z_{b}
and the cap provides the inverse identification
\pi^{\text{ab}}
. See
fig.

47
for a diagrammatic representation of the two paths. We emphasise that the above operators individually
cannot
be realised as open skeins. Whereas
Y_{z_{b}}^{ii}
and its conjugate are valued in different vector spaces (of upwards and downwards-travelling open paths, respectively), the cup and the cap are clearly not monotonic in the height-direction and thus not part of either spaces.

A quantum torus generator
X_{\gamma}
, for
[\gamma]=[\,\widetilde{\frakp}_{\Sigma}\,]\in H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
, can then be computed by first applying the cup operator
\iota^{\text{ab}}
, then the open path generator
Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}}
(with
\partial\widetilde{\frakp}^{\textrm{in/out}}=\{z_{b}\}
), and finally the cap operator
\pi^{\text{ab}}.
That is,

\displaystyle X_{\gamma}=\pi^{\text{ab}}\circ Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}}\circ\iota^{\text{ab}}.

(4.84)

Compared with similar maps
\iota,\pi
in the definition (
3.118
) of a Liouville Verlinde operator, the cups and caps in the abelian theory are much simpler. In particular, the fusion product of an abelian degenerate operator and its conjugate is just the identity operator, unlike the fusion product (
3.38
) in Liouville theory. This is a simple consequence of the fact that the tensor product between two
GL(1,\mathbb{C})
-representations (equivalently Heisenberg Fock spaces)
F_{\alpha_{1}}
and
F_{\alpha_{2}}
, with weights (equivalently momenta)
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
respectively, is the representation

\widetilde{F}_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\alpha_{2}}=\widetilde{F}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}

(4.85)

with weight
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
. Hence, we have that the cups and caps are defined as

\iota^{\textrm{ab}}:\widetilde{F}_{\textrm{id}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{F}_{\textrm{deg}}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\textrm{deg}}^{*}\quad\text{ and }\quad\pi^{\textrm{ab}}:\widetilde{F}_{\textrm{deg}}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\textrm{deg}}^{*}\longrightarrow\widetilde{F}_{\textrm{id}}.

(4.86)

Here,
\widetilde{F}_{\text{deg}}^{*}
is the representation labelling the downwards travelling open skein.

In the presence of gauged punctures, in addition to the quantum torus generators
X_{\gamma}
associated with closed loops
\gamma\in H_{1}^{\textrm{odd}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
, we also have quantum torus generators
X^{ij}_{\mathfrak{b}_{p}}
associated with relative homology classes
\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{p}^{ij}\in H_{1}^{\textrm{odd}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{X};\mathbb{Z})
connecting lifts of distinct marked points, which quantise the spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}^{ij}_{\mathfrak{b}_{p}}
. In order to define these
open
quantum torus generators, we introduce a family of
wedge operators

\iota^{\textrm{ab}}_{\alpha}:\widetilde{F}_{\alpha}\longrightarrow\widetilde{F}_{\textrm{deg}}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\pi^{\textrm{ab}}_{\alpha}:\widetilde{F}_{\textrm{deg}}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\alpha}\longrightarrow\widetilde{F}_{\alpha^{\prime}},

(4.87)

that correspond to the fusion product of an abelian vertex operator
\widetilde{V}^{\text{ab}}_{\alpha}
, with generic momentum
\alpha
, and an abelian degenerate operator
\widetilde{V}^{\text{ab}}_{\text{deg}}
. The corresponding skein diagrams are drawn in
fig.

48
. Unlike the cup
\iota^{\textrm{ab}}
and cap
\pi^{\textrm{ab}}
, the wedge operators
can
be realised inside
\mathbb{A}
.

4.4
On
q
-parallel transport for arbitrary Riemann surfaces

Consider an arbitrary Riemann surface
C
equipped with a pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
. Fix a path groupoid that respects
\mathcal{P}
and is homotopic to the path groupoid on the (gauged) three-punctured sphere when restricted to each pair of pants. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be an arbitrary open path on
C
. Since
q
-nonabelianisation is a local procedure, the
q
-parallel transport along
\mathfrak{p}
may be computed by decomposing the path
\mathfrak{p}
as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 78\relax}}\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 26\relax}}\cdots\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 74\relax}},

(4.90)

where each
\mathfrak{p}^{\textrm{\maljapanese\char 77\relax}}
is an (connected) open path embedded in a single pair of pants. Since the knowledge of the quantum FN S-matrix is sufficient to compute the
q
-parallel transport along any path inside the three-punctured sphere, the only remaining ingredient in the computation of the
q
-parallel transport map on an arbitrary Riemann surface is the gluing operation.

Recall the definition of a gauged puncture: we cut open a small disk around a puncture, and fix a marked point on the boundary. It is convenient to think of the thickened boundary component as an annulus, and of the marked point as lying on the inner boundary of the annulus (see
fig.

51
). Lifting this configuration to the cover
\Sigma
, this allows us to define an ordering between the closed pants-cycles and the open 1-cycles with respect to the marked point. The 1-cycles are defined such that they can be collapsed onto the boundary without any obstruction from the open paths. This is illustrated in
fig.

51
.

Figure 51
:
Ordering of the lifts of the pants-cycle
\gamma
and an open 1-cycle around a gauged puncture on
\Sigma
.

To glue two gauged punctures, we identify their boundary components as well as their marked points. However, this introduces the lateral ordering ambiguity illustrated in
fig.

52
. Recall from
fig.

43
that
\gamma
was the loop encircling the puncture while
\gamma^{\prime}
was the loop on the other side of the open path. On a tube though, there is no canonical way to distinguish between
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
. The best we can do is to fix a choice of
\gamma
versus
\gamma^{\prime}
on each pants-tube, and stay consistent with that choice. This is the (open) skein analogue of the CFT ambiguity in the choice of internal momenta on the pants-tube with a degenerate operator, as illustrated in
fig.

19
.

Figure 52
:
There is no canonical way to order the lifts of closed pants-cycles in the presence of an open path. The two possible choices differ by factor of
q^{\pm 2}
.

Indeed, let us see what happens to the definition of the mass eigenvalues as we move a pants cycle across an open path. Suppose the open path originates on sheet
1
in
\Sigma\times\{0\}
. Then the pants cycles
\gamma_{\text{left}}
and
\gamma_{\text{right}}
(denoted
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
previously) on either side of the open path are related as

\gamma_{\textrm{right}}^{\textrm{odd}}=-\gamma_{\textrm{left}}^{\textrm{odd}}-\frac{1}{2}\nu

(4.91)

where
\nu
is a small clockwise loop around the open path. This implies that the evaluation of the corresponding abelian Verlinde operators is related by

M_{\textrm{right}}=q/M_{\textrm{left}}.

(4.92)

Note that the orientation reversal of the pants cycle
\gamma_{\textrm{right}}\mapsto-\gamma_{\textrm{left}}
across the pants tube inverts the mass on the RHS. On the other hand, if the open path originates on sheet
2
in
\Sigma\times\{0\}
, the orientations of the closed cycles are reversed, and

\gamma_{\textrm{right}}^{\textrm{odd}}=-\gamma_{\textrm{left}}^{\textrm{odd}}+\frac{1}{2}\nu,

(4.93)

so that

M_{\textrm{right}}=q^{-1}/M_{\textrm{left}}.

(4.94)

Thus, depending on whether the open path originates on sheet
1
or sheet
2
, the mass eigenvalue is shifted by a factor of
q^{\pm 1}
. Suppose we wish to write all the expressions in terms of
M_{\textrm{left}}
. Then, we can collect the above action into an operator

\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{q}^{-1}&0\\
0&\Delta_{q}\end{pmatrix}\bigg(\dots\bigg)^{\textrm{op}}=\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{q}&0\\
0&\Delta_{q}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\dots\bigg)^{\textrm{op}},

(4.95)

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{q}^{\pm 1}(M)=q^{\pm 1}M

(4.96)

is a difference operator acting on the length coordinate
M
, and the superscript ”
op
” denotes orientation reversal across the pants tube. Note that
\Delta_{q}
is precisely the difference operator
\widehat{T}
that we encountered in
eq.

3.114
. That is,
q
-parallel transports across a pants-tube is obtained by inserting the difference operator
\widehat{T}
.

Remark 4.9
.

Suppose
\widetilde{\frakp}_{p}
is an open path that starts and ends on a pants tube labelled by the pants cycle
\gamma_{p}
. In that case, we may equivalently think of the open path generator
Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}_{p}}\in\mathbb{A}
as being implicitly composed with wedge operators
\iota^{\textrm{ab}}_{\alpha_{p}},\pi^{\textrm{ab}}_{\alpha_{p}}
, and identify
Y_{\widetilde{\frakp}_{p}}
with the open quantum torus generator
X_{\widetilde{\frakp}_{p}}\in\mathbb{T}
. Thus,
\Delta_{q}^{\pm 1}
is also the difference operator realisation of the quantum torus generators
X_{\widetilde{\frakp}_{p}}
.

4.4.1
Example: 4-punctured sphere

Figure 53
:
The path
\beta
on the 4-punctured sphere is split into two paths
\beta_{L}
and
\beta_{R}
, each of which is decomposed into three pieces
\iota
,
d
and
f
.

As an example we compare the
q
-parellel transport along the 1-cycle
\beta
on the 4-punctured sphere, as in
fig.

53
, with the Moore-Seiberg result in
eq.

3.109
. Let us denote the restriction of
\beta
to the right (left) pants by
\beta_{R}
(
\beta_{L}
). The
q
-parallel transport along both components is very similar to
eq.

4.35
.
44
44

44

When compared to
eq.

4.35
, the paths
\iota
and
f
cross the branch-cut here. However, this does not contribute any additional
q
-factors.
Indeed, as before, we decompose
\beta_{R}
(and
\beta_{L}
) into three pieces
\iota,d
and
f
where
\iota
labels the first part of the path that hasn’t crossed the double wall yet,
d
labels the middle part in between the walls, and
f
labels the final part after the crossings.

The
q
-nonabelianisation along the path
d
is built only from the direct lifts
Y_{d^{(1)}}
and
Y_{d^{(2)}}
. Note that we may write

Y_{d^{(1)}}=X_{\gamma_{2}^{(1)}}Y_{m}^{11}\rightsquigarrow qM_{2}Y_{m}^{11}\quad\text{ and }\quad Y_{d^{(2)}}=X_{\gamma_{2}^{(2)}}Y_{m}^{22}\rightsquigarrow qM_{2}^{-1}Y_{m}^{22},

(4.97)

where
Y_{m}
is the same generator that appears in
eq.

4.35
. Here, we have evaluated the masses as discussed in
remark

4.5
. In addition,
Y_{d^{(1)}}
is accompanied by a winding factor of
q
as illustrated in
fig.

54
. Ultimately, we obtain the
q
-parallel transport along
d
to be

P^{q}_{d}=q^{2}\begin{pmatrix}M_{2}\,Y_{m}^{11}&0\\
0&q^{-1}M_{2}^{-1}\,Y_{m}^{22}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.98)

This form for the
q
-parallel transport matrix along
d
is rather convenient as it allows us to reuse the conclusions from
eq.

4.35
. It follows that the
q
-parallel transport along
\beta_{R}
(and
\beta_{L}
) is of the form

P^{q}_{\beta_{R}}=q^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\big(\ldots\big)Y^{11}_{\beta_{R}}&q^{-1}\big(\ldots\big)Y^{12}_{\beta_{R}}\\
\big(\ldots\big)Y^{21}_{\beta_{R}}&q^{-1}\big(\ldots\big)Y^{22}_{\beta_{R}}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.99)

Since both
\beta_{L}
and
\beta_{R}
are open paths terminating on marked points, we may evaluate the corresponding open path generators as explained in §
4.3
. Upon evaluation, we can compare this with the MS parallel transport in
eq.

3.109
. Note that it is sufficient to count only the
q
-factors because both quantum parallel transports reduce to identical semi-classical parallel transports (after appropriately rescaling the MS parallel transport basis).

Although the space of open skeins is a
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]-
module, let us temporarily write the above
q
-parallel transport as

P^{q}_{\beta_{R}}=q^{\frac{3}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\big(\ldots\big)\,q^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{11}_{\beta_{R}}&\big(\ldots\big)\,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y^{12}_{\beta_{R}}\\
\big(\ldots\big)\,q^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{21}_{\beta_{R}}&\big(\ldots\big)\,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y^{22}_{\beta_{R}}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.100)

We have already established that the MS parallel matrix inside a 3-sphere has no extra
q
-factors except for an overall factor of
q
in the front (see §
3.2
), i.e.

P^{\textrm{MS}}_{\beta_{R}}=q\begin{pmatrix}\big(\ldots\big)&\big(\ldots\big)\\
\big(\ldots\big)&\big(\ldots\big)\end{pmatrix}.

(4.101)

Comparing this expression with (the evaluation of)
eq.

4.100
, we note that the MS parallel transport and
q
-parallel transport agree in form, up to the overall winding factor, if we redefine the open path generators by half-integral powers of
q
as

\displaystyle\widehat{Y}^{11}_{\beta_{R}}:=q^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{11}_{\beta_{R}},\qquad\widehat{Y}^{22}_{\beta_{R}}:=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y^{22}_{\beta_{R}},

(4.102)

\displaystyle\widehat{Y}^{12}_{\beta_{R}}:=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y^{12}_{\beta_{R}},\qquad\widehat{Y}^{21}_{\beta_{R}}:=q^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{21}_{\beta_{R}}.

We emphasise that this is not a universal redefinition that brings all
q
-parallel transports on any
C
in a similar form to the MS parallel transport, but rather it is a redefinition that (at least) works in this specific example.

Remark 4.10
.

It is not surprising that the winding factors of
q
-parallel transport and MS parallel transport do not coincide. Whereas
q
-nonabelianisation picks up a (sheet-dependent) winding factor from every turn with respect to the foliation, the Moore-Seiberg parallel transport only picks up a (uniform overall) winding factor around punctures. However, we do ultimately expect the two results to completely agree for closed loops (corresponding to Verlinde loop operators).

Nonetheless, it is fairly straight-forward to translate between the two quantum parallel transports. For instance, given a (fully evaluated)
q
-parallel transport along any non-self-intersecting curve inside the 3-punctured sphere, we can recover the corresponding MS parallel transport by “forgetting” the additional winding factors and including an overall factor of
q^{\pm 1}
for every turn around a puncture. In the other direction, we forget the overall
q
-factor in the MS parallel transport, and include sheet-dependent winding factors (as in
fig.

32
) from all windings with respect to the foliation.

Figure 54
:
The winding of the diagonal path
d
with respect to the FN foliation.

Yet, ignoring winding factors, we expect
q
-parallel transports and MS parallel transports to be in full agreement. Especially, the composite expressions for
P^{q}_{\beta}
and
P^{\textrm{MS}}_{\beta}
must agree with each other. As discussed in §
4.3
, we may compose the two
q
-parallel transport matrices along
\beta_{R}
and
\beta_{L}
by inserting the matrix (
4.95
) of difference operators whenever the path crosses from one pair of pants to another. Assuming that the path starts on the left pair of pants, we find that the
q
-parallel transport along
\beta=\beta_{R}\circ\beta_{L}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P^{q}_{\beta}&=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{q}^{-1}&0\\
0&\Delta_{q}\end{pmatrix}\bigg(P^{q}_{\beta_{R}}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{q}^{-1}&0\\
0&\Delta_{q}\end{pmatrix}\left(P^{q}_{\beta_{L}}\right)^{\textrm{op}}\bigg)^{\textrm{op}}\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{q}^{-1}&0\\
0&\Delta_{q}\end{pmatrix}\left(P^{q}_{\beta_{R}}\right)^{\textrm{op}}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{q}&0\\
0&\Delta_{q}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}P^{q}_{\beta_{L}},\end{aligned}

(4.103)

where
\Delta_{q}^{\pm 1}(A)=q^{\pm 1}A
is a difference operator that acts on the exponentiated internal momentum
A
, and the superscript ”
op
” denotes the
q
-parallel transport with
A\mapsto A^{-1}
. This indeed agrees with the form

\displaystyle P^{\textrm{MS}}_{\beta}=\widehat{\mathbf{T}}^{-1}_{\alpha}\,P^{\textrm{MS}}_{\beta_{R},Q-\alpha}\,\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{\alpha}\,P^{\textrm{MS}}_{\beta_{L},\alpha}\,

(4.104)

of
eq.

3.116
. This establishes consistency between the two quantum parallel transports (up to the winding factors).

5
Liouville blocks from spectral networks

The ultimate ambition in this section is to construct Liouville conformal blocks from just the data of a spectral network. The initial perspective that we take to achieve this is that of the free-field representation, in which Liouville theory is described in terms of a free boson field supplemented by non-local interaction terms, called screening charges, which are specified by a set of contours on the surface
C
.

In this section, we relate the choice of contour system to the choice of a spectral network. In particular, we show that contour systems for
standard
Liouville conformal blocks are associated with networks of Fenchel-Nielsen type, while we propose that contour systems for Fock-Goncharov type networks are associated with more general ”Goncharov-Shen” conformal blocks.

We start in §
5.1
with introducing the free-field formalism, while emphasising the role of the screening charges. In §
5.2
we phrase free-field conformal blocks, with one type of screening charges, as Penner-type matrix model integrals. We interpret the matrix model as a generalised Landau-Ginzburg model and identify the spectral cover
\Sigma
using the matrix model potential
W^{\text{mat}}
. The spectral cover is singular at finite rank
N
of the matrix model, and smoothens out in the large
N
limit.

Penner-type matrix models have long been known to be related to Liouville conformal blocks. In particular, interest in the free-field description of Liouville theory
[
31
,
32
]
and its relation to
\beta
-deformed matrix models and the AGT conjecture
[
5
]
, was revived with the appearance of the Dijkgraaf-Vafa paper
[
26
]
. Many aspects of the correspondence were fleshed out in subsequent years, culminating in proofs of certain cases
[
86
,
3
,
2
]
.
Even so, with the exception of the follow-up paper
[
20
]
with Cheng, the role of the contour system seems largely unexplored. In §
5.4.3
we argue that the correspondence between Penner-type matrix models and Liouville conformal blocks only works if the contours are chosen with respect to a degenerate network of Fenchel-Nielsen type.

However, in the course of this analysis we stumble upon some (known) complications with the Penner-type matrix models. In particular, resurgence properties of the large
N
matrix model partition function cannot be reproduced from the single matrix model. As a remedy, Marino, Schiappa and Schwick propose in
[
81
]
to consider 2-matrix models, which from the free-field perspective correspond to the inclusion of both types of Liouville screening charges. While this seems promising, we argue that the difficulties arise because the spectral curve of the Penner-type matrix model is singular. Indeed, the contours of these matrix models are defined in terms of trajectories of a degenerate spectral network, whose topology changes are restricted.

Instead, we make the case that the free-field description should be altered to find a complete description of conformal blocks, in a way that is compatible with the
c=1
construction of
[
59
]
. That is, we propose to consider the free-field formalism with respect to the smooth spectral curve and to insert the screening charges with respect to a non-degenerate spectral network. In §
5.4
we describe the resulting CFT abelianisation map, which compared to
[
59
]
is enriched with the appearance of the Maulik-Okounkov R-matrix
[
82
]
.

5.1
Basics of the traditional free-field formalism

We start this section with a brief intro to the original free-field representation (as introduced in
[
31
,
32
]
and reviewed in for instance
[
24
, chapter 8]
and
[
105
, section 3]
).
45
45

45

See also
[
68
, Introduction]
for a historic overview.
For simplicity, we start with assuming that
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a genus zero Riemann surface. We extend the discussion later in this section to general Riemann surfaces through the gluing procedure.

5.1.1
Free field algebra

A (Euclidean) free boson theory on
C
is defined by the action

S=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{C}d^{2}z\,\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left(\widehat{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi+Q\widehat{R}\varphi\right).

(5.1)

As before,
\hat{g}
is a constant-curvature background metric on
C
and
Q=b+1/b
is the background charge. To discuss (holomorphic) conformal blocks, we restrict ourselves to the chiral sector of this theory.
In the absence of any interaction terms, the free boson theory admits a straightforward quantisation. In the quantum theory, the bosonic field
\varphi
has a chiral mode expansion

\varphi(z)=\rmq-i\rma_{0}\log z+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\times}}\frac{i}{n}\rma_{n}z^{-n},

(5.2)

in terms of operators
\{\rmq,\rma_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
that obey the (extended) Heisenberg commutation relations

[\rma_{n},\rma_{m}]=\frac{n}{2}\delta_{m+n},\quad[\rma_{m},\rmq]=-\frac{i}{2}\delta_{m,0}.

(5.3)

We define the vacuum
|0\rangle
as the state annihilated by all non-negative modes
\rma_{n}
, with
n\geq 0
, whereas the Fock space

\displaystyle F_{0}\cong\mathbb{C}[\rma_{-1},\rma_{-2},\ldots]

(5.4)

is the vector space generated by acting with any negative modes
\rma_{-n}
, with
n>0
, on the vacuum. The Heisenberg current is defined as the field

\displaystyle J(z):=\,\partial_{z}\varphi(z)=-i\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\rma_{n}z^{-n-1},

(5.5)

and the commutation relations for the bosonic modes are equivalent to its OPE

J(z)J(w)\sim\frac{-1}{2(z-w)^{2}}.

(5.6)

Altogether, this algebra forms the
Heisenberg vertex algebra
.

The (holomorphic) stress-energy tensor
T(z)
of the free boson theory, with background charge
Q
, can be expressed in terms of the Heisenberg current as

T(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\rmL_{n}z^{-n-2}=-:J(z)^{2}:+\,Q\,\partial_{z}J(z).

(5.7)

Its modes
L_{n}
can thus be expressed in terms of the bosonic modes
\hat{a}_{n}
through the Sugawara relations

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \rmL_{n}&=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}:\!\rma_{n-r}\rma_{r}\!:+iQ(n+1)\rma_{n}~\text{for}~n\neq 0,\\
\rmL_{0}&=2\sum_{r>0}\rma_{-r}\rma_{r}+\rma_{0}(\rma_{0}+iQ),\end{aligned}

(5.8)

This implies that the Virasoro algebra can be embedded into the Heisenberg algebra as a subalgebra of central charge
c=1+6Q^{2}
. (The same is true for the anti-holomorphic sector.)

The primary vertex operators of the free boson theory are the (normally-ordered) exponentials
46
46

46

For convenience, we write
V_{\alpha}(z)
for both a Liouville primary operator and its free-field representation. However, we shall later see in §
5.4
that the free-field representation
V_{\alpha}(z)
actually lives on a (degenerate) cover of
C
.

V_{\alpha}(z)=:\!e^{2\alpha\varphi}\!:(z).

(5.9)

The OPE relations above show that

\displaystyle T(z)\,V_{\alpha}(w)\sim\frac{\alpha(Q-\alpha)}{(z-w)^{2}}\,V_{\alpha}(w)+\frac{1}{z-w}\,\partial V_{\alpha}(w),

(5.10)

so that
V_{\alpha}(z)
has conformal dimension

\displaystyle\Delta(\alpha)=\alpha(Q-\alpha).

(5.11)

Acting with the primary field
V_{\alpha}(z)
on the vacuum
|0\rangle
generates the Fock space
F_{\alpha}
with highest weight state

\ket{\alpha}:=\lim_{z\to 0}V_{\alpha}(z)\ket{0},

(5.12)

with

\rma_{0}\ket{\alpha}=-i\alpha\ket{\alpha},\quad\rma_{n}\ket{\alpha}=0\quad\forall n>0,

(5.13)

while the remainder of the module is generated by the action of the negative modes on
\ket{\alpha}
. The embedding (
5.8
) turns the Fock space
F_{\alpha}
into a Virasoro module, and one can furthermore show that

F_{\alpha}\cong\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}

(5.14)

by comparing the characters of both spaces. Hence, we shall refer to
F_{\alpha}
as the free-field realisation of
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
.

Note that in passing to a free-field realisation, we lose some structure that accompanies the Virasoro algebra. For instance, while the fusion product between two Virasoro modules is of the form

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{2}}=\bigoplus_{\alpha}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha},

(5.15)

the fusion product between the corresponding Fock spaces

F_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes F_{\alpha_{2}}=F_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}

(5.16)

is particularly simple (see also §
4.3.2
). Nonetheless, we shall see that this will allow us to write down explicit correlation functions in Liouville theory.

The normally ordered product of two primary vertex operators is given by

\displaystyle V_{\alpha_{1}}(z)V_{\alpha_{2}}(w)=(z-w)^{-2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\,:\!V_{\alpha_{1}}(z)V_{\alpha_{2}}(w)\!:.

(5.17)

The above equation is not single-valued for generic momenta
\alpha
and
\beta
, but is instead valued on a logarithmic covering with branch-points at the insertion points
z
and
w
. We may trivialise the covering by choosing branch-cuts, and will define the principal branch as the branch where
(z-w)^{2\alpha\beta}
is positive when
z-w>0
. Physically, the branch-cuts define (logarithmic) defect lines that end on the operators
V_{\alpha}(z)
and
V_{\beta}(w)
.

Henceforth, we shall implicitly assume normal-ordering everywhere, and avoid writing the product explicitly. We will also refer to the label
\alpha
as a momentum, anticipating the connection to Liouville theory.

Remark 5.1
.

Since the Liouville momentum
\alpha
has the form
\alpha=\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathit{p}
, with
Q>0
and
\mathit{p}\in\mathbb{R}
, it is convenient to decompose the zero-mode
\rma_{0}=-\frac{iQ}{2}+\hat{p}
.

5.1.2
Free-field correlators

The background charge
Q
changes the canonical inner product on the Fock spaces into

\braket{\alpha_{1}|\alpha_{2}}=\delta_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=Q}.

(5.18)

This implies that the dual of
F_{\alpha}
is

F_{\alpha}^{*}=F_{Q-\alpha},

(5.19)

which matches the reflection symmetry of Liouville theory.

Since the primary field
V_{\alpha}(z)
acts as an operator (following
eq.

5.16
)

V_{\alpha}(z):~F_{\beta}\to F_{\beta+\alpha}

(5.20)

that shifts Fock space labels, (holomorphic) correlation functions may be defined as vacuum expectation values

\langle{V_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1})\ldots V_{\alpha_{n}}(z_{n})}\rangle:=\bra{0}V_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1})\ldots V_{\alpha_{n}}(z_{n})\ket{0}

(5.21)

of the composition of the corresponding vertex operators. Note that such corrrelators are defined with respect to an
n
-punctured genus zero surface and are only non-vanishing when

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\alpha_{k}=Q.

(5.22)

The correlators (
5.21
) can be easily computed using the OPE (
5.17
), producing the (multi-valued) expression

\langle{V_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1})\ldots V_{\alpha_{n}}(z_{n})}\rangle=\delta_{\sum\alpha_{k}=Q}\,\prod_{k<l}\,(z_{k}-z_{l})^{-2\alpha_{k}\alpha_{l}}.

(5.23)

It is tedious but straightforward to show that these correlators satisfy the (holomorphic) conformal Ward identities. Note, in particular, that the commutation relation between a single Virasoro generator
L_{k}
on a primary field
V_{\alpha}(z)
(interpreted as in
eq.

5.20
),

[L_{k},V_{\alpha}(z)]=z^{k+1}\,\partial_{z}V_{\alpha}(z)+(k+1)\,z^{k}\,\Delta(\alpha)\,V_{\alpha}(z),

(5.24)

is realised in terms of differential operators. As an immediate consequence, the correlators of primary fields are sufficient to determine all other correlators involving descendants.

The
\delta-
constraint on the right hand side of
eq.

5.23
is called the momentum conservation constraint. We can try to relax this constraint by inserting operators that are conformally invariant (i.e. have conformal dimension
\Delta=0
) but carry a non-trivial momentum. Unfortunately, there are no such local operators. Yet, we observe that the primary field

\displaystyle S_{+}(z):=V_{b}(z),

(5.25)

with conformal dimension
\Delta(b)=1
, transforms under the action

[L_{k},S_{+}(z)]=z^{k}\left(z\partial_{z}+(k+1)\right)S_{+}(z)=\partial\!\left(z^{k+1}S_{+}(z)\right),

(5.26)

as a total derivative. Therefore, the non-local operator

\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\gamma}:=\oint_{\gamma}S_{+},

(5.27)

supported on a contour
\gamma
on
C
, fulfills our requirements. The operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}^{+}
is called a
screening charge
in the CFT literature. In the vertex algebra language, it is also known as an intertwiner. Note that adding
N
screening charges
\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\gamma}
loosens the momentum constraint
\sum\alpha_{k}=Q
to

\sum_{k=1}^{n}\alpha_{k}+Nb=Q,\quad N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

(5.28)

Remark 5.2
.

In parts of the free-field literature, the contours are taken to be intervals (usually along the real axis) between the punctures of
C
. For instance, in the original papers
[
32
,
31
]
, as well as in
[
59
, around eq. (3.27)]
, it is claimed to be sufficient that the resulting integrals are convergent. In other parts of the literature it is argued that it is necessary to work with closed contour systems, generated by Pochhammer-like contours (see for instance
[
38
,
103
]
), in order to preserve the full conformal symmetry. We will return to this conundrum in
remark

5.7
.

In §
5.2
we will see that, from a matrix model perspective, it is natural to consider contour systems defined with respect to arbitrary (degenerate) spectral networks. For FG-type networks this implies the need to allow open contours, whereas for FN-type networks it suffices to work with closed contours (we make this precise in
remark

5.7
as well).

Remark 5.3
.

Similarly, it is possible to introduce screening charges

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\gamma}=\oint_{\gamma}S_{-}(z)

(5.29)

with momentum
1/b
. Since the OPE (
5.17
) of the primary fields
V_{b}(z)
and
V_{1/b}
is single-valued, both types of screening charges can be inserted simultaneously in full free-field correlators without spoiling conformal invariance. In that case, the overall momentum conservation condition is relaxed to

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\alpha_{k}+Nb+\frac{N^{\prime}}{b}=Q,\quad N,N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

(5.30)

reflecting the intrinsic
b\leftrightarrow 1/b
symmetry in Liouville theory.

We conclude that the augmented free-field correlator

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\text{ff}}_{\Gamma}(z_{n},\ldots,z_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}d^{N}y\,\langle V_{\alpha_{1}}(z_{1})\ldots V_{\alpha_{n}}(z_{n})\,S_{+}(y_{1})\ldots S_{+}(y_{N})\rangle,

(5.31)

which is defined with respect to a closed contour system
\Gamma
on the surface
C
, still preserves the Virasoro algebra and generalises the momentum constraint to
eq.

5.28
. It can furthermore be simply evaluated to

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\text{ff}}_{\Gamma}(z_{n},\ldots,z_{1})

\displaystyle=\!\prod_{1\leq k<l\leq n}(z_{k}-z_{l})^{-2\alpha_{k}\alpha_{l}}\int_{\Gamma}d^{N}y\!\prod_{1\leq s<t\leq N}(y_{s}-y_{t})^{-2b^{2}}\prod_{s,l}\,(y_{s}-z_{l})^{-2b\alpha_{l}},

(5.32)

where all factors
(x-y)^{-2b^{2}}
are defined on the principal branch of the logarithm.

Free-field correlators
\mathcal{F}^{\text{ff}}
are thought to be closely related to the Virasoro conformal blocks
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}
through a formal analytic continuation from discrete to arbitrary complex values of both external as well as internal momenta
[
86
,
3
,
2
]
. This relationship may be best summarised by comparing the decomposition of the full Liouville correlation function either in terms of free-field or Virasoro conformal blocks. After the analytic continuation, it is expected that

\displaystyle\sum_{\text{allowed}~\vec{\alpha}}\,\mathcal{F}^{\text{ff},\Gamma}_{\vec{\alpha}}(z)\,\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\vec{\alpha}}^{\text{ff},\Gamma}(\bar{z})\mapsto\int d\nu(\vec{\alpha})\,\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}_{\vec{\alpha}}(z)\,\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\text{Vir}}_{\vec{\alpha}}(\bar{z}),

(5.33)

where the contour system
\Gamma
is chosen to reflect the fusion channel parametrised by the internal momenta
\vec{\alpha}
, and the measure
d\nu(\vec{\alpha})
is given by the product of Liouville 3-point functions, as in
eq.

3.16
. In particular, the free-field correlator
\mathcal{F}^{\text{ff},\Gamma}_{\vec{\alpha}}
should be compared with the Liouville conformal block
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Liou}}_{\vec{\alpha}}
defined in
eq.

3.22
.

We will detail examples of this relation in §
5.3
. (In particular, we will argue in that subsection that (degenerate) Fenchel-Nielsen contours lead to Liouville blocks with the desired normalisation.)

Gluing free-field correlation functions

Conformal blocks on Riemann surfaces of higher genus can be obtained using the gluing construction introduced in §
3.1
provided there are no contours terminating on the puncture that is being closed. (In particular, if free-field conformal blocks were defined through (degenerate) FN contours, they can always be glued together.) The one ingredient that changes from the previous discussion is the momentum conservation constraint (
5.23
). The Liouville momenta of vertex operator insertions on a genus
g
surface must now obey the constraint

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}=Q(1-g).

(5.34)

This may be directly derived from the free-field path integral, but can also be inferred as follows. Suppose that we construct an
n
-point conformal block on a genus
g
surface by taking a genus zero surface with
2g+n
vertex operator insertions, and sewing
g
pairs of insertions using the plumbing construction from §
3.1
. Note that this requires each pair of vertex operators that is being sewn together to carry conjugate momenta
\alpha
and
Q-\alpha
. In other words, each pair that is being sewn together contributes a total charge
Q
to the momentum conservation relation. The genus zero momentum conservation constraint then reads

\underbrace{\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}}_{\text{not glued}}+gQ=Q\implies\underbrace{\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}}_{\text{not glued}}=Q(1-g),

(5.35)

and thus directly implies the genus
g
-momentum conservation constraint.

5.2
Penner-type matrix models and spectral networks

As we mentioned above, a conventional choice of contours in the free-field literature is a combination of Pochhammer contours. Instead, in this section we argue that it is more natural to consider contour systems defined by (degenerate) spectral networks. We motivate this through the correspondence between free-field correlators and partition functions of
\beta
-deformed matrix models (see for instance the discussion in
[
26
,
100
]
with references to
[
71
,
74
]
).

5.2.1
Penner-type matrix models

The partition function of a holomorphic matrix model (featuring for instance in
[
25
]
) is defined as an integral of the form

\displaystyle Z_{\Gamma}^{\textrm{mat}}(\hbar,N)=\int_{\Gamma}\,dM\,\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}\,\text{Tr}\,W^{\text{mat}}(M)\right),

(5.36)

over the space of
N\times N
-matrices
M
. The holomorphic function
W^{\text{mat}}(M)
is known as the matrix model potential, while the parameter
\hbar
is the (complex) Planck constant.
47
47

47

We emphasise that even though
\hbar
-dependence has not always been explicit (except in §
4.1.3
), it has always been implicitly around (see for instance footnote
19
). So far we have set it to
1
to simplify notation.
The integration cycle
\Gamma
is a middle-dimensional integration cycle chosen such that the integral converges.

Z_{\Gamma}^{\textrm{mat}}
can be written as an integral over eigenvalues
y_{s}
by diagonalising the matrix
M
(which we assume to be generic here) as

\displaystyle M=U\Lambda U^{\dagger},\quad\textrm{with}~\Lambda=\text{diag}(y_{1},\dots,y_{N}).

(5.37)

The integration measure transforms as

\displaystyle dM=\prod_{s=1}^{N}\,dy_{s}\,\prod_{s<t}\,(y_{s}-y_{t})^{2}\,dU,

(5.38)

where the Vandermonde determinant
\prod(y_{s}-y_{t})^{2}
arises from the Jacobian of the change of variables. The resulting partition function

\displaystyle Z_{\Gamma}^{\textrm{mat}}(\hbar,N)=\int_{\Gamma}\,\prod_{s=1}^{N}\,dy_{s}\,\prod_{s<t}\,(y_{s}-y_{t})^{2}\,\prod_{s=1}^{N}\,\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}\,W^{\text{mat}}(y_{s})\right)

(5.39)

describes a system of particles on the complex plane with logarithmic repulsion and external potential
W^{\text{mat}}(y)
.

The
\beta
-deformed version of this matrix model is defined by the integral
48
48

48

This model should be called a ”generalised” matrix model, as it cannot be formulated reasonably as an integral over matrices.

\displaystyle Z^{\beta-\textrm{mat}}_{\Gamma}(\hbar,N)=\int_{\Gamma}\,\prod_{s=1}^{N}\,dy_{s}\prod_{1\leq s,t\leq N}(y_{s}-y_{t})^{2\beta}\,\prod_{s=1}^{N}\,\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}\,W^{\text{mat}}(y_{s})\right),

(5.40)

where

\displaystyle\beta=\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{\epsilon_{2}}=-b^{2}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\hbar^{2}=-\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}.

(5.41)

Note that the sign of
\beta
makes a difference: for Re
(\beta)>0
different eigenvalues repel each other, whereas for Re
(\beta)<0
they instead attract each other.

The integral (
5.40
) is of a familiar form. Indeed, suppose that

\displaystyle W^{\text{mat}}(y)=-2\hbar b\,\sum_{k=1}^{n}\,\alpha_{k}\,\log(y-z_{k}),

(5.42)

which is known as a multi-Penner-type potential. Then we find that
Z^{\beta-\textrm{mat}}_{\Gamma}
is proportional to the free-field correlator
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{ff}}_{\Gamma}
with
N
screening charges
Q_{+}
, computed in equation (
5.32
). That is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{ff}}_{\Gamma}=\prod_{1\leq k<l\leq n}(z_{k}-z_{l})^{-2\alpha_{k}\alpha_{l}}\,Z^{\beta-\textrm{mat}}_{\Gamma}.

(5.43)

More intrinsically, the Liouville field
\varphi(y)
is related to the matrix
M
as

\displaystyle\partial\varphi(y)=\frac{1}{\hbar}\,\textrm{Tr}\left(\frac{1}{y-M}\right).

(5.44)

In the saddle-point approximation, the integral
Z^{\beta-\textrm{mat}}
receives its dominant contributions at the critical points of the potential
W^{\text{mat}}
, for which

\displaystyle\frac{dW^{\text{mat}}(y)}{dy}=0.

(5.45)

The saddle points of
