Global free-field representation on smooth coverings

Observe that exchanging the bosons
\varphi_{1}\leftrightarrow\varphi_{2}
in the defining relation
eq.

5.155
,

(Q\partial-\partial\widetilde{\varphi}_{2})(Q\partial-\partial\widetilde{\varphi}_{1})=\sum_{k=0}^{2}W^{\prime}_{(k)}(Q\partial)^{N-k}.

(5.175)

produces a second, albeit isomorphic, free-field realisation of the Virasoro algebra
\mathbf{Vir}
. The only difference between the two representations is that the present spin
2
current,

W^{\prime}_{(2)}(z)=\partial\varphi_{2}(z)\partial\varphi_{1}(z)-Q\partial^{2}\varphi_{1}(z)=-\partial\varphi(z)\partial\varphi(z)-Q\partial^{2}\varphi(z),

(5.176)

is defined with respect to a background charge
-Q
, when expressed in terms of the Liouville boson
\varphi
. We will denote the free field representation generated by
W_{(k)}
by
\mathbf{Vir}^{(1)}
, the one generated by
W^{\prime}_{(k)}
by
\mathbf{Vir}^{(2)}
, and the corresponding stress-energy tensors by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} T^{(1)}_{\text{Vir}}(z)&=-:\partial\varphi(z)\partial\varphi(z):+\,Q\,\partial^{2}_{z}\varphi(z)\quad\text{ and }\\
T^{(2)}_{\text{Vir}}(z)&=-:\partial\varphi(z)\partial\varphi(z):-\,Q\,\partial^{2}_{z}\varphi(z).\end{aligned}

(5.177)

Suppose now that
\Sigma\to C
is a smooth covering.
From the perspective of the free-field realisations
\mathbf{Vir}^{(i)}
, observe that exchanging the bosons
\varphi_{1}\leftrightarrow\varphi_{2}
(equivalently,
\varphi\mapsto-\varphi
) is indistinguishable from sending the background charge
Q\mapsto-Q
. The abelianised stress-energy tensor (
5.171
)

T_{\text{Vir}}^{\text{ab}}(z)=\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(1)})\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)})-Q\,\partial^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)})

is therefore well-defined across lifts of branch-cuts on
\Sigma
if we could simultaneously swap the sign of the background charge!

Figure 60
:
The Maulik-Okounkov R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
intertwines the free-field realisation
\mathbf{Vir}^{(1)}
(with background charge
+Q
) on sheet 1 with the free-field realisation
\mathbf{Vir}^{(2)}
(with background charge
-Q
) on sheet 2 of the double covering
\Sigma\to C
.

Fortunately, it is well-known that there exists a free-field operator which does just that. It is called the
Maulik-Okounkov (MO) R-matrix

[
82
]
58
58

58

The idea that the Maulik-Okounkov R-matrix might be required in the
c\neq 1
generalisation of CFT-abelianisation was suggested to us by Andy Neitzke.
and defined through the relations
[
78
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1,2}\,(Q\partial-\partial{\varphi}_{1})(Q\partial-\partial{\varphi}_{2})=(Q\partial-\partial{\varphi}_{2})(Q\partial-\partial{\varphi}_{1})\mathcal{R}_{1,2}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{R}^{2}_{1,2}=\mathrm{id}.

(5.178)

These equations imply that the MO R-matrix intertwines the free-field representations
\mathbf{Vir}^{(i)}
as in

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1,2}\,T^{(1)}_{\text{Vir}}(z)=T^{(2)}_{\text{Vir}}(z)\,\mathcal{R}_{1,2}.

(5.179)

Consider now a trivialisation
\tau
of the covering
\Sigma\to C
. Recall that such a trivialisation is defined by a choice of branch-cuts on
C
and a labelling of the sheets over each connected region in
C-\{\text{branch-cuts}\}
.
Then, we propose to insert line defects supporting the R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
along the lifts of the branch-cuts, such that the background charge on either side of the line defect differs by a sign. We shall refer to these line defects as
branch-cut defects

\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
. We then define the
global free-field realisation
of the Virasoro algebra
\mathbf{Vir}
to be the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant bosonic field theory on the smooth covering
\Sigma
generated by the stress-energy tensor

T_{\text{Vir}}^{\text{ab}}(z)=\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(1)})\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)})-Q\,\partial^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)}).

(5.180)

We stress that this realisation does not depend on the details of the trivialisation
\tau
.
59
59

59

More precisely, there is only a global
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-choice related to changing all the labels
1\leftrightarrow 2
, which is equivalent to selecting a background charge
Q
vs.
-Q
.

As a curiosity, using
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance, we can equivalently represent the non-local stress-energy tensor
T_{\text{Vir}}^{\text{ab}}(z)
as

T_{\text{Vir}}^{\text{ab}}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\widetilde{T}_{\text{Vir}}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})+\widetilde{T}_{\text{Vir}}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})\right)=\widetilde{T}_{\text{Vir}}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})=\widetilde{T}_{\text{Vir}}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})

(5.181)

on sheets
1
and
2
respectively, where

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{\textrm{Vir}}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})

\displaystyle=-:\!\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(1)})\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(1)})\!:+\;Q\,\partial^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(1)})\quad\text{ and }

(5.182)

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{\textrm{Vir}}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})

\displaystyle=-:\!\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)})\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)})\!:-\;Q\,\partial^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)}),

That is, we may locally assign the free-field representation
\mathbf{Vir}^{(i)}
to sheet
i
of
\Sigma
. The branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
then serve as an interface between the two free-field representations. This is illustrated in
fig.

60
.

Generic vertex operators

In the above picture, in the presence of the branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
, we propose that the vertex operators are abelianised as

\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{\text{ab}}(z)=\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\otimes\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(2)}(z^{(2)}),

(5.183)

with

\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})=e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})=e^{(\alpha-Q)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}).

(5.184)

This is illustrated in
fig.

61
.

More precisely, for generic Liouville vertex operators
V_{\Delta_{\alpha}}
, with
\Delta_{\alpha}=\alpha(Q-\alpha)
, there are two choices for the abelianisation of
V_{\Delta_{\alpha}}
. Following
eq.

5.183
, the two choices are

\displaystyle V_{\Delta_{\alpha}}(z)\rightsquigarrow\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{\text{ab}}(z)=\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\otimes\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(2)}(z^{(2)}),\text{ and}

(5.185)

\displaystyle V_{\Delta_{\alpha}}(z)\rightsquigarrow\widetilde{V}_{Q-\alpha}^{\text{ab}}(z)=\widetilde{V}_{Q-\alpha}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\otimes\widetilde{V}_{Q-\alpha}^{(2)}(z^{(2)}).

We choose the first abelianisation when
|e^{-i\pi m}|=|e^{-i\pi b(2\alpha-Q)}|\leq 1
and the second abelianisation otherwise. This is consistent with the WKB choices from
section

2
.

Figure 61
:
The abelianisation of the vertex operator
V_{\alpha}(z)
on the base
C
into the tensor product of
\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})
and
\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})
on the cover
\Sigma
. The arrows in this figure will be explained in the next paragraph.

We will now show that
eq.

5.185
is invariant under the action of branch-cut defects. For simplicity in notation, we assume that we are in the first case with
|e^{-i\pi m}|=|e^{-i\pi b(2\alpha-Q)}|\leq 1
.

The matrix representation of
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
on the Fock space
F_{\alpha}
of the Liouville boson can be computed at low levels by writing the relation (
5.179
) as an infinite set of relations (see for instance
[
78
]
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1,2}\,\rmL^{(1)}_{-n_{1}}\ldots\rmL^{(1)}_{-n_{r}}|\alpha,Q\rangle=r(\alpha^{\prime})\,\rmL^{(2)}_{-n_{1}}\ldots\rmL^{(2)}_{-n_{r}}|\alpha^{\prime},-Q\rangle.

(5.186)

In particular, through eqns. (
5.8
) and (
5.13
), we find that
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
maps primary states to primary states,

\mathcal{R}_{1,2}\,\alpha(Q-\alpha)\ket{\alpha,Q}=-\,\alpha^{\prime}(Q+\alpha^{\prime})\,r(\alpha^{\prime})\ket{\alpha^{\prime},-Q},

(5.187)

up to the normalisation factor
r
. For the conformal dimension to be preserved, we require that

\displaystyle\alpha(Q-\alpha)=-\alpha^{\prime}(Q+\alpha^{\prime}).

(5.188)

The simplest way to preserve the conformal dimension is to introduce the operator

(12):\ket{\alpha,Q}\mapsto\ket{-\alpha,-Q},

(5.189)

which merely relabels what we call
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
by simultaneously negating both
\alpha
and
Q
. In other words, it acts on the tensor product

F_{\alpha}^{(1)}\otimes F_{-\alpha}^{(2)}\longrightarrow F_{-\alpha}^{(2)}\otimes F_{\alpha}^{(1)}

(5.190)

as a simple permutation operator. Thus, the operator
(12)
represents the sheet-involution map
\sigma:\Sigma\to\Sigma
on vertex operators.

Instead, by insisting that the operator
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
preserves the conformal dimension in a non-trivial manner, we find that

\mathcal{R}_{1,2}:\ket{\alpha,Q}\mapsto r(\alpha-Q)\ket{\alpha-Q,-Q}.

(5.191)

For simplicity, we set the normalisation factor
r=1
in the following. This implies that

\mathcal{R}_{1,2}:~e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\longleftrightarrow e^{(\alpha-Q)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}),

(5.192)

so that it immediately follows that the pair of insertions
(\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(1)},\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(2)})
is invariant upon crossing a branch-cut defect. We therefore refer to the tuple

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z^{(1)}),\,\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})\right)

(5.193)

as an
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-
equivariant
pair of vertex operators on
\Sigma
.

Note that it is only the combined
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant pair of insertions on
\Sigma
that is a (Virasoro) primary operator with respect to the stress-energy tensor
T_{\text{Vir}}^{\text{ab}}
. Furthermore, observe that an
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant pair satisfies

(12)\,\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})\overset{!}{=}\widetilde{V}_{Q-\alpha}^{(1)}(z^{(1)}).

(5.194)

This is equivalent to the fact that a Heisenberg-Verlinde operator evaluated on closed (clockwise) cycles around
z^{(1)}
and
z^{(2)}
must have inverse eigenvalues.

Recall from §
4
that the punctures
z^{(1)}
and
z^{(2)}
on
\Sigma
are labelled by conjugate
GL(1,\mathbb{C})
-representations. It is in order to suit this requirement that we introduced the MO R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
in the first place!

Remark 5.17
.

Combining the action of
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
and
(12)
, we find that the composite operator
\mathcal{R}^{\textrm{Liou}}:=(12)\circ\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
acts by conjugating the momentum

\mathcal{R}^{\textrm{Liou}}\,\ket{\alpha,Q}=r(Q-\alpha)\ket{Q-\alpha,Q}

(5.195)

of a Virasoro primary operator
V_{\alpha}(z)
on the base. It follows that the two-point correlation function (
5.21
)

\braket{V_{\alpha}(z)\,\mathcal{R}^{\textrm{Liou}}V_{\alpha}(w)}=r(Q-\alpha)(z-w)^{-2\Delta_{\alpha}}

(5.196)

is non-vanishing for all (generic)
\alpha
. This implies that
\mathcal{R}^{\textrm{Liou}}
defines an isomorphism

F_{\alpha}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}F_{Q-\alpha}

(5.197)

between the Fock space
F_{\alpha}
and its conjugate
F_{Q-\alpha}
as Virasoro representations. Hence,
\mathcal{R}^{\text{Liou}}
can be identified with the
Liouville reflection operator
(see also
[
82
, §14.3]
).

Remark 5.18
.

In fact, there are four operators
\mathcal{R}_{\pm\pm}
that relate isomorphic free-field Virasoro representations. Up to normalisation, these act as
60
60

60

Note that our operators
\mathcal{R}_{\pm\pm}
are permuted versions of the operators
R_{\pm\pm}
in
[
82
]
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{++}:\ket{\alpha,Q}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{id}}{\mapsto}\ket{\alpha,Q},\quad\mathcal{R}_{-+}:\ket{\alpha,Q}\mapsto\ket{Q-\alpha,Q},

(5.198)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{+-}:\ket{\alpha,Q}

\displaystyle\mapsto\ket{-\alpha,-Q},\quad\mathcal{R}_{--}:\ket{\alpha,Q}\mapsto\ket{\alpha-Q,-Q}

on the highest weight state of the Fock space
F_{\alpha}
. We may identify
\mathcal{R}_{--}=\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
with the MO R-matrix,
\mathcal{R}_{+-}=(12)
with the permutation operator, and
\mathcal{R}_{-+}=\mathcal{R}^{\textrm{Liou}}
with the Liouville reflection operator.

Remark 5.19
.

Although the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance constraint
W^{(1)}=0
is invariant under exchanging the bosons
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
, its representation on Fock spaces depends on the operator implementing the exchange (see a related discussion in
[
82
, Section 13.4.5]
). As we mentioned previously, whereas
(12)
acts as the permutation operator,

(12):\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{(}a_{n}\otimes 1)\,z^{-n-1}\longleftrightarrow\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(1\otimes\mathrm{a}_{n})\,z^{-n-1},

(5.199)

the R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
acts by permutation and conjugation (see
eq.

5.198
)

\mathcal{R}_{1,2}:\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{a}_{n}\otimes 1)\,z^{-n-1}\longmapsto\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(1\otimes\mathrm{a}_{n})\,z^{-n-1}\,\mathcal{R}^{\text{Liou}}.

(5.200)

The
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance constraint on
\widetilde{\varphi}
in the presence of
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
is therefore implemented as

\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{z})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}\cdot\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{z})=-i\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{a}_{n}\,\widetilde{z}^{-n-1}-i\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{a}_{n}\,\sigma(\widetilde{z})^{-n-1}\,\mathcal{R}^{\text{Liou}}=0.

(5.201)

This implies that we should not merely set
\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(1)})=-\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)})
, but that we should additionally conjugate the representation on which the Heisenberg modes act. Hence the pair of insertions

\ket{\alpha}\otimes\ket{\alpha-Q}

(5.202)

is indeed annihilated by the combination (
5.201
), explaining our nomenclature for an
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant pair (
5.193
).

Below, we will define branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\sigma}
supporting the permutation operator
(12)
. In that case, the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance simply reads

\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{z})+\mathbf{B}_{\sigma}\cdot\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{z})=-i\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{a}_{n}\,\widetilde{z}^{-n-1}-i\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{a}_{n}\,\sigma(\widetilde{z})^{-n-1}=0,

(5.203)

which is instead equivalent to trivially setting
\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(1)})=-\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)})
.

Remark 5.20
.

So far, we have defined the free-field formalism on the spectral cover
\Sigma
. However, from the above discussion it is also clear how to define free-field correlators on the base
C
in terms of the Liouville boson
\varphi
. We would assign a single free-field representation
\mathbf{Vir}^{(i)}
to all of
C
, and insert line defects supporting the Liouville reflection operator
\mathcal{R}^{\textrm{Liou}}
along the branch-cuts on
C
. This operator relates the abelianisations of the vertex operators
V_{\alpha}(z)
and
V_{Q-\alpha}(z)
across branch-cuts, which are equivalent from the perspective of Liouville theory. (In the semi-classical limit, the action of the reflection operator simply corresponds to a permutation of the sections
s_{i}
in the local basis
(s_{1},s_{2})
on either side of the branch-cut.)

Relating the singular and smooth abelianisations

Let us take one step back and take stock of what we have done so far in
section

5.4
. We started in §
5.4.1
with a reformulation of the traditional free-field formalism for singular coverings
\Sigma^{\text{sing}}\to C
. In particular, we found (see
eq.

5.168
) that primary vertex operators
V_{\alpha}(z)
are abelianised as

V_{\alpha}^{\text{ab}}(z)=e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{-\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}).

(5.204)

Instead, in §
5.4.2
we argued that for smooth coverings
\Sigma\to C
we need to dress the lifts of branch-cuts to
\Sigma
with the R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
, and this lead (see
eq.

5.183
) to the abelianisation

\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{\text{ab}}(z)=e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{(\alpha-Q)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}).

(5.205)

Below, we reconcile the two formulations.

Figure 62
:
Relation between the singular and the MO abelianisation from the perspective of the cover
\Sigma
. On the left we start in the global free-field realisation, where (lifted) branch-cuts are dressed with the R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
. We write
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}=(12)\,\mathcal{R}^{\text{Liou}}
and move the line defects labelled by
\mathcal{R}^{\text{Liou}}
towards each other. These line defects annihilate each other, while in the process conjugating all abelian vertex operators on sheet 2 (with respect to the background charge on this sheet). The resulting picture is consistent with the abelianised description of the traditional free-field formalism on singular coverings.

Let us decompose the MO R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1,2}=(12)\,\mathcal{R}^{\text{Liou}}.

(5.206)

That is, let us consider the branch-cut defect
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
as a product of two defects: the (traditional) branch-cut defect
\mathbf{B}_{\sigma}
dressed by the sheet-involution operator
(12)
and a new line defect
\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}
dressed by the reflection operator
\mathcal{R}^{\text{Liou}}
. Note that unlike the branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
and
\mathbf{B}_{\sigma}
that act as an interface between
\mathbf{Vir}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{Vir}^{(2)}
-regions, the line defect
\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}
is a genuine (topological) line defect in either free-field representations.
61
61

61

\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}
is a topological defect because it commutes with both the Heisenberg modes and the Virasoro modes
[
78
]
.

Now fix the branch-cut defect
\mathbf{B}_{\sigma}
in place, and move the line defect
\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}
across
\Sigma
. If the line defect crosses an abelian vertex operator insertion on sheet
i
, then it acts on this vertex operator by conjugation,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}\cdot\widetilde{V}^{(i)}_{\alpha}(z^{(i)})=\mathcal{R}^{\text{Liou}}\,\widetilde{V}^{(i)}_{\alpha}(z^{(i)})\cdot\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}=\left(\widetilde{V}^{(i)}_{\alpha}(z^{(i)})\right)^{*}\cdot\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}},

(5.207)

with respect to the background charge on that sheet (also see
eq.

3.135
and
fig.

24
).

Consider the local configuration around the lift of a branch-point to the cover
\Sigma
. (For simplicity, we choose a single branch-cut on
C
. This argument works for any number of branch-cuts.) Since the lift of the branch-point to
\Sigma
emits two branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
, we can choose to bring together the associated line defects
\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}
on (say) the second sheet as illustrated in
fig.

62
. This results in all abelian vertex operators on this sheet being conjugated, so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\text{Liou}}\,e^{(\alpha-Q)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})=\left(e^{(\alpha-Q)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})\right)^{*}=e^{-\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}),

(5.208)

where we have used that
\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}\cdot\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}=\mathrm{Id}
.

After eliminating all the line defects
\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}
as such, the remaining lifts of the branch-cuts are just decorated by the sheet-involution operator
(12)
. That is, the operation of taking apart the branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
on
\Sigma
relates the two abelianisations (
5.204
) and (
5.183
)! Note that the abelianisation
V^{\text{ab}}_{\alpha}(z)
is invariant under the action of the branch-cut defect
\mathbf{B}_{\sigma}
, since

(12):~e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\longleftrightarrow e^{-\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}).

(5.209)

This implies that the abelianisation map (
5.204
) is well-defined (after all) on smooth coverings when we dress its (lifted) branch-cuts with the operator
(12)
.

The action of the line defect
\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}
can be interpreted as inverting the radial orientation in the neighbourhood of punctures. Whereas the abelianisation map (
5.168
) from §
5.4.1
is defined with respect to a standard radial outward orientation on both sheets of the covering
\Sigma
, in the new formulation (involving the branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
) the orientation on the second sheet has changed to the radial inward orientation. This is illustrated in
fig.

63
. In the following, we will refer to the standard radial outward orientation on both sheets as the standard orientation, and to the altered radial orientation as the MO orientation.

Ultimately, we have therefore established that the abelianised field theory on the cover
\Sigma
can be defined in terms of the boson
\widetilde{\varphi}
with stress-energy tensor
T_{\text{Vir}}^{\text{ab}}
and either with primary operators
V_{\alpha}^{\text{ab}}
(in the standard orientation) or
\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{\text{ab}}
(in the MO orientation).

Figure 63
:
On the left: CFT abelianisation setup with respect to the MO orientation. On the right: CFT abelianisation setup with respect to the standard orientation.

Comparison with the
c=1
abelianisation map

In the following, we interpret the abelianisation map (
5.168
) as a deformation of the
c=1
abelianisation map proposed in
[
59
]
. Consider the free boson theory
\widetilde{\varphi}
with background charge
Q=0
on
\Sigma
. In this case, the abelianisation map (with respect to the standard radial orientation of
\Sigma
) is defined to be
[
59
]
62
62

62

Note that their
U(1)
current is defined with an additional factor of
i
when compared with our
U(1)
current
\partial\varphi(z)
.

\displaystyle T_{\text{Vir}}^{\text{ab}}(z)

\displaystyle\overset{Q=0}{=}-\frac{1}{4}:\!\big(\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(1)})-\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)})\big)^{2}\!:\quad\text{and}

(5.210)

\displaystyle V_{\alpha}^{\text{ab}}(z)

\displaystyle\overset{Q=0}{=}~e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{-\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}).

Using the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance of
\widetilde{\varphi}
, we immediately see that this is in agreement with our abelianisation map (
5.168
).

Let us introduce branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\sigma}
on
\Sigma
while still maintaining
Q=0
. By the discussion around
eq.

5.209
, the underlying physical theory and the abelianisation map (
5.210
) are unchanged under this operation. But now we can deform the
c=1
theory on
\Sigma
by introducing background charge
Q
on sheet
1
and
-Q
on sheet
2
. The branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\sigma}
ensure that the resulting configuration is consistent.

We interpret this to mean that the
c\neq 1
deformation simply amounts to creating a pair of background charges
\pm Q
at the interface of the two sheets (defined by the lifts of the branch-cuts) such that the net background charge on
\Sigma
is still zero. This will become important later in §
5.4.3
where free-field correlation functions on
\Sigma
will be computed. We have to keep in mind though that conformal properties on each sheet
are
deformed in the presence of a non-vanishing background charge on each sheet. For instance, conjugates (
5.208
) are now computed with respect to the background charge
-Q
on sheet
2
(versus
Q
on sheet
1
).

Screening charges

Next, we consider additional insertions of screening operators along lifts of the critical trajectories of the spectral network
\mathcal{W}
to the cover
\Sigma
. In §
5.3
, we argued that
12
-trajectories
w_{12}
(versus the
21
-trajectories
w_{21}
) on
C
in the
\mathbf{Vir}^{(1)}
-region support the screening operators

S_{+}(z)=e^{2b\varphi}(z)\quad\big(\text{versus }S_{-}(z)=e^{\frac{2}{b}\varphi}(z)\big).

(5.211)

Using the abelianisation map (
5.183
), we then find that the abelianisations of the screening operators in the MO orientation are

\widetilde{S}_{+}^{\text{ab}}(z)=e^{b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{-\frac{1}{b}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{S}_{-}^{\text{ab}}(z)=e^{\frac{1}{b}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{-b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}).

(5.212)

We then define the screening charges

\mathcal{Q}_{12}:=\int_{w_{12}}\widetilde{S}_{+}^{\text{ab}}(z)\,dz

(5.213)

supported on
12
-trajectories, and screening charges

\mathcal{Q}_{21}:=\int_{w_{21}}\widetilde{S}_{-}^{\text{ab}}(z)\,dz

(5.214)

supported on
21
-trajectories. Whereas on the base
C
, the screening charges (
5.213
) and (
5.214
) are ordinary line operators, they are integrals of bilocal expressions (depending on both
z^{(1)}
and
z^{(2)}
) on the cover
\Sigma
, and thus more exotic.

Suppose
w_{ij}^{(k)}
denotes the lift of the
ij
-trajectory to sheet
k
, then note that the junction between the lifts
w_{ij}^{(1)}
and
w_{ij}^{(2)}
is well-defined (and topological) because the MO R-matrix identifies

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{\pm}^{(1)}\leftrightarrow\widetilde{S}_{\pm}^{(2)}

(5.215)

across the branch-cut. The resulting local setup around a branch-point is illustrated in
fig.

64
.

Figure 64
:
CFT abelianisation of the screening charges
\mathcal{Q}_{ij}
on the base
C
in terms of the screening charges
\widetilde{S}^{(i)}_{\pm}
on the cover
\Sigma
.

Suppose we conjugate the screening charges
\widetilde{S}^{\text{ab}}_{\pm}
on the second sheet (with respect to the background charge
-Q
on that sheet) to obtain the screening charges in the standard orientation. This leads to the expressions

S_{+}^{\text{ab}}(z)=e^{b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{-b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})\quad\text{ and }\quad S_{-}^{\text{ab}}(z)=e^{\frac{1}{b}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{-\frac{1}{b}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}).

(5.216)

These are the
c\neq 1
generalisations of the screening contours defined in
[
59
]
. Indeed, the individual terms in the tensor products
S_{\pm}^{\text{ab}}
can be identified with free fermions
\psi_{\pm}
when
b=i
.

Remark 5.21
.

In the traditional definition of the free-field formalism on the base
C
, in contrast to double branch-points, simple branch-points lead to singularities due to the non-trivial monodromy of the Liouville boson
\varphi
. It was shown in
[
59
]
, for
Q=0
, that these singularities are resolved by inserting screening charges along the three trajectories emitted by the simple branch-point.
Our reinterpretation of the free-field formalism is intrinsically defined on the spectral cover
\Sigma
, but can be formulated on the base
C
using
remark

5.20
. We are optimistic that the conclusion of
[
59
]
still holds in this setting.

Notwithstanding this, inserting screening charges along the trajectories of a spectral network is crucial in the global free-field formalism to ensure that Liouville-Verlinde loop operators around branch-points on
C
are trivial. This is the quantum generalisation of the nonabelian flatness constraints for the standard
\mathcal{W}
-nonabelianisation method.

Recall from §
5.2
that the screening charges
\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}
on
C
are only allowed to end on a vertex operator
V_{\alpha}
for a constrained set of momenta
\alpha
. Here we analyse how the constraints on the momenta (listed in
table

1
) are modified in the presence of branch-cuts.

Suppose the screening charge
\mathcal{Q}_{12}
ends on an
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant pair of vertex operator insertions
(\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(1)},\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(2)})
on
\Sigma
. We then have a collision between
\widetilde{S}_{+}^{(1)}
and
\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(1)}
on sheet
1
, and
\widetilde{S}_{+}^{(2)}
and
\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{(2)}
on sheet
2
. It follows that the two OPEs

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{+}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\widetilde{V}^{(1)}_{\alpha}(w^{(1)})

\displaystyle=(z^{(1)}-w^{(1)})^{-\alpha b}:\!\widetilde{S}_{+}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\widetilde{V}^{(1)}_{\alpha}(w^{(1)})\!:\quad\text{ and }

(5.217)

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{+}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})\widetilde{V}^{(2)}_{\alpha}(w^{(2)})

\displaystyle=(z^{(2)}-w^{(2)})^{-\frac{1}{b}(Q-\alpha)}:\!\widetilde{S}_{+}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})\widetilde{V}^{(2)}_{\alpha}(w^{(2)})\!:

are both required to be regular as
z\to w
. That is, we should restrict the momentum
\alpha
such that
63
63

63

In the language of §
5.2
, we must simultaneously impose the constraint that allows
\mathcal{Q}_{+}
to end on
V_{\alpha}
and the constraint that allows
\mathcal{Q}_{-}
to end on
V_{Q-\alpha}
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\lim_{z\to w}\,(z^{(1)}-w^{(1)})^{-\alpha b}\to 0\\
&\lim_{z\to w}\,(z^{(2)}-w^{(2)})^{-\frac{1}{b}(Q-\alpha)}\to 0,\end{aligned}

(5.218)

where the limit is taken by moving
z
along the screening charge trajectory. For 12-trajectories of FN-type, that wind in the counter-clockwise direction around a puncture, we thus require that the constraints

\Im(\alpha b)<0\quad\text{ and }\quad\Im(\alpha/b)>1+\Im(b^{-2})

(5.219)

are both obeyed.

Note that the constraints (
5.219
)
cannot
be fulfilled simultaneously when
b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. This may be correlated with
remark

5.6
, where we note that for
\beta=-b^{2}<0
both types of screening charges will need to inserted along the same type of network trajectory. In contrast, when
b=i
, they reduce to the single constraint
\Re(\alpha)<0
.

Note as well that the first constraint in
eq.

5.219
is almost equivalent to the constraint
|e^{-i\pi b(2\alpha-Q)}|~\leq~1
that we imposed around
eq.

5.185
. In particular, this implies that 12-trajectories naturally end on generic vertex operators
\widetilde{V}^{\text{ab}}_{\alpha}
(with
|e^{-i\pi b(2\alpha-Q)}|~\leq~1
) when
b=i
.
64
64

64

There is a small gap in the constraints (
5.219
) for momenta
0\leq\Im(b\alpha)\leq 1+\Im(b^{-2})
. This gap means that we need to be careful in particular with degenerate vertex operators. We will discuss them in §
5.4.2
.

In contrast, counter-clockwise 21-trajectories of FN-type ending on the generic vertex operators would need to obey the constraints

\Im(\alpha/b)<0\quad\text{ and }\quad\Im(\alpha b)>1+\Im(b^{2}).

(5.220)

In this case, the second constraint is very similar to the constraint
|e^{-i\pi b(2\alpha-Q)}|~\geq~1
around
eq.

5.185
. In fact, the latter discussion tells us that we should have instead abelianised the vertex operators as
\widetilde{V}^{\text{ab}}_{Q-\alpha}
. That is, when
b=i
, 21-trajectories naturally end on generic vertex operators
\widetilde{V}^{\text{ab}}_{Q-\alpha}
(with
|e^{-i\pi b(2\alpha-Q)}|~\geq~1
).

That is, 12-trajectories naturally end on vertex operators
\widetilde{V}^{\text{ab}}_{\alpha}
, whereas 21-trajectories naturally end on vertex operators
\widetilde{V}^{\text{ab}}_{Q-\alpha}
. This agrees with a similar discussion around eq.
(3.27)
in
[
59
]
.

Remark 5.22
.

Here, we also remind the reader of
remark

5.7
, where we argue that even though screening contours are allowed to end on punctures, the resulting Ward identities are slightly modified (at next-to-leading order around the punctures). The exceptions are FN-type contours, which are equivalent to closed contours.

Degenerate vertex operators

Whereas generic Liouville vertex operators on the base
C
are abelianised into pairs of (conjugate) Heisenberg vertex operators on the cover
\Sigma
, the story for degenerate vertex operators is slightly different. We already saw a glimpse of this at the end of §
4.3
, where we found in
eq.

4.80
that (assuming the abelianisation map (
5.183
) for generic vertex operators) the abelianisation of a degenerate Liouville operator on
C
is given by a direct sum of two degenerate Heisenberg operators on either sheet of
\Sigma
. We identified these two contributions with the two independent solutions to the BPZ equation associated with the degenerate Liouville operator. Here, we will give an alternative argument from the CFT abelianisation perspective.

First, note that while for generic momenta
\alpha
the Fock spaces
F_{\alpha}
and
F_{Q-\alpha}
are isomorphic as Virasoro representations, this symmetry breaks down for degenerate momentum
\alpha_{p,q}
(defined in
eq.

3.35
). For instance, only
\alpha_{1,2}=-b/2
(and not
Q-\alpha_{1,2}
) satisfies the algebraic null-vector decoupling constraint

\big(\rmL_{-2}+\,b^{-2}\rmL_{-1}^{2}\big)\ket{\alpha_{1,2}}=0

(5.221)

for the highest weight state inside
\mathcal{V}_{1,2}
in the free-field representation
\mathbf{Vir}^{(1)}
when
Q\neq 0
(see also
eq.

3.37
).
65
65

65

This is different for
Q=0
, when both
\pm i/2
are degenerate momenta due to the coincidental identification between
-b/2
and
(-1/2b)^{*}
when
b=\pm i
.
It follows that the Liouville reflection operator
\mathcal{R}^{\text{Liou}}
, and therefore the R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
as well, is singular when acting on degenerate operators
[
78
]
. Consequently, the abelianisation map (
5.183
) in the MO orientation is ill-defined for degenerate operators. We are therefore forced to consider an alternative approach.

Recall that the fusion product (
3.38
) between a generic Virasoro representation
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
and the degenerate Virasoro representation
\mathcal{V}_{1,2}
is given by

\mathcal{V}_{1,2}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha-b/2}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha+b/2}.

(5.222)

Since
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha-b/2}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha+b/2}
are both generic representations, it
is
possible to use the abelianisation map for Virasoro representations to write the RHS of
eq.

5.222
as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigg(\widetilde{F}_{\alpha-b/2}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\alpha-b/2-Q}^{(2)}\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\widetilde{F}_{\alpha+b/2}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\alpha+b/2-Q}^{(2)}\bigg).\end{aligned}

(5.223)

We now propose that the degenerate representation
\mathcal{V}_{1,2}
in the MO orientation is abelianised as

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{1,2}\rightsquigarrow\widetilde{F}_{\text{deg}}=\widetilde{F}_{-b}^{(1)}\oplus\widetilde{F}_{b}^{(2)}.

(5.224)

As a first check,
\widetilde{F}_{\text{deg}}
defined in this way is indeed a sum of degenerate representations, with respect to the background charge on each sheet. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{\text{deg}}\otimes\bigg(\widetilde{F}_{\alpha}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\alpha-Q}^{(2)}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\widetilde{F}_{\alpha-b}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\alpha-Q}^{(2)}\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\widetilde{F}_{\alpha}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\alpha+b-Q}^{(2)}\bigg)

(5.225)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\widetilde{F}_{\alpha-b/2}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\alpha-b/2-Q}^{(2)}\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\widetilde{F}_{\alpha+b/2}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{\alpha+b/2-Q}^{(2)}\bigg),

where in the second line we have imposed
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance in the MO orientation. That is, if the net momentum contribution from two pairs of insertions in the MO orientation is
2\alpha^{\prime}-Q
(or
Q-2\alpha^{\prime}
), then the insertion on sheet
1
must contribute momentum
\alpha^{\prime}
(or
Q-\alpha^{\prime}
), whereas the insertion on sheet
2
must contribute momentum
\alpha^{\prime}-Q
(or
-\alpha^{\prime}
) in order for the pair to preserve the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance constraint (
5.201
).
66
66

66

We may justify the second equality by comparing with
remark

4.5
where we noted that an open path on any one sheet also changes the evaluation of the Heisenberg-Verlinde operator on the second sheet. Independently, we show below in
eq.

5.240
that a Heisenberg-Verlinde operator evaluated around a pair of punctures only depends on the net momentum contribution, and thus will not be able to differentiate between the first and second lines in
eq.

5.225
.
Finally, the definition (
5.224
) is consistent with
eq.

4.80
in §
4.3
.

We therefore propose that the degenerate Liouville operator
V_{1,2}(z)
is abelianised as

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}(z)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\oplus\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(2)}(z^{(2)}),

(5.226)

with the summands

\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})=e^{-b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})~\text{ and }~\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})=e^{b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})

(5.227)

being degenerate operators under the local stress-energy tensors
\widetilde{T}_{\text{Vir}}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})
and
\widetilde{T}_{\text{Vir}}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})
, respectively.

Remark 5.23
.

Recall from
eq.

5.185
that there are, in principle, two different ways,

e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{(\alpha-Q)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})\quad\text{ or }\quad e^{(Q-\alpha)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{-\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})

(5.228)

to abelianise a given vertex operator
V_{\Delta_{\alpha}}(z)
. We could write this as

V_{\Delta_{\alpha}}(z)\rightsquigarrow\bigg(e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{(\alpha-Q)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})\bigg)\oplus\bigg(e^{(Q-\alpha)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{-\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})\bigg).

(5.229)

For
\Delta_{\alpha}
generic, the WKB constraint eliminates one of the two terms in the direct sum. But for a degenerate momentum
\alpha=-b/2
, there is no such consistency condition. Thus, both summands in the abelianisation of
\mathcal{V}_{1,2}
generically contribute to the abelianisation map.

Remark 5.24
.

As we already saw in
remark

4.8
, generic vertex operators and degenerate vertex operators correspond to inequivalent types of brane insertions in the parent 6d theory. From the 6d construction, we also know that abelianised degenerate operators must correspond to individual brane insertions on the sheets of
\Sigma
(in contrast to generic vertex operators). The abelianisation map (
5.226
) for degenerate operators is thus consistent with a realisation in string theory.

When computing Heisenberg-Verlinde operators in
section

5.4.2
, or more generally, for calculating free-field quantum parallel transports on the cover, we will need to move the insertion point
\widetilde{z}
of the degenerate operator
e^{\mp b\widetilde{\varphi}}(\widetilde{z})
across
\Sigma
. We will therefore need to compare configurations with the degenerate operator being positioned on either sides of a branch-cut
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
. However, since the action of
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
on a degenerate operator is singular, there is no straight-forward way to do this.

It is helpful to recall that the degenerate operator
\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(i)}
can be identified with the boundary of a
GL(1,\mathbb{C})
-skein on sheet
i
of
\Sigma
. Since
GL(1,\mathbb{C})
-skeins are acted on trivially by branch-cuts (except for the branch-point constraint (
4.6
)), we propose that

e^{-b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\quad\text{ and }\quad e^{b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})

(5.230)

are smoothly exchanged as
z^{(1)}\leftrightarrow z^{(2)}
approach very close to each other.

In the standard orientation, we find a slightly different picture. The good news is that the abelianisation map (
5.204
)

V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}(z)=e^{-\frac{b}{2}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\otimes e^{\frac{b}{2}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})

(5.231)

for the degenerate operator
V_{1,2}
in this orientation is well-defined since the associated state
\ket{\alpha_{1,2}}\otimes\ket{-\alpha_{1,2}}
satisfies the null-decoupling equation for the mode expansion (
5.161
). By
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance, the abelianisation (
5.231
) is equivalent to either of the degenerate vertex operators

\displaystyle V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}(z)=e^{-b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\quad\text{or}\quad V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}(z)=e^{b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}).

(5.232)

These are also sensible insertions to consider physically. For instance, in
[
1
,
80
]
it is shown that the matrix model correlator with a degenerate insertion
e^{-b\widetilde{\varphi}}
obeys a BPZ-like equation. As argued around eq. (3.8) of
[
1
]
, this corresponds to non-compact brane insertions
\psi=\exp(b\varphi)
or
\psi^{*}=\exp(-b\varphi)
on the respective sheets of the covering.

The second free-field solution to the BPZ equation can be obtained by acting on the degenerate operator
V_{1,2}(z)
with a screening charge
\mathcal{Q}_{+}

[
20
,
21
]
. From the representation theory of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
(or equivalently, the Virasoro algebra), we conclude that a degenerate operator
V_{1,2}(z)
can at most support a single screening charge
\mathcal{Q}_{+}

[
21
]
. Together,
V_{1,2}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{+}\cdot V_{1,2}
span a
2
-dimensional representation of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
, which is a
q
-deformation of the
2
-dimensional representation of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
.
67
67

67

Here,
q
-deformation means that
\mathrm{Rep}SL(2,\mathbb{C})
and
\mathrm{Rep}_{q}SL(2,\mathbb{C})
are equivalent as categories but not as braided tensor categories. See for instance
[
55
, Example 1.19]
.
Hence,
V_{1,2}(z)
and
\mathcal{Q}_{+}\cdot V_{1,2}(z)
will indeed generate all solutions of the corresponding BPZ equation.

The previous arguments suggest that the complete abelianisation of a degenerate operator in the standard orientation is given by

V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}(z)\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{+}^{\text{ab}}\cdot V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}(z)

(5.233)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{+}^{\text{ab}}\cdot V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}(z):=\int S_{+}^{\text{ab}}(y)\,dy\,V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}(z).

(5.234)

Locally, we can treat
V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{+}^{\text{ab}}\cdot V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}
as operators (
5.20
) acting on adjacent generic representations. This yields

\displaystyle V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}~:

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}^{(1)}_{\alpha}\otimes\widetilde{F}^{(2)}_{-\alpha}\longrightarrow\widetilde{F}^{(1)}_{\alpha-b/2}\otimes\widetilde{F}^{(2)}_{-\alpha+b/2},

(5.235)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{+}^{\text{ab}}\cdot V_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}~:

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}^{(1)}_{\alpha}\otimes\widetilde{F}^{(2)}_{-\alpha}\longrightarrow\widetilde{F}^{(1)}_{\alpha+b/2}\otimes\widetilde{F}^{(2)}_{-\alpha-b/2},

and thus recovers the abelianisation

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigg(\widetilde{F}_{\alpha-b/2}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{-\alpha+b/2}^{(2)}\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\widetilde{F}_{\alpha+b/2}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{-\alpha-b/2}^{(2)}\bigg).\end{aligned}

(5.236)

of the fusion product (
5.222
) in the standard orientation.

So far, we were able to consistently draw parallels between CFT-abelianisation in the standard orientation and the MO orientation. But at present, we are unable to reconcile the abelianisation maps (
5.226
) and (
5.233
) for degenerate operators in the MO and standard orientations, respectively. Furthermore, we will see in
remark

5.26
that Heisenberg-Verlinde operators in the standard orientation do not produce the expected mass eigenvalues. Whereas there are unresolved issues with the abelianisation map (
5.226
) in the MO orientation, it does recover the expected results. We hope that a better understanding of the representation theory of the R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
will shed more light on this, and bridge the two prescriptions.

Heisenberg-Verlinde operators

The next step is to define loop operators supporting the degenerate vertex operators from the previous paragraph. We start once again with the MO orientation. With
eq.

5.236
in mind, we define the Heisenberg-Verlinde (HV) operator supported on a loop
\gamma
on
\Sigma
as

X_{\gamma}=\exp\bigg(\oint_{\gamma}b(\widetilde{z})\,\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{z})\bigg),

(5.237)

where the multiplicative factor
b(\widetilde{z})
is defined as

b(\widetilde{z})=\begin{cases}-\,b&\text{ on sheet }1,\\
+\,b&\text{ on sheet }2.\end{cases}

(5.238)

That is, we smear the degenerate operator
\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{\text{ab}}
along the loop
\gamma
.
68
68

68

Compare this with the definition of a Liouville-Verlinde operator in
eq.

3.118
.
We illustrate this in an example.

Let
\gamma=(\gamma^{(1)}-\gamma^{(2)})/2
be an odd, clockwise-oriented
1
-cycle surrounding a puncture supporting a generic insertion
\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}^{\text{ab}}(z)
. Then, the integral
\oint_{\gamma}\partial\widetilde{\varphi}
can simply be calculated using the residues of the OPEs

\displaystyle\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(w^{(1)})\,e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\frac{-\alpha}{w^{(1)}-z^{(1)}}\,e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\quad\text{and}

(5.239)

\displaystyle\partial\widetilde{\varphi}(w^{(2)})\,e^{(\alpha-Q)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})

\displaystyle=\frac{-(\alpha-Q)}{w^{(2)}-z^{(2)}}\,e^{(\alpha-Q)\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)}).

We find that the HV operator
X_{\gamma}
evaluates to

\exp\left(-\frac{b}{2}\oint_{\gamma^{(1)}}\partial\widetilde{\varphi}\right)\,\exp\left(\,\frac{b}{2}\oint_{-\gamma^{(2)}}\partial\widetilde{\varphi}\right)=e^{-\pi ib(2\alpha-Q)}=e^{-i\pi m}=M,

(5.240)

after rewriting
m=b(2\alpha-Q)
. Similarly, the HV operator
X_{\sigma_{*}\gamma}
evaluates to
M^{-1}
.

Thus, we find that the HV operator evaluated on an odd
1
-cycle around a puncture reproduces the corresponding mass eigenvalue. This is in alignment with our expectation that a Heisenberg-Verlinde operator
X_{\gamma}
quantises the corresponding FN spectral coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{hol}_{\gamma}\nabla^{\text{ab}}
(see sections
3.3.2
and
4.2.3
).

Remark 5.25
.

In fact, the HV operator computes an invariant of the Fock space
\widetilde{F}_{\alpha}
. We show this for
\widetilde{F}_{\alpha}^{(1)}
on sheet
1
for exposition. Let
v\in\widetilde{F}_{\alpha}^{(1)}
be an arbitrary state in the Fock space. The most general form for the corresponding vertex operator is given by

\sum_{N}P_{N}(z^{(1)})\,\partial^{N}_{z^{(1)}}e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)}),

(5.241)

where
P_{N}(z^{(1)})
is a polynomial in
z^{(1)}
. Then, a straightforward residue computation shows that the contour integral

\oint_{\gamma^{(1)}}\partial\widetilde{\varphi}=2\pi i\alpha

(5.242)

along a contour surrounding
z^{(1)}
does not depend on the choice of
v
.

Now, suppose a pants cycle
\mathfrak{a}
on
C
is labelled by a Virasoro representation
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
. Then, its abelianisation is a pair of Fock spaces
\widetilde{F}_{\alpha}^{(1)}
and
\widetilde{F}_{\alpha-Q}^{(2)}
associated to the lifts
A^{(1)}
and
A^{(2)}
of the pants cycle
\mathfrak{a}
to sheets
1
and
2
of
\Sigma
, respectively. Combining
eq.

5.242
with
eq.

5.240
, we then find that the HV operator
X_{A_{\text{odd}}}
, for
A^{\text{odd}}=(A^{(1)}-A_{(2)})/2
, is diagonal and that it evaluates to the corresponding FN length coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{A}
.

Finally, we compute the commutation relation between two HV operators
X_{\gamma}
and
X_{\gamma^{\prime}}
. It is shown in
[
59
, Eq.
6.5
]
that
\oint\partial\widetilde{\varphi}
satisfies the log-commutation relation
69
69

69

Recall that their
U(1)
current
\widetilde{J}
is defined with an additional factor of
i
when compared with our
\partial\widetilde{\varphi}
.

\displaystyle\bigg[\oint_{\gamma}\partial\widetilde{\varphi},\oint_{\gamma^{\prime}}\partial\widetilde{\varphi}\bigg]=2\pi i\braket{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}.

(5.243)

Then, a simple application of the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula shows that the HV operators satisfy the commutation relation

X_{\gamma}X_{\gamma^{\prime}}=q^{-2\braket{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}}X_{\gamma^{\prime}}X_{\gamma}

(5.244)

with
q=e^{-i\pi b^{2}}
(also compare with
eq.

4.7
). Thus, we are able to justify our claim in §
3.3.2
that the Heisenberg-Verlinde operator
X_{\gamma}
quantises the spectral coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}
.

Remark 5.26
.

One might also attempt to compute HV operators in the standard orientation. If we keep the Heisenberg-Verlinde operator defined as in
eq.

5.237
, we find that
X_{\gamma}
evaluates to
1
for odd
1
-cycles
\gamma
. But even if we alter the definition (
5.237
) of the Heisenberg-Verlinde operator so that
b(\widetilde{z})
has the same sign on both sheets, which seems to be sensible given the definition (
5.233
), we do not reproduce the expected result (
5.240
).

Extension to
W_{K}
algebras

Here, we briefly comment on the extension of the abelianisation formalism for higher-rank Toda theories. For a free-field description of the
A_{K-1}
Toda theory, we consider
K
bosonic fields
\varphi_{i}
on
C
, and the quantum Miura relation

(Q\partial-\partial\varphi_{1})(Q\partial-\partial\varphi_{2})\ldots(Q\partial-\partial\varphi_{K})=\sum_{k=0}^{K}W_{(k)}^{e}(Q\partial)^{K-k},

(5.245)

which defines the higher-spin currents (generators)
W_{(k)}
of the
W_{K}
algebra. The spin
1
current

W_{(1)}=\sum_{i}\partial\varphi_{i}=0

(5.246)

decouples from the theory when we focus on the
SU(K)\subset U(K)
subsector. (The superscript
e
stands for the identity of the symmetry group
S_{K}
, as will become clear soon.)

Similar as for the Virasoro algebra, we can find several different, albeit isomorphic, free-field descriptions by simply exchanging the bosonic fields
\varphi_{i}\leftrightarrow\varphi_{j}
. This is implemented by the Maulik-Okounkov R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{i,j}
, which is defined through the relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{i,j}\,(Q\partial-\partial{\varphi}_{i})(Q\partial-\partial{\varphi}_{j})=(Q\partial-\partial{\varphi}_{j})(Q\partial-\partial{\varphi}_{i})\mathcal{R}_{i,j},

(5.247)

as a generalisation of
eq.

5.178
in the presence of
K
bosons. The operator
\mathcal{R}_{i,j}
induces an algebra isomorphism through the relations

\mathcal{R}_{i,j}\,W_{(k)}^{e}=W^{(ij)}_{(k)}\,\mathcal{R}_{i,j}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{R}_{i,j}^{2}=\mathrm{Id}

(5.248)

where the
(ij)
superscript denotes the permutation
(ij):\varphi_{i}\leftrightarrow\varphi_{j}
in the defining expression. Note that this does not necessarily mean that
\mathcal{R}_{i,j}
acts as the permutation operator!

When acting on a tensor product
F_{\alpha_{i}}^{(i)}\otimes F_{\alpha_{j}}^{(j)}
, where
F^{(i)}
is the Fock space of the boson
\varphi_{i}
, the R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{i,j}
only depends on the difference
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}
. It then follows that
\mathcal{R}_{i,j}
must satisfy the Yang-Baxter relation

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{R}_{i,j}(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\mathcal{R}_{i,k}(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{k})\mathcal{R}_{j,k}(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})\\
&\qquad=\mathcal{R}_{j,k}(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})\mathcal{R}_{i,k}(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{k})\mathcal{R}_{i,j}(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}).\end{aligned}

(5.249)

when acting on the triple tensor product
F_{\alpha_{i}}^{(i)}\otimes F_{\alpha_{j}}^{(j)}\otimes F_{\alpha_{k}}^{(k)}
.

Suppose we are given an arbitrary surface
C
, a branched
K
-sheeted covering
\Sigma\to C
embedded in
T^{*}C
, and a trivialisation
\tau
of the covering. Similar to the
K=2
case, we would like to define a global free-field realisation of the
W_{K}
-algebra on the cover
\Sigma
in terms of a global boson
\widetilde{\varphi}
such that
\varphi_{i}
can be interpreted as the restriction of
\widetilde{\varphi}
to sheet
i
of the covering, subject to the
SL(K,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance condition
W_{(1)}=0
. We can then lift the generators
W_{(k)}
to
\Sigma
by defining
W_{(k)}^{\text{ab}}
similar to
eq.

5.180
.

This picture is consistent in the neighbourhood of a single
(ij)
-branch-cut if we insert the R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{i,j}
along the lift of this branch-cut. Indeed, the relation (
5.247
) ensures that the abelianisation
W_{(k)}^{\text{ab}}
is well-defined upon crossing the branch-cut. The Yang–Baxter equation (
5.249
) ensures consistency globally across
\Sigma
. Geometrically, this corresponds to the braid relation

\displaystyle(ij)(ik)(jk)=(jk)(ik)(ij),

(5.250)

and encodes the equivalence of different ways of moving branch-cuts past each other.
70
70

70

Similar to
K=2
, the global free-field representation is defined with respect to an induced orientation from
C
in the neighbourhood of each lifted branch-cut on the cover. The R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{i,j}
swaps the global orientation of
\Sigma
on one side of the branch-cut to ensure it is consistent with the
\tau
-induced orientation on
\Sigma
.

Remark 5.27
.

Similar to
remark

5.20
, the above global free-field realisation can be formulated on the base
C
by dressing the branch-cuts with Weyl reflection operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\text{Weyl}}_{(ij)}=(ij)\circ\mathcal{R}_{i,j}

(5.251)

where
(ij):F_{\alpha_{i}}^{(i)}\otimes F_{\alpha_{j}}^{(j)}\to F_{\alpha_{j}}^{(j)}\otimes F_{\alpha_{i}}^{(i)}
is the permutation operator represented on vertex operators.

Below, we will make a few remarks about a very special setting in which we can actually identify a Toda boson
\varphi
on the base akin to the Liouville boson, and realise
\widetilde{\varphi}
as the abelianisation of this Toda boson. In this situation, we can apply some of the methods we have developed previously.

Consider a so-called
minimal
puncture on
C
, labelled by a
W_{K}
-representation whose free-field realisation is given by

\underbrace{F_{\alpha}^{(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes F_{\alpha}^{(K-1)}}_{K-1\text{ terms}}\otimes\,F_{\alpha_{K}}^{(K)}

(5.252)

where
\alpha_{K}=-(K-1)\,\alpha
is determined by
SL(K,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance. This puncture corresponds to a primary vertex operator insertion
V_{\vec{\alpha}}^{\text{min}}
with semi-degenerate momentum
\vec{\alpha}=\alpha\omega_{1}
, where
\omega_{1}
is the first fundamental weight of
SL(K,\mathbb{C})
. Since the R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{i,j}
only depends on the difference in momentum
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}
, it acts as the identity on
V_{\vec{\alpha}}^{\text{min}}
for all
i,j\neq K
. Furthermore, we also have that

\mathcal{R}_{K}:=\mathcal{R}_{1,K}=\ldots=\mathcal{R}_{K-1,K},

(5.253)

and the action of
\mathcal{R}_{K}
can be deduced by comparing conformal dimensions as in
eq.

5.191
.

In the neighbourhood of a minimal puncture,
eq.

5.252
tells us that the bosonic fields
\varphi_{i}
are related as

\varphi:=\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}=\ldots=-(K-1)\varphi_{K},

(5.254)

where we refer to
\varphi
as the Toda boson. The semi-degenerate primary operator insertion at the puncture can therefore be expressed as

V_{\vec{\alpha}}^{\text{min}}(z)=e^{\alpha\varphi_{1}}(z)\otimes e^{\alpha\varphi_{2}}(z)\ldots\otimes e^{-(K-1)\alpha\varphi_{K}}=:e^{K\alpha\varphi}(z),

(5.255)

in terms of the Toda boson
\varphi
. This should be compared with a similar expression (
5.163
) in the
K=2
case.

The bosonic field
\widetilde{\varphi}
on the cover is simply the abelianisation of the Toda boson
\varphi
. The abelianisation of the semi-degenerate Toda primary operator
V_{\vec{\alpha}}^{\text{min}}
is given by

V_{\vec{\alpha}}^{\textrm{min, ab}}(z)=\widetilde{V}_{\vec{\alpha}}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\otimes\ldots\otimes\widetilde{V}_{\vec{\alpha}}^{(K)}(z^{(K)}),

(5.256)

in the
\tau
-induced orientation, with

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{\vec{\alpha}}^{(i)}(z^{(i)})

\displaystyle=e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(i)})\quad\text{ (for }i\neq K),\text{ and }

(5.257)

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{\vec{\alpha}}^{(K)}(z^{(K)})

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{R}_{1,K}\,e^{\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}}\big)(z^{(K)}).

This expression should be compared with the abelianisation (
5.183
) of a Virasoro primary operator in the
K=2
case.

More importantly, expressing the generators
W_{(k)}
in
eq.

5.245
solely in terms of
\varphi
allows us to write down the abelianised generators
W_{(k)}^{\text{ab}}
on the cover in terms of
\widetilde{\varphi}
localised to a single sheet. We may then assign a single free-field representation of
W_{K}
to each sheet of
\Sigma
(similar to the discussion around
eq.

5.181
for
K=2
). Thus, a minimal puncture allows us to extract a Liouville-like subsector inside the full
A_{K-1}
Toda theory.

For non-minimal punctures, corresponding to primary vertex operators
V^{\text{Toda}}_{\vec{\alpha}}(z)
with more general momenta
\vec{\alpha}
, the analysis is more complicated. Nonetheless, we can make use of the fact that the different types of
W_{K}
-representations labelling a puncture are classified in terms of the conjugacy class of the Toda monodromy around the puncture.

For instance, we could have alternatively determined the abelianisation of
V_{\vec{\alpha}}^{\text{min}}
by noting that the Toda monodromy around a minimal puncture lies in the conjugacy class of a diagonalisable
SL(K,\mathbb{C})
-matrix with
(K-1)
equal eigenvalues and one distinct eigenvalue. This allows us to fix the evaluation of the Heisenberg-Verlinde operators
X_{\gamma^{(i)}}
around the free-field insertion
e^{\alpha_{i}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(i)})
as

\mathrm{ev}\big(X_{\gamma^{(1)}})=\mathrm{ev}\big(X_{\gamma^{(2)}})=\ldots=\mathrm{ev}\big(X_{\gamma^{(K)}})^{-(K-1)}

(5.258)

where
\gamma^{(i)}
is a small (clockwise) loop around
z^{(i)}
in
\Sigma
, and through this, the values of the momenta
\alpha_{i}
.

Similarly, fixing the abelianisation of a generic Toda primary vertex operator to be of the form

V^{\text{ab}}_{\vec{\alpha}}(z)=\widetilde{V}_{{\alpha}_{1}}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\otimes\ldots\otimes\widetilde{V}_{{\alpha}_{K}}^{(K)}(z^{(K)}),

(5.259)

we can solve for the
{\alpha}_{k}
by imposing constraints on the Heisenberg-Verlinde operators on the cover. We call the resulting tuple
(\widetilde{V}_{{\alpha}_{1}}^{(1)}(z^{(1)}),\ldots,\widetilde{V}_{\widetilde{\alpha}_{K}}^{(K)}(z^{(K)}))
of vertex operators on the cover
\Sigma
an
SL(K,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant collection of vertex operators (of the given type).

5.4.3
CFT-nonabelianisation

In §
5.4.2
we proposed a global free-field representation of the Virasoro algebra with respect to any smooth covering
\Sigma\to C
in terms of a single (non-local) boson
\widetilde{\varphi}
and branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
supporting the R-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
. The branch-cut defects intertwine between a theory with background charge
Q
and
-Q
on sheets
1
and
2
of
\Sigma
, respectively. In this section, we conjecture the existence of a
CFT-nonabelianisation map

\displaystyle\psi^{\text{CFT}}_{\mathcal{W}}:\mathrm{CB}^{\text{equiv}}_{\text{Heis}}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{CB}_{\text{Liou}}(C)

(5.260)

that allows us to build Liouville conformal blocks on the base
C
using the global free-field construction on
\Sigma
. This construction is similar to the one proposed in
[
59
]
, suitably generalised to the case
Q\neq 0
. Our expectations can then be summarised as follows:

1.

We start with a spectral network
\mathcal{W}
subordinate to a smooth covering
\Sigma\to C
. We also fix a trivialisation
\tau
of the covering, but the final result is not dependent on this.

2.

We construct a basis for the relevant space

\displaystyle\mathrm{CB}^{\text{equiv}}_{\text{Heis}}(\Sigma)

(5.261)

of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant Heisenberg blocks on
\Sigma
. This basis is essentially determined by the global free-field representation for the particular space of Liouville blocks that we want to reproduce. In particular, it includes the insertion of the Maulik-Okounkov R-matrix along lifts of the branch-cuts to the cover
\Sigma
. (More details follow in the subsection on ”Equivariant Heisenberg conformal blocks”.)

3.

We insert into the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant Heisenberg blocks the exponentiated operator

\displaystyle E[\mathcal{W}]:=\exp(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{W}})

(5.262)

of screening charges along the
\mathcal{W}
-trajectories. Similar as in
\mathcal{W}
-nonabelianisation, we claim that inserting this operator will ensure that the resulting Liouville monodromies around the branch-points on
C
are trivial. Moreover, it guarantees that the construction reduces to the matrix model description in §
5.2
in the limit where the covering
\Sigma\to C
degenerates to a singular covering. (More details follow in the subsection on ”Introducing screening charges”. )

4.

For spectral networks of FN-type, the resulting collection of Heisenberg conformal blocks on
\Sigma
can be identified with the standard basis of Liouville conformal blocks on
C
(introduced in §
3.1
).
71
71

71

Remember that we defined Liouville conformal blocks as in
eq.

3.22
.
(More details follow in the subsection on ”Liouville conformal blocks”.)

5.

For spectral networks of FG-type, the construction provides a definition for the ”Goncharov-Shen blocks” proposed in
[
51
]
. (More remarks, including a comment on compatibility with the exact WKB method, follow in the subsection on ”Exact WKB and Goncharov-Shen blocks”.)

Equivariant Heisenberg conformal blocks

Let
\Sigma\to C
be a smooth double covering with trivialisation
\tau
. We would like to compute Heisenberg conformal blocks

\biggl\langle\prod_{k=1}^{n}\widetilde{V}_{\alpha_{k}}^{\text{ab}}(z_{k})\biggr\rangle_{\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}}

(5.263)

with respect to the MO orientation on
\Sigma
in the presence of branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
. In fact, since we assume the vertex operator insertions to be generic, it is possible to trade the branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
in the MO orientation for the simpler branch-cut defects
\mathbf{B}_{\sigma}
in the standard orientation. Since the two configurations are related by the action of a topological defect
\mathbf{D}^{\text{Liou}}
(i.e. a symmetry transformation), we expect the correlation functions to produce equivalent results. In this paragraph we therefore consider the simpler correlation functions

\biggl\langle\prod_{k=1}^{n}V_{\alpha_{k}}^{\text{ab}}(z_{k})\biggr\rangle

(5.264)

in the standard orientation. (We omit the subscript for correlators in the standard orientation.).

Although free boson correlation functions on arbitrary
\Sigma
do not admit nice expressions such as the genus zero correlation functions (
5.23
), it is nonetheless possible to evaluate them exactly through the Euclidean path integral

\biggl\langle\prod_{k=1}^{n}V_{\alpha}^{\text{ab}}(z_{k})\biggr\rangle=\int[D\widetilde{\varphi}]\,e^{-S[\widetilde{\varphi}]}\prod_{k=1}^{n}V_{\alpha_{k}}^{\text{ab}}(z_{k})

(5.265)

for a non-local free boson
\widetilde{\varphi}
on
\Sigma
. The free boson action

S[\widetilde{\varphi}]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Sigma}d^{2}\widetilde{z}\,\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left(\widehat{g}^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{\varphi}\,\partial_{\nu}\widetilde{\varphi}+Q(\widetilde{z})\,\widehat{R}\,\widetilde{\varphi}\right)

(5.266)

is very similar to the action (
5.1
) on the base, except that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is non-local, i.e.

\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(1)})+\widetilde{\varphi}(z^{(2)})=0,

(5.267)

and there is a sheet-dependent background charge

Q(\widetilde{z})=\begin{cases}+\,Q&\text{ on sheet }1,\\
-\,Q&\text{ on sheet }2.\end{cases}

(5.268)

We have argued in §
5.4.2
that the resulting free boson theory and the vertex operator insertions are well-defined in the presence of the branch-cut defects.

As is typical in a quantum field theory, we separate the zero-mode
\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}
from

\widetilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}+\underline{\widetilde{\varphi}},

(5.269)

and integrate over the zero-modes
\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}
and fluctuations
\underline{\widetilde{\varphi}}
separately. Since the free boson action (
5.266
) is Gaussian, the integral over
\underline{\widetilde{\varphi}}
can, in principle, be evaluated exactly. Whereas the zero-mode integral enforces the (generalised) momentum conservation constraint (
5.34
)
[
68
, §5]
. Now, observe that the net momentum

\pm\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\alpha_{k}-Q(1-g)\right)

(5.270)

on sheets
1
and
2
, respectively, exactly neutralise each other.
72
72

72

Here, we have assumed that the
\int_{\Sigma}d^{2}\widetilde{z}\sqrt{\hat{g}}\,Q(\widetilde{z})\widehat{R}\widetilde{\varphi}
term identically vanishes after imposing
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance. This is in line with our interpretation that the net total background charge on
\Sigma
is zero.
This implies that the momentum conservation constraint (
5.34
) is trivially obeyed for any number of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant pairs of vertex operator insertions. Thus, in the global free-field formalism there is
no longer
a restriction on the momenta of the vertex operator insertions (as long as they form
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant pairs).

Remark 5.28
.

We could also consider inserting copies of the stress-energy tensor
T_{\text{Vir}}^{\text{ab}}(z_{i})
into the correlation functions (
5.263
), for instance, to determine correlation functions of descendants of the primary operator insertions
V_{\alpha_{k}}^{\text{ab}}(z_{k})
. This is computed by the path integral

\int[D\widetilde{\varphi}]\,e^{-S[\widetilde{\varphi}]}\prod_{k=1}^{n}V_{\alpha_{k}}^{\text{ab}}(z_{k})\prod_{i=1}^{m}T_{\text{Vir}}^{\text{ab}}(z_{i}).

(5.271)

Finally, let us count the free parameters of the equivariant Heisenberg block (
5.263
) on
\Sigma
. The only modulus of the free boson theory is the conformal structure (equivalently, complex structure) on
\Sigma
. In any conformal class
[\,\widehat{g}\,]
of metrics on
\Sigma
, there is a canonical metric

\widehat{g}_{\lambda}=\lambda\otimes\overline{\lambda},

(5.272)

induced by a quadratic differential
\phi_{2}
on
C
. The moduli space of conformal structures
[\,\widehat{g}\,]
on
\Sigma
, compatible with the vertex operator insertions, is therefore equivalent to the moduli space of quadratic differentials
\phi_{2}
on
C
with prescribed singularities at the punctures: the latter can be identified with the Coulomb branch of the dual 4d
\mathcal{N}=2

SU(2)
gauge theory using the class S construction. Furthermore, the dimension of the Coulomb branch is equal to the dimension of the space of Liouville internal momenta through the AGT correspondence.
73
73

73

Concretely, in the semi-classical limit, the internal momentum
\alpha
of a Liouville block through a pants cycle on
C
limits to the holonomy
\oint_{A_{c}}\lambda
around a lift
A_{c}
of the pants cycle to
\Sigma
.
Thus, we are able to match the moduli of equivariant Heisenberg blocks with the
3g-3+n
internal momenta of Liouville blocks on
C
.

Let us collectively denote all the internal momenta by
\vec{\alpha}
. Then, a basis for the equivariant Heisenberg conformal blocks from
eq.

5.263
is given by

\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}_{\vec{\alpha}}\big(~\prod_{k=1}^{n}V_{\alpha_{k}}^{\text{ab}}(z_{k})\big)=\biggl\langle\prod_{k=1}^{n}V_{\alpha_{k}}^{\text{ab}}(z_{k})\biggr\rangle_{\vec{\alpha}}

(5.273)

where the correlation function on the right is evaluated with respect to the metric
\widehat{g}_{\lambda}
on
\Sigma
corresponding to
\vec{\alpha}
.

Introducing screening charges

Here we go back to the MO orientation. Since we would like to interpret the equivariant Heisenberg blocks on the cover
\Sigma
as Virasoro blocks on the base
C
, we will need to check that they do not detect branch-points on
C
. That is, we need to make sure that the Liouville-Verlinde operator around a branch-point is trivial.

Recall that a Liouville-Verlinde operator is described by moving a degenerate insertion
V_{1,2}(z)
across
C
inside the Liouville conformal block

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\text{Liou}}\big(V_{1,2}(z)\ldots\big).

(5.274)

On the cover
\Sigma
, the Liouville block is abelianised into the basis

B(z)=\left(\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}\big(\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\ldots\big),\,\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}\big(\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})\ldots\big)\right)

(5.275)

of Heisenberg correlators. We thus need to ensure that the basis
B(z)
remains invariant after traversing a small loop around any branch-point on
C
.

Similar to
\mathcal{W}
-abelianisation, it turns out that this requires a choice of a spectral network
\mathcal{W}
on
C
. The natural quantum extension is to insert screening charges
\mathcal{Q}_{ij}
along the lifts of the critical trajectories of
\mathcal{W}
using the operator

E[\mathcal{W}]:=\exp\big(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{W}}\big).

(5.276)

This is the
Q\neq 0
generalisation of the operator with the same name in
[
59
]
. Note that there is no restriction on the number of screening contours that can be inserted as the screening operators also form
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariant pairs. We denote equivariant Heisenberg blocks supporting the operator
E[\mathcal{W}]
by

\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}_{\vec{\alpha},E[\mathcal{W}]}=\biggl\langle\prod_{k=1}^{n}V_{\alpha_{k}}^{\text{ab}}(z_{k})\,E[\mathcal{W}]\biggr\rangle_{\vec{\alpha}}.

(5.277)

To argue that the insertion of the operator
E[\mathcal{W}]
is indeed necessary, recall that it should be possible to interpret the Heisenberg blocks in the basis
B(z)
as independent local solutions to the BPZ equation on
C
defined by
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Vir}}\big(V_{1,2}(z)\ldots\big)
– see for instance the discussion around
eq.

4.80
.
74
74

74

Only if
C
is a 3-punctured sphere and the network
\mathcal{W}
is of Fenchel-Nielsen type, we would expect to recover the standard hypergeometric solutions.
Since the BPZ equation can be described by an ordinary ODE in any open neighbourhood on
C
(see §
3.2.2
), the Heisenberg blocks making up the basis
B(z)
should generate the local sections in the exact WKB analysis of the ODE in such an open neighbourhood. In particular, in the vicinity of a branch-point, we may infer the behaviour of the degenerate operators
\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(k)}
on sheet
k
using the WKB analysis.

For instance, the exact WKB analysis implies that for any two points
z
and
z^{\prime}
in the same connected component of
C\backslash\mathcal{W}-\{\text{branch-cuts}\}
the bases
B(z)
and
B(z^{\prime})
are related by a diagonal
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-transformation, whereas across a branch-cut on
C
the two basis elements in
B(z)
are simply exchanged. Both of these statements naturally follow from the global free-field construction. Across a critical trajectory of
\mathcal{W}
, however, the bases
B(z)
and
B(z^{\prime})
are supposed to be related by a non-trivial triangular
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-transformation. This is not possible without an additional operator insertion, but is true when we insert the operator
E[\mathcal{W}]
into the Heisenberg blocks.

Figure 65
:
Through CFT abelianisation the nonabelian parallel transport associated with the degenerate vertex operator
V_{1,2}(z)
crossing a critical
ij
-trajectory on
C
can be described on sheet
i
of the covering in terms of the OPE of the lift
\widetilde{V}^{(i)}_{1,2}(z)
with the relevant screening charge
\widetilde{S}^{(i)}_{\pm}
. If the OPE is regular, the operator
\widetilde{V}^{(i)}_{1,2}(z)
can simply move through the lift of the trajectory. But when the OPE is singular, the parallel transport receives an additional contribution associated with the relevant detour path.

Indeed, suppose that
z
and
z^{\prime}
lie very close to each other on either side of a critical
ij
-trajectory. On the cover
\Sigma
, the degenerate insertions

\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(k)}(z^{(k)})\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(k)}(z^{\prime(k)})

approach a screening contour, that supports the operators
\widetilde{S}_{\pm}^{(k)}(w)
, from either side. Since the OPE between
\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(j)}
and
\widetilde{S}_{\pm}^{(j)}
on sheet
j
is regular, we can identify the corresponding Heisenberg blocks on either side of the
ij
-trajectory. For instance, for a
12
-trajectory, we find

\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}_{E[\mathcal{W}]}\big(\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(2)}(z^{(2)})\ldots\big)=\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}_{E[\mathcal{W}]}\big(\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(2)}(z^{\prime(2)})\ldots\big).

(5.278)

The
12
-trajectory is thus transparent to the degenerate operator
\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(2)}
on sheet
2
. This is indeed consistent with the exact WKB analysis. See
fig.

65
.

On the other hand, since the OPE between the operators
\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(i)}
and
\widetilde{S}_{\pm}^{(i)}
is singular, this leads to a discontinuity in the abelianisation map across the lift of the critical trajectory to sheet
1
. As an example, the OPE between
\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(1)}
and
\widetilde{S}_{+}^{(1)}
on sheet
1
has a singularity of order
b^{2}
.
75
75

75

More precisely, this is a singularity when
b\in i\mathbb{R}
. However, as per our discussion in §
5.4.2
, this is the only interval that allows for screening contours supported on generic spectral networks.
This implies that the Heisenberg blocks

\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}_{E[\mathcal{W}]}\big(\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\ldots\big)\neq\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}_{E[\mathcal{W}]}\big(\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(1)}(z^{\prime(1)})\ldots\big)

(5.279)

cannot be identified in a straight-forward manner. Yet, from
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-equivariance it follows that the two bases must be related as

\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}_{E[\mathcal{W}]}\big(\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})\ldots\big)=\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}_{E[\mathcal{W}]}\big(\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(1)}(z^{\prime(1)})\ldots\big)+\widetilde{\alpha}\,\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}_{E[\mathcal{W}]}\big(\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(2)}(z^{\prime(2)})\ldots\big).

(5.280)

for some
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}
. This gives us the triangular form of the quantum parallel transport that we are looking for.
76
76

76

A similar analysis holds true in the standard orientation as well. Especially, the divergences in the MO orientation persist also in the standard orientation.

More can be said if the Heisenberg blocks indeed generate the local sections in the exact WKB analysis of the BPZ equation. Suppose that
z
and
z^{\prime}
lie in the same open neighbourhood as the branch-point which emits the
12
-trajectory. Then the above quantum parallel transports are only consistent with the exact WKB analysis in this neighbourhood if the prefactor
\widetilde{\alpha}
is actually equal to the abelian parallel transport of the degenerate operator
\widetilde{V}_{1,2}^{(1)}(z^{(1)})
along a detour that starts at the lift
z^{(1)}
, loops around the lift of the branch-point to sheet 2 of the covering, and ends at the lift
z^{(2)}
. This is illustrated in
fig.

65
. We leave a verification of this claim for future work.
77
77

77

Note that it is essential that we insert the exponential
E[\mathcal{W}]
into the Heisenberg correlator and not just a single screening charge: in the latter case, the quantum parallel transport is determined by the OPE with this screening charge, and does not give the expected result (see also
[
89
]
).

Remark 5.29
.

In the singular limit
\Sigma\to\Sigma^{\text{sing}}=\Sigma^{(1)}\sqcup\Sigma^{(2)}
in which sheets
1
and
2
become disconnected from each other, the equivariant Heisenberg block supporting the exponential operator

E[\mathcal{W}]
degenerates as (also see
eq.

5.172
)

\displaystyle\biggl\langle\prod_{k=1}^{n}V_{\alpha_{k}}^{(1)}(z_{k}^{(1)})\,V_{\alpha_{k}}^{(2)}(z_{k}^{(2)})\,E[\mathcal{W}]\biggr\rangle_{\tau}\rightarrow

(5.281)

\displaystyle\sum_{M,M^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\biggl\langle\bigg(\int dz^{(1)}\,e^{b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\bigg)^{M}\bigg(\int dz^{\prime(1)}\,e^{\frac{1}{b}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{\prime(1)})\bigg)^{M^{\prime}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}e^{\alpha_{k}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z_{k}^{(1)})\biggr\rangle_{\Sigma^{(1)}}\times

\displaystyle\sum_{M,M^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\biggl\langle\bigg(\int dz^{(2)}\,e^{-b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})\bigg)^{M}\bigg(\int dz^{\prime(2)}\,e^{-\frac{1}{b}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{\prime(2)})\bigg)^{M^{\prime}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}e^{-\alpha_{k}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z_{k}^{(2)})\biggr\rangle_{\Sigma^{(2)}}.

In this case, each sheet is now individually required to satisfy the momentum conservation constraint for the correlation function to be non-vanishing. This introduces a restriction on the values of the internal as well as external momenta, as in the traditional free-field formalism.

Suppose that each sheet
\Sigma^{(i)}
is a genus zero surface, and the set of external momenta
\alpha_{k}
satisfies the constraint

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}+Nb+\frac{N^{\prime}}{b}=Q

(5.282)

as in the traditional free-field formalism (
5.28
). Then, the only non-vanishing contribution to the correlation function

\displaystyle\biggl\langle\bigg(\int dz^{(1)}\,e^{b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(1)})\bigg)^{N}\bigg(\int dz^{\prime(1)}\,e^{\frac{1}{b}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{\prime(1)})\bigg)^{N^{\prime}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}e^{\alpha_{k}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z_{k}^{(1)})\biggr\rangle_{\Sigma^{(1)}}\times

(5.283)

\displaystyle\biggl\langle\bigg(\int dz^{(2)}\,e^{-b\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{(2)})\bigg)^{N}\bigg(\int dz^{\prime(2)}\,e^{-\frac{1}{b}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z^{\prime(2)})\bigg)^{N^{\prime}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}e^{-\alpha_{k}\widetilde{\varphi}}(z_{k}^{(2)})\biggr\rangle_{\Sigma^{(2)}}

can be identified with the pull-back of the corresponding (double) matrix model partition function

Z_{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{mat}}[\hbar,N,N^{\prime}].

(5.284)

We can thus think of the singular limit of the Heisenberg block (
5.277
) as a formal generating function for (double) matrix model partition functions.

Liouville conformal blocks

If we apply the CFT-nonabelianisation map
\psi^{\text{CFT}}_{\mathcal{W}_{\text{FN}}}
to the basis of equivariant Heisenberg blocks

\mathcal{F}^{\text{Heis}}_{\Sigma,\,E[\mathcal{W}],\,\vec{\alpha}}\,,

(5.285)

we expect to recover the basis of
Liouville conformal blocks
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Liou}}_{C,\vec{\alpha}}
introduced in §
3.1
. This is consistent with
remark

5.7
, where we showed that free-field correlators with additional screening charges along FN-type contours obey the same Ward identities as Liouville conformal blocks. Relatedly, Liouville-Verlinde operators around punctures and pants cycles have the correct diagonal monodromies.

Suppose that we want to reproduce a Liouville block
\mathcal{F}^{\text{Liou}}_{C,\vec{\alpha}}
, defined as in
eq.

3.22
, with respect to a given pants decomposition of
C
. Then we start by fixing an FN-type spectral network
\mathcal{W}_{\text{FN}}
relative to the pants decomposition. This network is defined with respect to a double covering
\Sigma\to C
. We choose a trivialisation of this double covering, and dress the branch-cuts of the covering with line defects
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{R}}
.
Similarly as in the context of
\mathcal{W}
-abelianisation, CFT-nonabelianisation with respect to FN-type spectral networks commutes with the operation of gluing gauged 3-spheres. This implies that to verify the above proposal, we only need to check it on the level of the gauged 3-sphere. We leave an extensive analysis for future work and only make a few remarks here about our expectations.

The simplest example to consider is the 3-punctured sphere. Since its spectral cover
\Sigma
is a genus zero surface, and the expression (
5.23
) for a genus zero Heisenberg block does not explicitly depend on the background charge
Q(\widetilde{z})
(as long as the momentum conservation constraint is satisfied), it is possible to write down an expression similar to
eq.

5.49
. We expect that the resulting free-field integral reproduces the Liouville correlator

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{F}^{\text{Liou}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{\infty})=\\
&\sqrt{\frac{\left(-\gamma(b^{2})\,b^{2-2b^{2}}\right)^{(Q-\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{\infty})/b}\Upsilon_{b}\left(2\alpha_{0}\right)\Upsilon_{b}\left(2\alpha_{1}\right)\Upsilon_{b}\left(2\alpha_{\infty}\right)\Upsilon_{b}\left(0\right)}{\Upsilon_{b}\left(-\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{\infty}\right)\Upsilon_{b}\left(\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{\infty}\right)\Upsilon_{b}\left(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{\infty}\right)\Upsilon_{b}\left(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{\infty}-Q\right)}}.\end{aligned}

(5.286)

Recall that we obtained this result in
eq.

5.114
through matrix model techniques, but using the somewhat ad-hoc argument of flipping the gamma-functions associated with the degenerate double trajectory in the singular FN-network. Our claim is that CFT-nonabelianisation will reproduce this block on the nose.

Indeed, one would not expect the singular matrix-model integral, where we do not flip the gamma-functions associated with the degenerate double trajectory by hand, to be symmetric under permuting the parameters
\alpha_{k}
, since this integral is computed with respect to a degenerate FN spectral network that is not symmetric under all permutations of the three punctures. Instead, the CFT-nonabelianisation map
is
defined with respect to a spectral network that is symmetric under all permutations of the three punctures. The above result
Z^{\text{Liou}}_{3-\text{pt}}
is the simplest extension of the (honest) singular matrix-model result that is symmetric under the permutation of the parameters
\alpha_{k}
.

Exact WKB and Goncharov-Shen blocks

If we apply the CFT-nonabelianisation map
\psi^{\text{CFT}}_{\mathcal{W}}
to a spectral network of FG-type, the resulting conformal block is defined with respect to an ideal triangulation on
C
